Average-Case Complexity of Quantum Stabilizer Decoding
Andrey Boris Khesin, Jonathan Z. Lu, Alexander Poremba, Akshar Ramkumar, Vinod Vaikuntanathan

Abstract
Random classical linear codes are widely believed to be hard to decode. While slightly sub-exponential time algorithms exist when the coding rate vanishes sufficiently rapidly, all known algorithms at constant rate require exponential time. By contrast, the complexity of decoding a random quantum stabilizer code has remained an open question for quite some time. This work closes the gap in our understanding of the algorithmic hardness of decoding random quantum versus random classical codes. We prove that decoding a random stabilizer code with even a single logical qubit is at least as hard as decoding a random classical code at constant rate—the maximally hard regime. This result suggests that the easiest random quantum decoding problem is at least as hard as the hardest random classical decoding problem, and shows that any sub-exponential algorithm decoding a typical stabilizer code, at any rate, would immediately imply a breakthrough in cryptography. More generally, we also characterize many other complexity-theoretic properties of stabilizer codes. While classical decoding admits a random self-reduction, we prove significant barriers for the existence of random self-reductions in the quantum case. This result follows from new bounds on Clifford entropies and Pauli mixing times, which may be of independent interest. As a complementary result, we demonstrate various other self-reductions which are in fact achievable, such as between search and decision. We also demonstrate several ways in which quantum phenomena, such as quantum degeneracy, force several reasonable definitions of stabilizer decoding—all of which are classically identical—to have distinct or non-trivially equivalent complexity.



1
Introduction

In the classical realm, coding theory has made a remarkable impact across many different areas of mathematics and computer science, far beyond its original use in telecommunications and storage.
Presently, the span of coding-theoretic applications range across computational complexity theory (e.g. the PCP theorem and the hardness of approximation
[
ALM+98
,
FGL+96
,
Din07
]
), cryptography (e.g. as a basis of hardness for cryptographic protocols
[
Sha79
,
Ste93
,
FS96
,
McE78
,
Ale03
]
), combinatorics and extremal set theory (e.g. packing/covering problems
[
Ham50
,
Plo60
]
and list-decodable codes
[
GR06
]
), algorithms (e.g. sketching and streaming algorithms
[
Ind06
,
IR08
]
), and more.

One of the key motivations for the development of coding theory which has remained relevant due to its presence in most of the above applications is the search for
asymptotically good
codes—families of error correcting codes which have both constant rate and linear distance asymptotically.
This search naturally led to the rigorous study of
random codes
; in particular, random
linear
codes.
A classic result of coding theory known as the Gilbert-Varshamov bound is that a random linear code is asymptotically good with high probability
[
Gil52
,
Var64
]
.
Yet, despite their extraordinary error correcting properties, random linear codes are notoriously hard to decode in practice, and the fastest known algorithms still run in exponential or nearly exponential time
[
BKW03
,
Lyu05
]
.
This longstanding barrier against efficient algorithms has led to the formulation of the
Learning Parity with Noise
(
\mathsf{LPN}
)
[
BFKL94
]
problem—the task of decoding random linear codes in the presence of noise—which has since become a popular hardness assumption upon which much cryptography
[
HB01
,
Ale03
,
JW05
,
ACPS09
,
JKPT12
,
DDN14
,
AHI+17
,
BLVW18
]
and learning theory
[
BFKL94
,
FGKP06
]
has been built.
Formally, given

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k},\;\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2))

(1.1)

for a Bernoulli error
\mathbf{e}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)^{\otimes n}
of noise rate
p\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, the
\mathsf{LPN}(k,n,p)
assumption
1
1

1

This is the so-called
search
variant of
\mathsf{LPN}
problem; the
decision
variant of
\mathsf{LPN}(k,n,p)
states that it is computationally difficult to distinguish a sample as in (
1.1
) from an unstructured random sample
(\mathbf{A}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k},\mathbf{u}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n})
.
states that it is hard to recover the randomly chosen message
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
; here,
\mathbf{A}
serves as a generator matrix for a linear code. In terms of parameters, a typical choice is one in which
n=\poly(k)
and
p
is constant.

The hardness of the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem is well-supported and enjoys a firm theoretical foundation.
For example,
[
BLVW18
]
gave a random self-reduction into
\mathsf{LPN}
by proving that
\mathsf{LPN}
with a very high noise rate
p
is at least as hard as a its worst case variant—known as the
nearest codeword problem
(
\mathsf{NCP}
)—under some structural assumptions about the worst-case instance.
In addition,
[
KSS10
]
gave a search-to-decision reduction which renders both the search and the decision variants of the problem equivalent.
Despite several decades of study, the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem has seen virtually no asymptotic algorithmic improvements since the nearly-exponential time approaches of
[
BKW03
,
Lyu05
]
.
It is widely conjectured that
\mathsf{LPN}
is computationally intractable even for quantum computers
[
GJL+25
]
.

In the quantum realm, the development of quantum coding theory has focused heavily on the goal of constructing asymptotically good and practical
quantum error-correcting codes
[
Sho95
,
Got09
]
, primarily as a means of achieving fault-tolerance in a race to achieve practical logical quantum computation
[
AB08
,
KLZ98
]
.
Just as classical coding theory focuses primarily on linear codes, so too does quantum coding theory focus primarily on
stabilizer codes
, the quantum analog of linear codes
[
Got97
]
.
As in the classical case, random stabilizer codes are also
asymptotically good
with high probability
[
Smi06
,
Got24
]
and in general offer valuable insights into the fundamental limitations of quantum codes, such as achievable rates, code distances, or thresholds.
A stabilizer code can be specified by a Clifford operator
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
the same way a classical linear is specified by a generator matrix.
A
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer code specified by
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
has code states of the form
\mathbf{C}(\ket{0^{n-k}}\otimes\ket{\psi})
, where
\ket{\psi}
is a
k
-qubit state.
To generate a random
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer code, we simply choose a uniformly random Clifford
\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}

[
Smi06
,
PQS25
]
; this is always possible to do efficiently, since
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is a finite group of order
2^{O(n^{2})}
.

In sharp contrast with classical coding theory, little is known about the hardness of decoding random quantum stabilizer codes.
This is somewhat surprising because random stabilizer codes are ubiquitous in quantum information science: they appear
in the theory of quantum communication
[
Smi06
]
, quantum authentication and the verification of quantum computations
[
ABEM17
]
, quantum cryptography
[
DS18
]
and quantum gravity
[
HP07
,
HH13
]
.
Given the historical impact of
\mathsf{LPN}
across numerous areas of computer science, one may reasonably expect that the hardness of random quantum stabilizer decoding—if properly understood—could occupy a similar foundational role in quantum information science, more broadly.
We are therefore motivated to study the fundamental question:

Question 1.1
.

How hard is it to decode a typical quantum stabilizer code?

This question has remained open for quite some time
[
HL11
,
IP15
,
KL20
]
, in part because characterizing the complexity of certain quantum coding tasks has proven non-trivial even in the worst case.
In fact, all prior techniques to determine the hardness of stabilizer decoding have been limited to
worst-case
syndrome decoding problems
[
HL11
,
IP15
]
.
An answer to the above question would not only sharpen our understanding of quantum stabilizer decoding in
typical
instances, but may also provide new insights into the complexity of quantum information processing tasks
[
BEM+23
]
, or whether it is possible to base cryptography on hard problems in
quantum
, rather than classical, error-correction
[
Pie12
]
.

Learning Stabilizers with Noise

Learning Parity with Noise

Input:

\big(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}\,\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\big)

\big(\mathbf{A}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k},\;\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2)\big)

Noise:

Depolarizing noise
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}

Bernoulli noise
\mathbf{e}\sim\text{Ber}_{p}^{\otimes n}

Task:

Find
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}

Find
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}

Figure 1:
Comparison between
\mathsf{LSN}

[
PQS25
]
and
\mathsf{LPN}

[
BFKL94
]
. In both cases, the input features a classical description of a
random
code; in the case of
\mathsf{LSN}
, it is specified by a random
Clifford
encoding of a quantum stabilizer code, whereas in the case of
\mathsf{LPN}
it consists of a random
generator matrix
of a classical linear code. The problem parameters are characterized by the logical (qu)bits
k
, physical (qu)bits
n
, and the error probability per physical (qu)bit
p
.

In a first attempt to formally explore Question
1.1
,
[
PQS25
]
recently defined the
Learning Stabilizers with Noise
(
\mathsf{LSN}
) problem as a natural quantum analog of
\mathsf{LPN}
; this is the task of decoding a random quantum stabilizer code in the presence of noise.
More precisely, the task is to find
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
given

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\,\mathbf{E}\,\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right)

(1.2)

where
{\mathbf{C}}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
is a random
n
-qubit Clifford (say, described by a circuit) and
\mathbf{E}\,\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
is a noisy quantum
stabilizer state
, for some Pauli error
{\mathbf{E}}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
from a local depolarizing channel.

[
PQS25
]
showed that
\mathsf{LSN}
can be solved in exponential time using only a single sample by way of the
pretty good measurement

[
BK00
,
Mon19
]
.
In addition, they showed that
\mathsf{LSN}
is contained in the unitary complexity class
\mathsf{unitaryBQP}^{\mathsf{NP}}

[
BEM+23
]
.
While these results provide an initial assessment of the hardness of the
\mathsf{LSN}
problem through the lens of both algorithms and complexity, the central issue of how to characterize (or lower bound) the average-case hardness itself, as raised in Question
1.1
, remains unresolved.

More formally, we may break down Question
1.1
into more fine-grained questions about the hardness of random stabilizer decoding.
The first is a question of lower-bounding quantum decoding by more familiar problems.

Question 1.2
.

Is random stabilizer decoding at least as hard as other (e.g. classical) average-case tasks which are widely believed to be hard?

A positive answer would provide rigorous theoretical foundations towards the complexity of decoding stabilizer codes in
typical
instances.
Closely related to the question of hardness lower bounds is the existence of non-trivial decoding algorithms; in particular:

Question 1.3
.

Do random quantum stabilizer codes admit any sub-exponential time decoders, similar to random linear codes?

While sub-exponential algorithms for certain parameter regimes of
\mathsf{LPN}
are known
[
BKW03
,
Lyu05
]
, no such algorithm has ever been discovered for decoding a random quantum stabilizer code in
any
(non-trivial) parameter regime.
This is particularly surprising, given that stabilizer decoding in quantum error correction has been studied intensely from many angles for the past three decades.

Another important structural component in the characterization of the average-case complexity of a problem is the existence of
self reductions
: either between decision and search variants, or between worst-case and average-case variants.
Such reductions certify the robustness of the problem to certain useful deformations (e.g. distinguish two cases vs. find a solution) in how the problem is defined.
Both of these, for example, have been studied extensively in the case of
\mathsf{LPN}

[
BLVW19
,
KSS10
]
. This naturally raises the question:

Question 1.4
.

Is stabilizer decoding self-reducible? That is, does stabilizer decoding admit search-to-decision, decision-to-search, and random self-reductions?

1.1
Contributions and Scope

Our limited understanding of random stabilizer codes reflects broader gaps in the foundations of quantum error correction.
These gaps have important implications for many different areas of research, including coding theory, algorithms, complexity theory, and cryptography.
The overarching goal of this work is to expose these limitations and to develop the necessary mathematical techniques to overcome them.
Concretely, we seek to

•

bring the study of random quantum stabilizer codes to the same theoretical footing as those of random classical linear codes, and

•

to lay the theoretical foundations for how to view problems in quantum error correction, more generally, through the lens of computational complexity and cryptography.

To that end, we show the following results about decoding random quantum stabilizer codes, which collectively answer Question
1.1
to roughly the same degree as our understanding of random classical linear codes.
First, to Question
1.2
, we prove that decoding a random stabilizer code with a
single
logical qubit is at least as hard as decoding a random classical linear code with constant rate
R=\frac{k}{n}=\Theta(1)
.
More precisely, we reduce
\mathsf{LPN}
with
n=ck
, where
c>1
is a chosen constant to
\mathsf{LSN}
with
any
rate, e.g. with a single logical qubit
k=1
, such that the probability parameters
p
of each differ only by a constant.
This result lower bounds the hardness of
\mathsf{LSN}
by
\mathsf{LPN}
itself, which is widely conjectured (with significant evidence) to be computationally intractable.
In fact,
\mathsf{LPN}
with constant rate is the hardest regime of classical random decoding, in the sense that no sub-exponential algorithms for
\mathsf{LPN}
have been devised in this regime (for non-trivial, e.g. constant, error probability
p
) have been found after decades of effort.
Therefore, our reduction also provides evidence that the answer to Question
1.3
is negative.
Specifically, since
\mathsf{LPN}
at constant rate reduces to any instance of
\mathsf{LSN}
, even for one logical qubit,
any
sub-exponential algorithm constructed for
\mathsf{LSN}
would also serve as a sub-exponential algorithm for
\mathsf{LPN}
at constant rate.
Such an algorithm would imply significant breakthrough in classical complexity theory and cryptography, which assumes and relies on the strong hardness of
\mathsf{LPN}
.

Next, we explore Question
1.4
across several directions. We prove that, both in the worst and average case, stabilizer decoding satisfies a search-to-decision reduction.
The reverse notion, a decision-to-search reduction, is seldom considered in classical literature because it is usually trivial.
We observe, however, that for
\mathsf{LSN}
this reduction is not trivial because a proposed solution is not clearly verifiable.
Nonetheless, we are able to prove a decision-to-search reduction, showing that the decision and search versions of stabilizer decoding are equivalent in a certain sense.
On the other hand, we prove significant barriers against the existence of a random self-reduction for stabilizer decoding.
More precisely, an operator implementing a reasonable reduction should have bounded sparsity, because a completely dense operator will scramble the noise to an undecodable level.
Yet, we prove that no sparse operators can implement a reduction.
Similarly, natural reduction operators scramble the stabilizer tableau itself, in addition to the underlying code space.
However, we prove also that under reasonable assumptions about the structure of the reduction itself, no such reduction exists either.
These results together imply that random self-reductions for
\mathsf{LSN}
must either have an exotic structure so as to overcome these barriers, or not exist at all.

One consequence of these results is that they highlight the many
qualitative
differences between quantum and classical decoding, which pushes back against folklore wisdom within quantum error correction that decoding quantum codes is merely a slight generalization of decoding classical codes.
Furthermore, a useful facet of the proof techniques we use to answer the above questions is that they give explicit interpretations as to the specific quantum phenomena which cause
\mathsf{LSN}
to differ so strongly from
\mathsf{LPN}
.
These effects include quantum degeneracy, high uniformity in the mixing capabilities of entangling operators, and the non-commutative nature of unitary operators.
We highlight the particular quantum effects at play in our proofs.

Finally, we also discuss in detail the many natural ways to formulate the problem of decoding a stabilizer code.
For example, these include outputting the logical state, finding a valid recovery operator, or retrieving the exact Pauli error applied.
All such definitions are equivalent on a classical code, and yet quantumly some definitions appear to be strictly harder problems than others, via the implicit and unnecessary embedding of short vector problems.
This subtlety suggests that care is required in defining quantum decoding in the first place, as the task does not enjoy a strong amount of definition robustness as compared classical decoding.

1.2
Technical Overview

Before we introduce the full definitions and proofs, we give an overview of the techniques and key insights in this paper.

1.2.1
Random Classical versus Quantum Stabilizer Decoding

The precise definition of decoding a stabilizer code varies across quantum coding literature, and includes notions such as finding the maximum-likelihood error or maximum-likelihood error equivalence class.
However, the most natural definition of decoding is simply a procedure that on input the code and noisy code state outputs the underlying logical state.
In the case of classical linear codes, for example, a code is a
n\times k
binary generator matrix, and the task is to output the logical bitstring under noise.
Quantumly, the code is a description of a Clifford operator (e.g. as a circuit), but the task is also to output the logical state.
We first briefly outline the distinctions in noise model, as well as two distinct notions we will consider of “outputting the logical state”.

Classically, every bit of the codeword has some probability
p
independently of being flipped.
Quantumly, every qubit of the code state has a probability
p
of having a random Pauli in
\{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\}
being applied to it.
If a qubit does get corrupted by a Pauli, the Pauli is chosen uniformly at random.
Such an error model is known as depolarizing noise and is the natural generalization of the classical noise model.
Following
[
PQS25
]
, we define Search
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
to be the distributional task of recovering a bitstring
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, which is chosen uniformly at random, given
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, where
\mathbf{E}
is the error and
\mathbf{C}
is a Clifford operator chosen uniformly at random.
Such a formulation allows the problem to have classical output without severely affecting the inherent quantumness of the problem.
To fully capture the quantumness of the logical state, we also define Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k,n,p)
where the logical state is instead a Haar-random
k
-qubit state.
In average-case analysis, which is intimately connected to cryptography, it is of interest to determine whether any efficient algorithm can solve a problem with probability non-negligibly higher than random guessing.
For this reason, we say that a solver succeeds if it outputs
\mathbf{x}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
If
k=\omega(\log n)
, then the addition of
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
is irrelevant.
However, since we will be interested in the regime even when
k=1
, this extra term makes a significant difference.
The decision version is concerned with distinguishing two possibilities: given a random Clifford
\mathbf{C}
and a state
\rho\in(\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})^{\otimes n}
, either
\rho
is the maximally mixed state or
\rho
is
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
with a random logical string
\mathbf{x}
and error
\mathbf{E}
drawn from a depolarizing distribution.
There are two variants of the above.
In the first, we define
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
by providing
m
noisy codewords.
Each “sample” has a fresh error
\mathbf{E}
and fresh code
\mathbf{C}
, but has the
same
secret logical state
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
Such a problem is not obviously natural for the purposes of practical quantum error correction, but is nevertheless reasonable as a theoretical problem in the context of complexity and cryptography.
More importantly, the multi-sample version turns out to be a crucial intermediate problem in the reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
We also consider a multi-sample variant with random states, known as
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}
.

Figure 2:
Web of reductions between random classical (blue) and quantum (purple) decoding problems. The parameters (logical (qu)bits, physical (qu)bits, and error probability) shown are exact.
Arrows imply a reduction in time
\poly(n)
. The reductions to
\mathsf{LSN}
hold for any choice of
k\geq 1
, including a single logical qubit
k=1
. Here
\mathsf{LPN}
is the classical decoding problem,
\mathsf{sympLPN}
is a symplectically structured version of
\mathsf{LPN}
,
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
is quantum decoding with
m
samples of noisy codewords with the same fixed random logical computational basis state,
\mathsf{LSN}=\mathsf{LSN}^{1}
, and
\mathsf{stateLSN}
uses Haar-random logical states instead of a random logical computational basis state.

We prove that some of these variants are equivalent by giving efficient reductions from one to the other.
For example, Decision
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
and Decision
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k,n,p)
are exactly equivalent because the underlying distributions of the logical state are both maximally mixed.
By exploiting the fact that in certain cases the Haar-random state can be replaced by a
t
-design, we can also show that Search
\mathsf{LSN}
reduces to Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
.
The converse holds if the success probability of the
\mathsf{LSN}
solver is non-negligibly higher than
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}
(rather than
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
), or when
k=O(\log n)
.
The former is proven by using syndrome decoding as an intermediate problem and a technique that is a form of randomized syndrome matching, while the latter is proven by performing a measurement via randomized syndrome matching and analytical techniques with the measurement distribution of Haar-random states.
We remark that many of these equivalences in the classical analog are immediate, while quantumly our proofs are much more non-trivial.

Theorem 1.5

(informal)
.

\mathsf{LSN}
and
\mathsf{stateLSN}
are equivalent decision problems. Search
\mathsf{LSN}
reduces to Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, and Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
reduces to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
if the
\mathsf{LSN}
solver succeeds with probability substantially larger than
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}
(as opposed to
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
), or if
k=O(\log n)
.

The complete diagram of average-case reductions which we give in this paper is shown in Fig.
2
.
So far, we have filled in the equivalences between
\mathsf{stateLSN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}
.
The reduction from
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
to
\mathsf{LSN}^{m^{\prime}}
with
m>m^{\prime}
is trivial by way of ignoring
m-m^{\prime}
samples.
The remaining reductions are between decision and search, which we discuss later, and path of reductions from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.

Starting from
\mathsf{LPN}(k=\lfloor np/6\rfloor,2n,p/6)
, we here briefly sketch the steps to reduce to
\mathsf{LSN}(1,n,p)
which we discuss in greater detail in the remainder of the overview.
We first reduce
\mathsf{LPN}
to an intermediate problem—a version of
\mathsf{LPN}
we define whose generator matrix columns satisfy a certain symplectic orthogonality condition and whose error model is a symplectic version of depolarizing noise—which we call
\mathsf{sympLPN}
.
This reduction relies on two steps.
First, we extend a smaller
\mathsf{LPN}
instance to a slightly larger
\mathsf{LPN}
instance, carefully using the extra degrees of freedom to enforce the symplectic orthogonality condition and maintain independent noise.
We then show how to transform the independent bit flip noise model to depolarizing noise.
In the process, the error parameter
p
increases by a small constant factor.
Despite this reduction, the symplectic-type
\mathsf{LPN}
instance still looks qualitatively different from a
\mathsf{LSN}
instance, where a Clifford circuit and a quantum state are the inputs rather than a matrix and a noisy matrix-vector product.
To bridge this gap, we develop a new representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
which is entirely classical.
This representation (a) is exactly equivalent to the standard definition of
\mathsf{LSN}
discussed above and (b) looks manifestly like a modified version of a
\mathsf{LPN}
-type instance of the form
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e})
.
This representation renders a reduction from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
much more plausible.
In fact, our reduction from the symplectic-type
\mathsf{LPN}
to the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
has no dependence on
k
in
\mathsf{LSN}
, whereas in the
\mathsf{LPN}
instance from which we reduce the value of
k
can be linear in
n
.
We show that this independence from
k
is a direct consequence of quantum degeneracy, which manifests in the classical representation as an extra “junk” register of
n
bits which obfuscates the real value of the logical bitstring even if there is only only one logical qubit.
More precisely, the existence of this junk register arises from the fact that neither stabilizers nor logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators affect a logical computational basis state.
Therefore, we obtain a reduction from constant-rate
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
with any number of logical qubits
k
, with error probabilities within a constant factor of each other.
This answers Question
1.2
.
Note that we have omitted a key detail above, namely that the reduction from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
only holds in the decision variant, whereas we wish to show that Search
\mathsf{LSN}
is hard.
Almost always, a decision-to-search reduction is trivial because it is implied from efficient certification of solutions.
As we discuss below,
\mathsf{LSN}
solutions are unlikely to be efficiently verifiable, so a decision-to-search reduction is also unlikely.
We circumvent this by devising a decision-to-search reduction for
\mathsf{LSN}^{2}
, and proving a stronger reduction from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly(n)}
, which we outline below.

Theorem 1.6

(informal)
.

Fix any
k\geq 1
and
p\in(0,1)
which is not necessarily a constant.
There exists a reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}(\lfloor np/6\rfloor,2n,p/6)
to
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
.
In particular, for a constant error probability
p
, constant rate
\mathsf{LPN}
reduces to
\mathsf{LSN}
with a single logical qubit.

One application of this reduction is an explanation for the absence of
any
known sub-exponential algorithm for decoding quantum stabilizer codes in
any
non-trivial parameter regime, despite decades of study in quantum error correction.
On the classical side, in spite of much effort, no known sub-exponential algorithm succeeds on
\mathsf{LPN}
with constant rate.
The above reduction shows that the search for these two types of algorithms are connected, and gives evidence for a negative answer to Question
1.3
.

Corollary 1.7

(informal)
.

The existence of
any
sub-exponential time (in
n
) algorithm which solves
\mathsf{LSN}
at
any
rate
\frac{k}{n}
implies a substantial breakthrough in classical cryptography in the form of a sub-exponential time algorithm for
\mathsf{LPN}
at constant rate.
2
2

2

Note that the runtime for
\mathsf{LPN}
decoders is typically stated with respect to
k
, the length of the secret. In the case of
\mathsf{LSN}
, it is more natural to think of the block length
n
as the relevant parameter. Since we consider constant rates, the asymptotics are the same either way. We discuss these differences in
Section

3.2
.

Our work thus presents a new and surprising strong barrier for the existence of sub-exponential decoders of quantum codes.

1.2.2
Classical Representations of
\mathsf{LSN}

Figure 3:

A schematic representation for the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.
Everything is written in symplectic representation.
Each of the
m
samples given are of the form
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{e}_{i})
, with the goal of finding
\mathbf{y}
.
The secret bitstring
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is a random string which remains
fixed
across samples.
Here
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is a
2n\times n
matrix, where
n-k
columns represent stabilizers and the remaining
k
represent logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators.

\mathbf{B}_{i}
is a
2n\times k
matrix of the logical
\mathbf{X}
operators.

\mathbf{r}_{i}
is a randomly generated junk vector and
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is an error drawn from
n
independent single-qubit depolarizing channels each with probability
p
.
The block multiplication structure emphasizes that the stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
’s act trivially on the secret string
\mathbf{y}
; only the logical
\mathbf{X}
’s affect
\mathbf{y}
.
For illustrative clarity, this schematic ignores the fact that in the true classical representation
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is randomized in such a way that there is no distinction between stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators.

One of the main tools which we use to bridge the apparent qualitative gap between the definitions of
\mathsf{LSN}
and
\mathsf{LPN}
is a new
classical
representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
, shown in Fig.
3
.
The primary motivation for the development of such a representation is to narrow the conceptual gap between the formulations of
\mathsf{LPN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}
, towards the effort of a reduction from the former to the latter.
As given,
\mathsf{LPN}
has input
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})
where
\mathbf{A}
is a generator matrix.
On the other hand,
\mathsf{LSN}
has input
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})
for a Clifford circuit
\mathbf{C}
.
If
\mathsf{LSN}
could be represented in an equivalent form that (a) removes the quantum input, becoming fully classical, and (b) structures the given noisy code state in the form of a (non-exponential size) matrix multiplication added to some small error, the reductive path becomes significantly more plausible.

The classical representation task is given as follows. Let
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
be a random matrix whose columns are all orthogonal in the symplectic inner product.
Let
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times k}
be a random matrix with the same property, such that all columns of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
are collectively linearly independent.
Let
\mathbf{r}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be a random string, and
\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
be the symplectic representation of a Pauli drawn from the depolarizing distribution.
Fix a random string
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
The search task is, given
m
samples of
\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i}\Big)
, to find
\mathbf{y}
(in the decision version, to distinguish the given states from random).
We show below that
\mathbf{A}_{i}
corresponds to the stabilizer and logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators of the code, while
\mathbf{B}_{i}
represents the logical
\mathbf{X}
operators.
Intuitively, in
\mathsf{LSN}
logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators and stabilizers do nothing to the logical computational basis state
\mathbf{x}
, and thus can be interpreted as “gauge” degrees of freedom in the problem.
With a randomly chosen code, these operators obfuscate what is actually relevant—the logical
\mathbf{X}
’s—by adding random “junk” to the decoding problem, which we encode into the junk vector
\mathbf{r}_{i}
.
It is in fact this
n
bits of junk—a consequence of a stronger form of quantum degeneracy which includes both stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
’s—which increases the hardness of
\mathsf{LSN}
to the level of
\mathsf{LPN}
regardless of how many true logical qubits we give to the stabilizer code.

Two remarks about this classical formulation are in order.
First, we observe that this representation does indeed meet the criterion of being a simple (small) matrix multiplication with errors problem, as illustrated in Fig.
3
.
Second,
\mathsf{LPN}
is framed in terms of a generator matrix, which produces a codeword that is corrupted with noise.
Stabilizer decoding, on the other hand, is often more naturally phrased as decoding from a syndrome given by the stabilizer tableau.
Yet, in this classical representation, we have constructed a decoding problem which combined both a “generator” component (logical Paulis) and the parity check component (stabilizers) into one larger generator matrix.
This construction is unusual in this sense, consisting of embedding a parity check matrix into a larger generator matrix.

We now sketch a derivation of the classical representation, starting from
\mathsf{LSN}
itself.
Specifically, we outline a reduction from
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
to a
m
-copy classical representation problem.
The key insight is that
\mathsf{LSN}
can be framed entirely as correctly deducing a certain logical operator.
Specifically, given a code state corrupted by some random Pauli error
\mathbf{E}
, we may measure the syndrome
\mathbf{v}
to obtain a syndrome decoding problem.
If we do not care about the weight of the solution, then it is easy to find
some
Pauli which produces the same syndrome
\mathbf{v}
, e.g. by Gaussian elimination.
In fact, we may sample randomly from this space of solutions.
Such solutions, however, will differ from the true error by some combination of stabilizers and logical operators.
A randomly chosen Pauli
\mathbf{P}
with syndrome
\mathbf{v}
may be interpreted as having three components: the error itself
\mathbf{E}
, a uniformly random element of the stabilizer subgroup, and a uniformly random logical operator which we can further break down to a uniformly random logical
\mathbf{X}
and a uniformly random logical
\mathbf{Z}
.
If our noisy state is of the form
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, then
\mathbf{C^{\dagger}PEC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
equals
\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{z}}
, where
\mathbf{z}
differs from the true logical state
\mathbf{x}
by precisely the random
\mathbf{X}
logical encoded within
\mathbf{P}
.
But finding this
\mathbf{X}
logical part is precisely the task given by the classical representation—it is the task of finding
\mathbf{y}
, the logical
\mathbf{X}
part of a symplectically-represented Pauli
\mathbf{P}
which also includes some random contributions from stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators as specified by
\mathbf{r}_{i}
, in the presence of an error
\mathbf{e}
.
Therefore, the classical representation task encodes solutions to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
We apply similar arguments to give a converse, proving that the classical representation and standard
\mathsf{LSN}
are completely equivalent.
We henceforth use the classical representation to reason about the remainder of the reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
.

1.2.3
Symplectic
\mathsf{LPN}
: A Bridge Between
\mathsf{LPN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}

Figure 4:

Embedding structure for the reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
(in the classical representation).
The matrix embeddings are drawn approximately to scale.
For the code, a
\mathsf{LPN}
generator matrix is extended by a technique of “symplectic completion” into a
\mathsf{sympLPN}
generator matrix.
Further columns, corresponding to logical
\mathbf{X}
operators, are added to complete the embedding into
\mathsf{LSN}
.
For the logical state, a
\mathsf{LPN}
secret is extended into a
\mathsf{sympLPN}
secret, which is all then embedded into the
junk
portion of the
\mathsf{LSN}
logical state.
This junk embedding can be observed visually by comparing this figure to Fig.
3
.
(The actual
\mathsf{LSN}
secret is chosen completely randomly.)
For the noise, a Bernoulli error is extended to a larger Bernoulli error to embed
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
.
Mapping from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
, the dimensions remain the same, but the distribution is modified to be depolarizing instead of Bernoulli.

The classical representation narrows the conceptual gap between quantum and classical decoding from the quantum side.
We further close this gap from the classical side by defining a quantum-inspired structured version of
\mathsf{LPN}
, which we call
\mathsf{sympLPN}(n,p)
.

\mathsf{sympLPN}(n,p)
differs from
\mathsf{LPN}
primarily in the distribution of generator matrices
\mathbf{A}
.
Rather than letting
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathbb{Z}^{n\times k}_{2}
be a uniformly random matrix, we sample
\mathbf{A}
uniformly at random from the set of full-rank matrices in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
such that all columns are orthogonal under the symplectic inner product.
Since the symplectic inner product requires even dimension to be well-defined, we have replaced
n
with
2n
in the above.
Moreover, for connection with
\mathsf{LSN}
, we always fix
k=n/2
in
\mathsf{sympLPN}
.
Finally, in
\mathsf{sympLPN}
we use a depolarizing noise model (in symplectic representation) instead of Bernoulli noise.
The intuition behind such a construction is that the classical representation looks like a
\mathsf{LPN}
instance, except that (a) the generator matrix is structured in a certain way that causes certain columns to be symplectically orthogonal, (b) the noise model is depolarizing instead of Bernoulli, and (c) only part of the vector being multiplied actually contains the secret.
In this sense
\mathsf{sympLPN}
serves as a hybrid problem between
\mathsf{LPN}
and the classical formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}
which includes (a) and (b) but not (c).
Our proof technique is then to reduce
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
, and then reduce
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.

To the first reduction, we begin with a generator matrix
\mathbf{A}
sampled uniformly at random.
The goal will be to pad
\mathbf{A}
with some extra rows, using these new degrees of freedom to ensure that the columns become not only symplectically orthogonal, but a uniformly random symplectically orthogonal matrix.
Specifically, suppose we begin with a uniformly random matrix which is slightly smaller than
2n\times\ell
, namely
(2n-(1+\epsilon)\ell)\times\ell
for some small constant
\epsilon>0
such that
(1+\epsilon)\ell
is an integer.
We construct
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(1+\epsilon)\ell\times\ell}
and stack
\mathbf{A}
on top of it, creating a new matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}
such that the distribution of
\mathbf{B}
is negligibly close to a uniformly random full-rank symplectically orthogonal matrix.
The construction proceeds as follows: write

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{N}_{1}\\
\mathbf{M}\\
\mathbf{N}_{2}\\
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\end{bmatrix},

(1.3)

where
\mathbf{N}_{1},\mathbf{N}_{2}
are
(n-(1+\epsilon)l)\times l
and
\mathbf{M},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
are
(1+\epsilon)l\times l
.
In this decomposition, we may re-express the condition of symplectic orthogonality as that of a certain matrix being
symmetric
.
In particular consider the matrix,

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}:=\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\intercal}\mathbf{N}_{2}+\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}.

(1.4)

Then
S_{ij}
(
S_{ji}
) is the inner product of the top half of the
i
th (
j
th) column with the bottom half of the
j
th (
i
th) row of
\mathbf{B}
.
Hence, the symplectic inner product between columns
i
and
j
of
\mathbf{B}
is precisely given by
S_{ij}+S_{ji}
.
This is zero for all
i,j
if and only if
\mathbf{S}
is a symmetric matrix.
This new structure suggests a simple way to sample
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
in the desired way.
First, sample a uniformly random symmetric matrix
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
.
Then, subtract off the known part of Eqn. (
1.4
), creating
\mathbf{T}:=\mathbf{S}^{\prime}-\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\intercal}\mathbf{N}_{2}
.
From there, pick a uniformly random
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}
; this step is efficient since it amounts to randomly sampling solutions to a linear equation.
The only remaining uncertainty to check is the homogeneity of this approach.
That is, whether or not for every choice of
\mathbf{A}
there are (up to negligible failure probability) the same number of symplectic completions
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
, which would ensure that the distribution of
\mathbf{B}
truly is nearly that of a uniformly random symplectically orthogonal full-rank matrix.
We prove this property by direct counting arguments.

The above technique of symplectic completion addresses the code part of the reduction, but now we have enlarged the decoding problem by adding more rows.
We therefore must also supplement by adding some of our own noise so that the new noise distribution matches the desired one.
If we do not do this, then the transformed instance is easily distinguished from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
because there will never be noise in the padded rows.
Unfortunately, the only noise that we can add is uniformly random bit flips, because we can only write down the final result
z_{j}:=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{y}+e_{j}
for each padded row
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{j}
.
Since we do not know the secret
\mathbf{y}
, we have no control over the distribution of
z_{j}
for any choice of the distribution of the new error bit
e_{j}
unless it is either 0 or
\frac{1}{2}
.
To circumvent this issue, we first generate a random number of indices in the padded region in which to apply noise—so that the total error weight is approximately correct—and then let the resultant bits in those regions of the noisy codeword be uniformly random.
To erase the positional structure of the padding being at the end, we then apply a random permutation of our bits.
The same permutation is applied to both the first and last
n
bits, to ensure that symplectic orthogonality is preserved.
Careful analysis of a formal combination of these two techniques—randomized symplectic completion and noise injection—give a random full-rank symplectically orthogonal code with independent bit-flip noise.
To complete the reduction, we show how to add extra noise from an independent distribution such that the new aggregate noise distribution is depolarizing.
The parameters
\ell,\epsilon
can be chosen such that the total change in error probability is a factor of 6, as shown in Fig.
2
.

Finally, we outline the remaining piece of the reduction—from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Starting from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
, we have an instance
(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n},\;\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n})
, and we wish to embed it into the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
, which is of the form
(\mathbf{A},\;\mathbf{B},\;\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e})
.
By examining the dimensions alone, it becomes apparent that the way to encode
\mathsf{sympLPN}
into
\mathsf{LSN}
is by embedding the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
matrix into the
junk
part of the
\mathsf{LSN}
encoding matrix.
Such an embedding reveals an important problem, however: we must choose the matrix
\mathbf{B}
and the
\mathsf{LSN}
secret
\mathbf{x}
randomly ourselves, but then a Search
\mathsf{LSN}
solver would do no good—it beings us no value for a solver to output the
\mathbf{x}
we chose ourselves.
To circumvent this issue, we formally reduce from the
decision
versions of
\mathsf{sympLPN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Now, because the only goal is to decide if everything is random or if the given string is actually a noisy codeword, we can rely on the observation that the transformed
\mathsf{LSN}
problem is random (actually a noisy codeword) if and only if the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
problem is random (actually a noisy codeword).
Therefore, a distinguisher for
\mathsf{LSN}
implies a distinguisher for
\mathsf{LPN}
, which completes the reduction between the decision problems.
Figure
4
illustrates the entire sequence of embeddings from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.

For almost all classical problems, the issue of being able only to reduce directly to a decision problem when we in reality desire a reduction to a search problem is entirely trivial, because decision-to-search reductions always exist due to the ability to efficiently verify proposed solutions.
In the setting of
\mathsf{LSN}
, however, it is not at all clear how one might verify a proposed solution.
Specifically, given a noisy code state
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, and a proposed solution
\ket{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
, is there a (quantum) algorithm to check with high probability whether
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
?
Given nothing else but
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, this task is equivalent to checking if
\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
differ by a low-weight Pauli, which is essentially a short vector problem.
We therefore are unable to find a direct decision-to-search reduction for
\mathsf{LSN}
.

Fortunately, we are able to prove a decision-to-search reduction in the case of multiple samples.
More precisely, we can reduce Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{2m}(k,n,p)
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.
In this case, the key proof technique is to use randomness to our advantage.
Specifically, split up the samples into two halves.
For one set of the query the Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
solver exactly as given.
For the other, generate a random bitstring
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and apply a logical operator
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}
to each sample.
If the given Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{2m}
instance has true noisy code states with logical state
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, then there is a non-negligible probability that the solvers will output
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}
, at which point we can subtract to verify that we obtained
\mathbf{y}
.
If the instance has completely random states, then applying
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}
does nothing to the distribution, the Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
solver will have success probabilities negligibly close to random guessing.
Hence, there is a non-negligible advantage, giving a reduction.

The fact that a decision-to-search reduction appears to require multiple samples—a rather unphysical formulation of quantum decoding—is not only interesting in its own right, but it complicates our reduction from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
, because we now must reduce to multi-sample Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
instead of Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
itself.
The multi-sample version is potentially easier than
\mathsf{LSN}
, and therefore we must prove a new reduction directly to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{2}
.
This reduction is the true reduction which we give in the main text, but suffers more technical complications because we must carefully embed a single
\mathsf{sympLPN}
sample into a multi-sample version of
\mathsf{LSN}
.
In spite of this difficulty, we are able to give a reduction by devising a randomized version of a hybrid argument.
Effectively, we hide the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
input into a randomly chosen sample for the
\mathsf{LPN}^{m}
solver, and interpolate by choosing samples before the chosen one to be true code states and samples after to be completely random strings.
By analyzing this scheme using the averaging principle, we prove that a reduction to multi-sample Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
holds for any
m=\poly(n)
, thereby completing the full reduction from Decision
\mathsf{LPN}
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
, as shown in Fig.
2
.

1.2.4
Worst-case Decoding

Before we outline our results in self-reducibility, we will define and discuss equivalences between the worst-case variants of stabilizer decoding.
This is because our self-reduction results require both worst-case and average-case notions of decoding.
The classical formulation of decoding linear codes is, in the worst case, known as the nearest codeword problem
\mathsf{NCP}(k,n,w)

[
BLVW19
]
.
In this setting a code is specified by a generator matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k}
, where
n\geq k
.
Noisy codewords are of the form
\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
, where
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
.
The decision version of
\mathsf{NCP}
is to determine, given
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
whether there is a weight
\leq w
error
\mathbf{e}
such that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
The search version is to find
\mathbf{x}
, promised that
\mathbf{y}
is indeed of the above form.
Quantumly, we may define an analogous decision task as follows: given a Clifford operator
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
(e.g. specified classically as a circuit) and a state
\ket{\phi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n}
, decide if there exists a weight
\leq w
Pauli
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\ket{\psi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
such that
\ket{\phi}=\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
.
We denote the quantum nearest code state problem
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
.
The search formulation of
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
suffers from a fragility to changes in the definition, which we now sketch.

Figure 5:
Web of reductions between worst-case quantum decoding problems. The parameters are the logical qubits
k
, physical qubits
n
, and maximum error weight
w
.
\mathsf{QNCP}
is the task of recovering the underlying logical state;
\mathsf{recQNCP}
synthesizing a correct recovery operator;
\mathsf{errQNCP}
outputting a low-weight recovery operator;
\mathsf{QSDP}
the problem of finding a low-weight error causing a given syndrome. The dotted arrow with an asterisk signifies that the search-to-decision reduction for
\mathsf{errQNCP}
actually requires a Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
solver for every
w^{\prime}\leq w
, not just a solver with
w
itself.

The following three formulations of Search
\mathsf{NCP}
are exactly equivalent in the sense that if an efficient algorithm succeeds in any one task with probability
q
, there is an efficient algorithm on any other task which succeeds with probability
q
: find
\mathbf{x}
, synthesize a vector
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{Ax}
, or find
\mathbf{e}
.
This is because if we know
\mathbf{x}
, we can compute
\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{Ax}
.
If we know
\mathbf{e}
, we can compute
\mathbf{Ax}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{e}
and use Gaussian elimination to compute
\mathbf{x}
.
Finally, finding the recovery vector
\mathbf{v}
amounts to finding
\mathbf{e}
itself.
In the setting of quantum codes, however, these three notions are
not
immediately equivalent.
The analogous three formulations for Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
are as follows:
find
\ket{\psi}
(Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
); find any
\mathbf{E^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{E^{\prime}E}
is a stabilizer (Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
); and find a low-weight
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{E^{\prime}E}
is a stabilizer of the code, e.g. find
\mathbf{E}
itself, if all stabilizers are high weight (Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
).
We say that
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
is a
recovery operator
for
\mathbf{E}
if
\mathbf{E^{\prime}E}
is a stabilizer.
Certainly
\mathsf{QNCP}
reduces to
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, which reduces to
\mathsf{errQNCP}
, but the reverse directions are significantly less clear.
In particular, suppose we have a solver for
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, which generates with probability
2/3
some Pauli
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{E^{\prime}E}
is a stabilizer.
Any algorithm which upon calling this solver can solve
\mathsf{errQNCP}
with non-negligible probability is by
definition
a solver for a certain short vector problem (SVP), similar to the (binary) SVP problem introduced in
[
AHI+17
]
.
Specifically, the SVP is finding a low-weight element of the coset space
\mathbf{E}\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the stabilizer subgroup.
By using the symplectic representation, wherein
n
-qubit Paulis are represented by a bitstring of length
2n
(the first
n
bits indicating
\mathbf{X}
’s and last
n
bits indicating
\mathbf{Z}
’s), such a coset is the coset space of a linear subspace over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and is a bona fide short vector problem.
We believe therefore that it is unlikely that a reduction exists from
\mathsf{errQNCP}
to
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, given that it implies an efficient algorithm for a variant of SVP.

It is similarly not immediately clear as to whether
\mathsf{recQNCP}
reduces to
\mathsf{QNCP}
.
Given a solver to
\mathsf{QNCP}
which with probability
2/3
yields the correct state
\ket{\psi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
, how could we extract the correct recovery operator?
There is no direct analog of “subtracting” out the logical state in the classical sense, wherein
(\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})-\mathbf{Ax}=\mathbf{e}
.
Nonetheless, it turns out that
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}
are exactly equivalent, but to prove it we must pass through the intermediate problem of
syndrome decoding
.
This problem, which we call
\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
, is the problem of synthesizing a valid recovery operator given a syndrome.
It is entirely classical in the sense that both input and output are classical.
From the standard picture of stabilizer codes, the syndrome is obtained by measuring the stabilizers one by one, recording
+1
if the stabilizer commutes with the error, and
-1
otherwise.
In our case, it is convenient to define
\mathsf{QSDP}
completely in terms of the symplectic representation.
First, write each stabilizer of the code
\mathbf{S}_{i}
as a symplectic vector
\mathbf{h}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
.
We express the symplectic representation explicitly via the bijection
\Symp
.
The syndrome
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
becomes a vector such that
v_{i}=0
if
\mathbf{S}_{i}
commutes with the error and
1
if not.
The decoding problem is to then output some
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
such that
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime})\mathbf{E}
is a stabilizer.
A corresponding decision problem is to decide if there exists a low-weight error
\mathbf{e}
which produces the given syndrome
\mathbf{v}
.
As decision problems,
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{QSDP}
are tightly equivalent in that an efficient algorithm succeeding with probability
q
for one implies an efficient algorithm succeeding with probability
q
for the other.
Since the syndrome does not depend on the underlying logical state, such an equivalence proves that the choice of the logical state itself for Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
bears no relevance to its hardness.
The reduction from
\mathsf{QNCP}
to
\mathsf{QSDP}
is immediate: measure the syndrome and call the syndrome decoder.
In the other direction, we exploit the fact that Gaussian elimination enables us to produce Pauli errors which when applied to a clean code state produce
some
noisy code state with the same syndrome as the one given,
\mathbf{v}
.
This noisy code state will differ from a clean code state by a low-weight Pauli error if and only if the original code state differs from a clean code state by a low-weight Pauli error, completing the reduction.
Reducing from Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
to
\mathsf{QSDP}
is a straightforward generalization of the decision reduction.

The reduction from
\mathsf{QSDP}
to Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
is slightly more subtle.
Again we can use Gaussian elimination to produce a Pauli error
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
that gives the same syndrome as the given one.
However, we must explicitly transform this error to that of a valid recovery operator.
In general,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
is a product of a valid recovery operator, some logical
\mathbf{X}
, and some logical
\mathbf{Z}
.
Thus, it is enough to deduce what the logical parts are.
We may do this by calling the
\mathsf{QNCP}
solver twice.
First, we call it on
\mathbf{E^{\prime}C}\ket{0^{n}}
, which gives the logical
\mathbf{X}
part.
Then, it call it on
\mathbf{E^{\prime}C}\ket{0^{n-k},+^{k}}
, which gives the logical
\mathbf{Z}
part.
Finally, since Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
and Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
are both tasks for synthesizing recovery operators, we can show by techniques similar to the proof of equivalence between Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
and Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}
that the two tasks are equivalent.
Hence, we have proven the following piece of Fig.
5
.

Theorem 1.8

(informal)
.

Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
and Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}
are equivalent.
Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
, Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
, and Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
are all equivalent in the sense that an efficient algorithm which solves any one problem with high probability exists if and only if an efficient algorithm which solves any other problem with high probability exists.
Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
reduces to Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
.

These equivalences are proven formally in the setting where a solution to
\mathsf{QSDP}
,
\mathsf{QNCP}
,
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, or
\mathsf{errQNCP}
is an algorithm that is correct with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
For any of these problems, that correctness probability can be amplified to
1-\negl(n)
.
For
\mathsf{errQNCP}
, this may be done by repeating the algorithm, and verifying if the output is indeed a low-weight error and has the correct syndrome.
For
\mathsf{QSDP}
, we can run the solver many times and group outputs together
if they differ by a stabilizer. This is efficiently computable, since it is easy to test membership in a
stabilizer subgroup. To amplify, we choose the equivalence class corresponding to the group with the
most members. This majority vote boosts the success probability close to 1.
Using the equivalence of
\mathsf{QSDP}
with
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, the success probabilities of these two problems can likewise be amplified.

1.2.5
Reductions Between Search and Decision

We next outline the reductions we make between search and decision variants of the stabilizer decoding problem, both in the worst and average case.
For search-to-decision reductions, all of our approaches involve sequentially learning one bit of information at a time by querying a Decision solver with a carefully constructed input.
In the worst case, we use an approach inspired by the technique of a classical decoding search-to-decision reduction.
Specifically, we reduce Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
to Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w^{\prime})
, assuming that we have a solver for every
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
This assumption is quite strong—it enables a reduction from the hardest version of the search problem—but it is unclear if any reduction can be made, even from the weaker variant Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
, with a weaker assumption.
The reduction proceeds by learning one bit of the Pauli error itself at a time.
In particular, we first query the Decision solver for the existence of a solution with weight
\leq w
.
If one exists, then we apply
\mathbf{X}
to the first qubit and query with weight
\leq w-1
.
We do the same with
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}
until we get a
YES
response.
If we do get a
YES
on Pauli
\mathbf{P}
, we know that a low-weight error has
\mathbf{P}
on it.
If we never get a
YES
, we know that the error we seek is not supported on the first qubit.
We can then proceed in sequence to the next qubit, and so on.
In the average case, can directly reduce from Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m_{1}}(k,n,p)
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m_{2}}(k,n,p)
, where
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
but both are
\poly(n)
, so long as
p\gg\frac{\log n}{n}
.
In this case, the key insight is that the randomness of the encoding Clifford operator enables us to apply extra Clifford operations of our choice without affecting the distribution of the Clifford.
Specifically, we can select a random Pauli and apply a controlled version of it where the control is the first logical qubit.
One can show that if the Pauli is applied, then the problem has the distribution of a true noisy code state, while if it is not applied, then the problem has the distribution of a random state.
Therefore, the Decision solver which can distinguish these two situations will determine the value of the first logical qubit.
This procedure can be repeated sequentially to extract the entire logical state, thereby solving the search problem.
We have in this sketch omitted technical complications arising from the fact that all qubits needs to be re-randomized, but the qubit which serves as the control cannot be randomized by the controlled Pauli.
This issue can be addressed by using the Pauli error which comes as part of the
\mathsf{LSN}
problem to scramble this final qubit.

As for decision-to-search reductions, we sketched in the previous section the procedure in the average case.
However, the proof strategy relied critically on the fact that the decision problem was to distinguish between a true
\mathsf{LSN}
noisy code state and a completely random state.
In the worst case, the decision problem is formulated as determining whether or not a solution exists at all, which appears substantially harder.
In fact, in light of the search-to-decision reduction from Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
to Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
, a decision-to-search reduction would imply at least some form of equivalence between Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
and Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
, which effectively would require solving the short vector problem discussed earlier.
We therefore believe it unlikely that such a reduction holds in the worst case.
For the same reasons, however, there is an immediate decision-to-search reduction to Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
.
We summarize our reductions below.

Theorem 1.9

(informal)
.

There is a reduction between Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m_{1}}(k,n,p)
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m_{2}}(k,n,p)
, where
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
are both
\poly(n)
and
k=\omega(\log n/n)
.
There is a reduction between Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{2m}(k,n,p)
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.
There is a reduction between Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
and the set of problems Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w^{\prime})
for all
w^{\prime}\leq w
, and a reduction from Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
to Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
.

1.2.6
Random Self-(ir)reducibility

One feature of
\mathsf{LPN}
is its worst-case to average-case reduction, the random self-reduction shown by
[
BLVW19
]
.
They show that for a class of worst-case instances of
\mathsf{LPN}
, balanced linear codes, an instance
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})
can be re-randomized.
In particular, by applying a well-chosen distribution of sparse matrices
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times n}
, they show that
(\mathbf{RA},(\mathbf{RA})\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{Re})
is close to a uniformly random instance, with
\mathbf{RA}
close to a uniformly random code and
\mathbf{Re}
close to independent Bernoulli noise.
The parameters of the reduction are rather weak: the initial error weight is
\sim\log^{2}n
, and the problem reduces to
\mathsf{LPN}
with error probability
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Nevertheless, it still establishes that a particularly hard version of
\mathsf{LPN}
is harder than a non-trivial class of easier worst-case instances.

We show that there are significant barriers to a similar type of reduction for quantum stabilizer codes.
In this case, the analogous reduction would find some distribution of Cliffords
\mathcal{R}_{n}
such that for certain starting Cliffords
\mathbf{C}
,
(\mathbf{RC},\mathbf{REC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})
is close to a sample of
\mathsf{LSN}
, for
\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
In particular, the sample can be rewritten as
(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{C},\mathbf{(RER^{\dagger})RC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})
.
Hence, showing that
\mathbf{RC}
is close to uniformly random while
\mathbf{RER^{\dagger}}
is still a low-weight error would be sufficient to complete the reduction.
However, there is a subtlety here, namely that
\mathbf{RC}
need not be a uniformly random Clifford to have a reduction.
Indeed, there could be two Cliffords
\mathbf{C}_{1}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}
that both implement the same encoding on input states
\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
.
Extra degrees of freedom which do not affect the distribution of the encoding need not be included in the design of
\mathbf{R}
, as we can always randomize these degrees of freedom without any change to the encoded state.

However, while different Cliffords can correspond to the same encoding of code states, if the stabilizer subspaces
\langle\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{C}_{1}\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}\mathbf{C}_{1}^{\dagger}\rangle
and
\langle\mathbf{C}_{2}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{C}_{2}\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}\mathbf{C}_{2}^{\dagger}\rangle
are different, the encodings are always different.
Hence, we may demonstrate barriers for reductions by showing that these stabilizer subspaces cannot be randomized.
Our first barrier uses precisely this model.
We show with entropy arguments that no distribution of “sparse” Cliffords, i.e. Clifford operators whose symplectic representation is sparse, has sufficient entropy to randomize over stabilizer subspaces.
A sparse Clifford distribution is the most reasonable distribution for randomizing an encoding without amplifying errors, just as sparse matrices
\mathbf{R}
were the most promising to randomize linear codes
\mathbf{A}
while not amplifying the error weight of
\mathbf{e}
excessively.
Indeed, a distribution of Cliffords that is not sparse but somehow does not amplify almost all low-weight errors seems unlikely to exist.
We show that no distribution of sparse Cliffords suffices either, because the uniform distribution over stabilizer subspaces has entropy
\Omega(n^{2})
, but any distribution of sparse Cliffords has entropy
o(n^{2})
.

A similarly challenging subtlety is that it is technically only necessary to scramble the distribution of
subspaces
, not the basis representing the subspaces.
In other words, it is possible to have a distribution over stabilizer tableaus which is far from uniform, but represents a uniformly random stabilizer subspace.
While possible, such distributions are exotic and no explicit examples are known.
Unfortunately, we show significant barriers against a reduction that randomizes the tableau.
We prove unconditionally using a symmetry argument that no tableau-randomizing reduction exists if the worst-case code is arbitrary.
The question remains as to whether a reduction could exist for a restricted set of codes.
We study this question under a concrete model, namely a sequence of random local Cliffords.
This model has some semblance of locality, yet is not bounded absolutely in sparsity.
In this setting, we show that it is impossible to randomize over stabilizer tableaus without also randomizing errors.
The key method is by characterizing the rate of Pauli mixing—in order for a distribution of Clifford operators to randomize stabilizer tableaus (not just the stabilizer subspace but a basis), the distribution must be randomizing at least one Pauli, namely one of the stabilizers.
But our Pauli mixing results show that the rate of mixing of any two Paulis are nearly identical, so that if one Pauli is taken close to a uniform distribution, then the error
\mathbf{E}
must also be randomized soon after.
It is therefore impossible to use this model to randomize the stabilizer tableau without also amplifying the error.
The techniques for this specific model show that even randomizing a single Pauli forces any others to be randomized as well, a result that is interestingly much stronger than necessary here, and that could be of independent interest.
Taken together, these results imply that if any notion of a random self-reduction exists for stabilizer decoding, it must satisfy very exotic properties, including (a) having unbounded sparsity while also not mapping most Paulis to maximal weight and (b) scrambling the code spaces without scrambling the stabilizer tableau.
Alternatively, the reduction would have to increase the dimensions
k,n
of the code, a slightly unconventional model that we do not consider here.

1.3
Related Work

Classically, the hardness of decoding a random classical linear code has been studied thoroughly
[
ACPS09
,
GRS08
,
JW05
,
FGKP06
,
AG11
,
Ale03
,
Pie12
,
HB01
,
Lyu05
,
YZW+19
]
.
Often,
\mathsf{LPN}
is phrased in terms of a
query problem
: an oracle which knows the secret
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
can be queried, at which point it gives a random draw of
\mathbf{a}_{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and
e_{i}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)
and returns
\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{x}+e_{i}
.
An efficient algorithm, for example, is allowed to query oracle as many times
n
as it would like, bounded only by being
n=\poly(k)
for efficiency’s sake.
For the purpose of coding theory, however, we are interested in a more fine-grained formulation.
Specifically, the goodness of a code is loosely measured by its rate and distance.
The distance is not within our control, but the rate
R=\frac{k}{n}
, where
n
is the number of physical bits, or, equivalently, the number of queries.
Therefore, in our work, we use
n
to specifically study
\mathsf{LPN}
with a fixed number of queries.
This formulation explicitly interprets
\mathsf{LPN}
as decoding a randomly chosen code of a given rate, and also makes clear the valid parameter regime for certain attack algorithms.
For example,
[
BKW03
]
gives a
2^{O(k/\log k)}
time algorithm which operates in the regime when
n
is superpolynomial in
k
, and
[
Lyu05
]
gives a
2^{O(k/\log\log k)}
time algorithm which operates in the regime where
n
is slightly more than linear in
k
.
Importantly, there is no sub-exponential algorithm known after many decades of effort in the regime where
n=\Theta(k)
, i.e. at constant rate.

Until the work of
[
PQS25
]
, the study of decoding a stabilizer code has focused on the worst case, and usually on a certain variant of decoding known as maximum-likelihood decoding
[
HL11
,
IP15
,
KL20
]
.
Such a model has two flavors in the quantum realm.
First, given a noisy code state, the task is to output the most likely error to have been applied, assuming that the error was drawn from a given fixed distribution such as the depolarizing channel.
The second flavor is known as degenerate maximum-likelihood decoding, and arises from the fact that many Paulis act identically on a code state; these are the
stabilizers
of the code.
Therefore, one can instead ask what the most likely equivalence class of errors occurred given the noisy code state, i.e. the set of all errors which differ mutually by a stabilizer.
The presence of such an equivalence class is an inherently quantum effect known as
quantum degeneracy
.
Because there are exponentially many stabilizers, the latter model is known to be
#P
-complete in the worst case
[
IP15
]
, rather than
NP
-complete as in the former case
[
HL11
,
KL20
]
.
Intuitively, this upgraded hardness arises from the fact that a carefully designed stabilizer code can have error equivalence class with extremely similar probabilities.
As a result, one must add up exponentially many error probabilities to compute the probability of each equivalence class and compare them, giving an intractable counting problem.

In practice, however, the purpose of decoding is unambiguous—to recover the underlying logical state.
It does not matter how one chooses to approach this recovery, whether through maximum likelihood decoding or any other technique.
Our formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}
—both in worst- and average-case variants—is therefore to recover the logical state, consistent with
[
PQS25
]
.
Moreover, given a random stabilizer code, it is not clear whether the effects quantum degeneracy play any significant role in complexity at all.
As a consequence,
[
IP15
]
remarked that an important question of study is precisely Question
1.1
, and the role of quantum degeneracy in it.
Part of our contributions may be viewed as a direct answer to these questions—in our reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
at constant rate to
\mathsf{LSN}
with even a single qubit, we show that this increased hardness of decoding random stabilizer codes is explicit sourced from quantum degeneracy.
Therefore, in the worst case, quantum degeneracy lifts certain models of stabilizer decoding into a much harder complexity class, while in the average case, quantum degeneracy lifts the easiest random quantum decoding problem to at least the level of the hardest random classical decoding problem.

As discussed previously, the formal definition of decoding random stabilizer codes was proposed only recently by
[
PQS25
]
.
Their initial assessment of this problem,
\mathsf{LSN}
, included an exponential-time attack algorithm and a proof that
\mathsf{LSN}
is contained within a certain unitary complexity class known as
\mathsf{unitaryBQP}^{\mathsf{NP}}
.
They do not, however, make progress on Question
1.1
.
Namely, they explicitly leave Questions
1.2
and
1.3
open.
Moreover, they consider only random self-reductions in the context of self-reducibility as phrased in Question
1.4
, and are only able to show a random self-reduction that (a) reduces into an unnatural distribution of problem instances that depends extremely heavily on the worst case instance, which is not the conventionally accepted definition of an average-case problem, (b) succeeds with at most inverse-quasipolynomial probability, which is negligible, and (c) only applies in a regime of worst-case instances wherein even brute-force computation succeeds in quasipolynomial time.
Despite these limitations of their reduction, it is unclear as to how it could be improved at all.
In addition to answering the remaining questions about self-reducibility (between search and decision), this work sheds light into why the random self-reduction of
[
PQS25
]
is difficult to improve upon, by proving that reasonable reductions do not exist at all.

Organization.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we give the requisite facts about quantum coding theory and random quantum states to which we refer in the remainder of the paper.
Section
3
formally defines the
\mathsf{LSN}
problem, as well as its worst-case variant, which we call the quantum nearest code state problem (
\mathsf{QNCP}
) in reference to the classical nearest codeword problem (
\mathsf{NCP}
).
Here, we define multiple variants of these problems—all of which being classically equivalent—and discuss how certain quantum phenomena render them inequivalent (or, in cases where they are non-trivially equivalent, we prove them).
In Section
4
, we introduce a useful representation of the
\mathsf{LSN}
task which is completely classical.
The classical representation is a key technical tool for our proofs.
We show in Section
5
that
\mathsf{LSN}
satisfies a certain search-to-decision reduction, as well as a decision-to-search reduction (which is non-trivial, as we will show, because solutions are not efficiently verifiable).
However, under certain structural assumptions,
\mathsf{LSN}
does not admit a random self-reduction.
In Section
6
, we prove that
\mathsf{LPN}
with constant rate reduces to
\mathsf{LSN}
with arbitrary
k
, including
k=1
.
Finally, we conclude with discussion in Section
7
.

2
Preliminaries

We establish our notation for common objects in stabilizer coding theory, including the Pauli and Clifford groups, stabilizer codes, and the noise model we consider.

2.1
Pauli and Clifford Groups

Let
\mathrm{U}(d)
be the group of
d\times d
unitary matrices.
The operators
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\in\mathrm{U}(2)
denote the usual Pauli operators, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}:=|0\rangle\langle 0|+|1\rangle\langle 1|,\;\mathbf{X}:=|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|,\;\mathbf{Y}:=-i|0\rangle\langle 1|+i|1\rangle\langle 0|,\;\mathbf{Z}:=|0\rangle\langle 0|-|1\rangle\langle 1|.

(2.1)

The Pauli operators square to the identity, and further satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{P}_{j}=-\mathbf{P}_{j}\mathbf{P}_{i},\;\mathbf{XY}=\mathbf{Z},\;\mathbf{YZ}=\mathbf{X},\;\mathbf{ZX}=\mathbf{Y}.

(2.2)

A
n
-qubit Pauli operator is an operator of the form
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{P}_{n}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})^{\otimes n}
, where
\mathbf{P}_{i}\in\set{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
.
When convenient, we omit the explicit
\otimes
and write the Paulis as strings
\mathbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{n}
.
The
weight
of
\mathbf{P}
, denoted
\wt(\mathbf{P})
is given by the number of operators in the string which are not
\mathbf{I}
.
The set of
n
-qubit Pauli operators form a group in the following way.

Definition 2.1

(Pauli group)
.

Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}:=\set{c\mathbf{P}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{P}^{(n)}\,:\,c\in\set{\pm 1,\pm i},\mathbf{P}^{(i)}\in\set{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}}

(2.3)

be the set of
n
-qubit Pauli operators with a phase.
By Eqn. (
2.2
),
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
is a group under the standard product, known as the
n
-qubit
Weyl-Heisenberg group
.
We call
\mathcal{P}_{n}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}/\langle i\mathbf{I}\rangle
the
n
-qubit
Pauli group
, which consists of all Pauli strings with no phase.

Almost always, when we refer to a Pauli, we are implicitly excluding the identity string
\mathbf{I}
; this is inferred from context.
We next define the Clifford group, a larger group of operators whose normalizer is the Pauli group.

Definition 2.2

(Clifford group)
.

The
n
-qubit Clifford group
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{n}:=\set{\mathbf{V}\in\mathrm{U}(2^{n})\,:\,\mathbf{V}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{V}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\,\forall\mathbf{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}

(2.4)

under the standard product.

Let
\ket{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{0}\pm\ket{1})
.
A basic fact about the Clifford group is that it can be generated by gates
\mathbf{H}_{i}
,
\mathbf{S}_{i}
, and
\mathbf{CX}_{ij}

[
NC10
]
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|+\rangle\langle 0|_{i}+|-\rangle\langle 1|_{i}),\;\mathbf{S}_{i}:=|0\rangle\langle 0|_{i}+i|1\rangle\langle 1|_{i},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{CX}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=|00\rangle\langle 00|_{ij}+|01\rangle\langle 01|_{ij}+|11\rangle\langle 10|_{ij}+|10\rangle\langle 11|_{ij}.

(2.6)

The subscript on
i
indicates that the gate only acts on the
i^{\text{th}}
qubit. The coefficient
i
in the description of
\mathbf{S}_{i}
is
\sqrt{-1}
.
Implicitly, the remaining qubits all have the identity acted upon them.
By generated, we mean that every
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
can be written as a finite product of these generators.

Definition 2.3

(Clifford circuit)
.

A
n
-qubit Clifford circuit is a sequence of gates, each of which is
\mathbf{H}_{i}
,
\mathbf{S}_{i}
, or
\mathbf{CX}_{ij}
for some
i,j
. A specification of this circuit is done so classically.

2.2
Stabilizer Codes and Logical Operations

A set of
n
-qubit Paulis
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is called
independent
if for any subset
\mathbf{P}_{i_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{i_{m}}
,
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{P}_{i_{j}}\neq\mathbf{I}
.
Equivalently, no Pauli in the set can be written as the product of other Paulis in the set.

Definition 2.4
