(Stabilizer codes)
.

A
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer code
S
is the space of states which are
+1
eigenstates of
n-k
independent commuting Pauli strings
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. The set of
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer codes is denoted
\Stab(n,k)
.

If a state is a linear combination of
+1
eigenstates of
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
, then the state itself is also a
+1
eigenstate of the same operators. Thus a stabilizer code is a linear subspace of
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n}
.
One can specify a stabilizer code by providing a valid choice of
n-k
Pauli strings.
The resulting
(n-k)\times n
table of Pauli operators is called the
stabilizer tableau
representation of the corresponding code.
Due to the Gottesman-Knill theorem
[
Got24
]
, a stabilizer code is generated by a corresponding Clifford operator
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
via the map
\ket{0^{n-k}}\otimes\ket{\psi}\to\mathbf{U}\ket{0^{n-k}}\otimes\ket{\psi}
.
The stabilizers of this code are
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathbf{Z}_{1}&=\,\,\mathbf{Z}&\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{Z}_{2}&=\,\,\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{Z}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{I}\\
&\vdots\,\,\,\,\,\,&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}&=\,\,\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{Z}&\mathbf{I}&\cdots&\mathbf{I}\end{matrix}

(2.7)

Remark 2.5
.

One way to generate a uniformly random
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer code is to choose a uniformly random Clifford
\mathbf{U}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
, and to output
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}

[
Smi06
,
PQS25
]
.

The stabilizers
\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
themselves generate a subgroup of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, which we denote
\mathcal{S}
.
Stabilizer codes generated by Paulis which are each either purely comprised of
\mathbf{X}
operators or of
\mathbf{Z}
operators are known as Calderbank-Steane-Shor (CSS) codes
[
CS96
,
Ste96
]
.
CSS codes are in a certain sense the most general class of stabilizer codes which are “essentially classical”.

We remark that two stabilizer tableaus can specify the same stabilizer code because there are many choices of basis for a vector space.
Operations which preserve the code space are known as
logical operators
.
In this work, we will be primarily interested in logical operators which are themselves Pauli operations.

Definition 2.6

(Logical Paulis)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n-k}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be a stabilizer tableau of a code
S
which generates a subgroup
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathcal{P}_{n}
. The logical Paulis of
S
is the set
\Log(S)=\set{\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\,:\,\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{Q}\in\mathcal{S},\forall\mathbf{P}\in\mathcal{S}}
.

The logical Paulis of
S
themselves form a group.

Definition 2.7

(Code distance)
.

The distance of a code
S
is
d=\min\set{\wt(\mathbf{P})\,:\,\mathbf{P}\in\Log(S)}
.

The detection of errors in stabilizer codes occurs by measuring the stabilizer generators
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
, which each yield a sign
\pm 1
known collectively as a syndrome
\mathbf{a}\in\set{\pm 1}^{n-k}
.

a_{i}=-1
if and only if the error
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{P}_{i}
anticommute.
A basic fact about the code distance is that any Paulis of weight
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
give unique stabilizer syndromes.

Symplectic Representation.

It is often convenient to represent the stabilizer tableau as a binary matrix, which can be done by mapping Paulis into bitstrings known as symplectic representations.

Definition 2.8

(Symplectic isomorphism)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{P}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be a (phase-free)
n
-qubit Pauli operator, where
\mathbf{P}^{(i)}\in\set{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. We define

\displaystyle\Symp\,:\,\mathcal{P}_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}

(2.8)

to map
\mathbf{P}
to
\mathbf{y}
, such that
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i+n})
is
(0,0)
if
\mathbf{P}^{(i)}=\mathbf{I}
,
(1,0)
if
\mathbf{P}^{(i)}=\mathbf{X}
,
(0,1)
if
\mathbf{P}^{(i)}=\mathbf{Z}
, and
(1,1)
if
\mathbf{P}^{(i)}=\mathbf{Y}
.

\Symp
is a bijective map because there is a bijection between
\set{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
.
It is also a homomorphism between
\mathcal{P}_{n}
as a group under the standard product and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
as a group under addition.
As such,
\Symp
is an isomorphism that completely characterizes elements of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Definition 2.9

(Symplectic form and inner product)
.

Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times 2n}

(2.9)

be the
symplectic form
.
The symplectic inner product between
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
is defined as
\mathbf{v}\odot\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{v}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mathbf{w}
.
If
\mathbf{v}\odot\mathbf{w}=0
, then we say that
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are
symplectically orthogonal
.

Vectors
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are symplectically orthogonal if and only if their corresponding Paulis
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{v}),\;\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
commute.

Definition 2.10

(Symplectic check matrix)
.

A matrix
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
is a
symplectic check matrix
if every pair of rows in
\mathbf{H}
are symplectically orthogonal. The symplectic check matrix corresponding to a stabilizer tableau
\mathcal{T}=\set{\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}}
, denoted
\Symp(\mathcal{T})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times m}
, is a symplectic check matrix such that the
i
th row of
\Symp(\mathcal{T})
is
\Symp(\mathbf{P}_{i})
.

A symplectic check matrix is simply a bitstring representation of a valid stabilizer tableau.
It follows that a uniformly random symplectic check matrix in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
corresponds to a uniformly random
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer code.

The stabilizer tableau/symplectic check matrix and the corresponding Clifford circuit are equivalent in the sense that one can be efficiently computed from the other.

Theorem 2.11

(Equivalence of circuits and tableaus)
.

Given a stabilizer tableau of
n-k

n
-qubit Paulis
\mathbf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
or a symplectic check matrix, there exists a classical algorithm running in polynomial time which computes a description of a Clifford circuit
\mathbf{C}
such that the tableau
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{C}\mathbf{Z}_{n-k}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
generates the same stabilizer subgroup as
\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}
. The same holds vice versa: given
\mathbf{C}
, there is a polynomial time classical algorithm computing a corresponding stabilizer tableau.

Proof.

The forward direction is a classic result based on a symplectic variant of Gaussian elimination, and the backward direction is equivalent to the Gottesman-Knill theorem
[
Got24
,
NC10
]
.
∎

In the symplectic representation, the syndrome
\mathbf{a}\in\set{\pm 1}^{n-k}
transforms into
\mathbf{v}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\mathbf{a})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
.
If an error
\mathbf{E}
gives stabilizer syndrome
\mathbf{a}
on a code
S
, then
\mathbf{H\Omega}\Symp(\mathbf{E})=\mathbf{v}
, where
\mathbf{H}
is the corresponding check matrix.

E
is a stabilizer or logical operator if and only if

\mathbf{H\Omega}\Symp(\mathbf{E})=0
.

Definition 2.12

(Sympectic representation of Clifford operator)
.

For any Clifford operator
\mathbf{C}
, it has an action on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
by conjugation, taking
\mathbf{P}
to
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{C}^{-1}
.
We define the matrix
\mathbf{T}=\Symp(\mathbf{C})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times 2n}
to be the corresponding linear map such that

\mathbf{T}\cdot\Symp(\mathbf{P})=\Symp(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{C}^{-1}).

(2.10)

Linear maps
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times 2n}
that preserve the symplectic inner product
\odot
correspond precisely to Clifford operators
\mathbf{C}
.
Furthermore, any two Cliffords that have the same symplectic representation differ by a Pauli operator, i.e. if
\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{1})=\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{2})
, then
\mathbf{C}_{1}=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{C}_{2}
for some Pauli operator
\mathbf{P}
.

2.3
Noise Models

For the purpose of this work, a noise model is a distribution of linear operators which are applied to a quantum state.
It is known that any noise model is equivalent to a noise model in which the support of the distribution consists exclusively of
n
-qubit Pauli operators
[
Sho95
]
.
The most standard such model—and the model in which we will be interested—is a model in which every Pauli occurs with equal probability.

Definition 2.13

(Depolarizing channel)
.

The 1-qubit depolarizing channel
\mathcal{D}_{p}
with parameter
p\in[0,\frac{3}{4}]
is a distribution over
1
-qubit Pauli operators such that
\Pr[\mathbf{I}]=1-p
, and
\Pr[\mathbf{P}]=\frac{p}{3}
for each
\mathbf{P}\in\set{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
.
As a quantum channel, the action of
\mathcal{D}_{p}
on a state
\rho\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{p}(\rho)=(1-p)\rho+\frac{p}{3}\mathbf{X}\rho\mathbf{X}+\frac{p}{3}\mathbf{Y}\rho\mathbf{Y}+\frac{p}{3}\mathbf{Z}\rho\mathbf{Z}=\left(1-\frac{4}{3}p\right)\rho+\frac{4}{3}p\operatorname{Tr}[\rho]\frac{\mathbf{I}}{2}.

(2.11)

For a
n
-qubit state, we use the noise model
\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
, i.e. independent single-qubit depolarizing noise on each qubit.
Such a model is the most widely considered for purposes of using quantum error-correcting codes as a
quantum memory
, though there are more sophisticated models required for error-corrected quantum
computation
which we do not consider here.
Nevertheless, even for models restricted to Pauli errors, there are many reasonable distributions that one can consider for practical purposes.
For example, we can decouple the
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
errors (so that the probability of a
\mathbf{Y}
error is equal to the probability that
both
an
\mathbf{X}
and a
\mathbf{Z}
error occur), or bias the noise so that one Pauli error is substantially more likely than the others.
While such models are certainly useful as techniques to capture the exact noise model of a certain quantum device, they are not natural relative to uniformly random stabilizer codes, because such a code is symmetric under a relabeling of
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}
, and
\mathbf{Z}
.
Therefore, for the purpose of studying the complexity of decoding a random stabilizer code, it is natural to fix
\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
as the error model.

Recall that classically, the noise model is always given by
\mathsf{Ber}(p)
independently for each bit.
We now show that there is a duality between the Bernoulli and depolarizing distributions, in the sense that we can always decompose a depolarizing distribution with probability parameter
p
into the product of independent random
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
, and
\mathbf{Z}
errors each with a different probability
q
.
In the symplectic representation, this decomposition appears as the sum of three independent Bernoulli-type random variables.

Lemma 2.14

(Depolarizing decomposition)
.

Let
b_{\mathbf{X}},b_{\mathbf{Y}},b_{\mathbf{Z}}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(q)
be independent random bits.
Then
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{X}^{b_{\mathbf{X}}}\mathbf{Y}^{b_{\mathbf{Y}}}\mathbf{Z}^{b_{\mathbf{Z}}}
(as a phase-free Pauli) is distributed as
\mathcal{D}_{p}
, where
p=3[q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2}]
for any
q\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
p\in[0,\frac{3}{4}]
.

Proof.

Under
\mathcal{D}_{p}
, the probabilities of
\mathbf{I},\;\mathbf{X},\;\mathbf{Y},\;\mathbf{Z}
are respectively
1-p,\;p/3,\;p/3,\;p/3
.
Consider the distribution of a sampling process in which we apply a
\mathbf{X}
error with probability
q
, and independently a
\mathbf{Z}
error with probability
q
.
Then the probabilities of
\mathbf{I},\;\mathbf{X},\;\mathbf{Y},\;\mathbf{Z}
are respectively
(1-q)^{2},\;q(1-q),\;q^{2},\;q(1-q)
.
Now, also apply a
\mathbf{Y}
error with probability
q
.
Then the probabilities of
\mathbf{I},\;\mathbf{X},\;\mathbf{Y},\;\mathbf{Z}
become
(1-q)^{3}+q^{3},\;q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2},\;q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2},\;q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2}
.
This is precisely the distribution of
\mathcal{D}_{p}
as claimed.
∎

Corollary 2.15

(Bernoulli representation of depolarizing noise)
.

For
p\in[0,\frac{3}{4}]
, let
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
have a distribution such that
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e})\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
.
Then
\mathbf{e}
is equidistributed as
(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Z}})+(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}})
,
where
e_{\mathbf{X}},e_{\mathbf{Y}},e_{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
are i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}(q)^{\otimes n}
and
q
is the unique solution to
p=3[q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2}]\leq 3q
such that
q\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
.

It is also useful to observe that given a certain Bernoulli or depolarizing random variable, we can always add another Bernoulli or depolarizing random variable to increase the noise parameter to a desired level.

Lemma 2.16

(Bernoulli convolution)
.

Let
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)
for
p\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Then for any
q\in[p,\frac{1}{2}]
,
\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(q)
, where
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(u)
is an independent random variable and
u=\frac{q-p}{1-2p}
.

Proof.

\Pr[\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=1]=p(1-u)+u(1-p)=p+u-2pu=p+(q-p)=q
.
∎

Lemma 2.17

(Depolarizing convolution)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
be single-qubit phase-free Paulis such that
\mathbf{P}_{1}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}
and, independently,
\mathbf{P}_{2}\sim\mathcal{D}_{u}
where
u=\frac{q-p}{1-\frac{4}{3}p}
for any
p\in[0,\frac{3}{4}]
and
q\in[p,\frac{3}{4}]
.
Then
\mathbf{P}_{1}\mathbf{P}_{2}\sim\mathcal{D}_{q}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{1}\mathbf{P}_{2}
. Then
\Pr[\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{I}]=(1-p)(1-u)+3\frac{p}{3}\frac{u}{3}
since
\mathbf{P}=I
if and only if
\mathbf{P}_{1}=\mathbf{P}_{2}
.
Likewise, for each
\mathbf{P}_{0}\in\set{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
,
\Pr[\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{0}]=(1-p)\frac{u}{3}+\frac{p}{3}(1-u)+2\frac{p}{3}\frac{u}{3}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{P}\sim\mathcal{D}_{(1-p)u+(1-u)p+\frac{2}{3}pu}
.
By simplifying,
(1-p)u+(1-u)p+\frac{2}{3}pu=p+u-\frac{4}{3}pu=p+p+(1-p)=q
as claimed.
∎

2.4
Haar Measure Over States

To generate instances of
\mathsf{LPN}
, we sample a random logical state
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and encode it into a random code before adding noise.
Quantumly, one way to analogously generate a random logical state is to choose a uniformly random
k
-qubit state.
This notion of a uniformly random state is given by the Haar measure
[
Mel24
]
.

Definition 2.18

(Haar measure)
.

The Haar measure
\mu
over the group of
D\times D
unitary matrices
\text{U}(D)
is the unique probability measure which is invariant under translation. That is, for all
\mathbf{V}\in\text{U}(D)
and integrable functions
f
,

\displaystyle\int f(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{U})\,d\mu(\mathbf{U})=\int f(\mathbf{U})\,d\mu(\mathbf{U})=\int f(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V})\,d\mu(\mathbf{U}).

(2.12)

We refer to a
Haar-random state

\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}
to be a state constructed by sampling
\mathbf{U}\sim\mu
a unitary drawn from the Haar measure over
\text{U}(2^{k})
and then letting
\ket{\psi}=\mathbf{U}\ket{0^{k}}
.
It will be useful in this work to study the measurement distribution of a Haar-random state.
We therefore give the following lemma.

Lemma 2.19

(Measurement probabilities are marginally
Beta
-distributed)
.

Let
\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}
and let
K=2^{k}
.
For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, let
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=|\langle\mathbf{x}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
.
Then for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
,
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\sim\text{{Beta}}(1,K-1)
, where the
\text{{Beta}}(a,b)
distribution has density
f(z)\propto z^{a-1}(1-z)^{b-1}
for
z\in[0,1]
and
f(z)=0
otherwise.

Proof.

One way to generate a Haar-random state is via standard complex Gaussians, i.e. for
\ket{\psi}\propto\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\alpha_{\mathbf{x}}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
, let
\alpha_{\mathbf{x}}=Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{x}}+iZ_{\text{Im},\mathbf{x}}
, where all
Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{x}},Z_{\text{Im},\mathbf{x}}
across
\mathbf{x}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians.
After performing this sampling, we normalize to obtain a valid quantum state.
Thus,

\displaystyle p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=|\alpha_{\mathbf{x}}|^{2}=\frac{Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{x}}^{2}+Z_{\text{Im},\mathbf{x}}^{2}}{\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{y}}^{2}+Z_{\text{Im},\mathbf{y}}^{2}}=\frac{W_{\mathbf{x}}}{W_{\mathbf{x}}+\overline{W}_{\mathbf{x}}},

(2.13)

where
W_{\mathbf{x}}=Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{x}}^{2}+Z_{\text{Im},\mathbf{x}}^{2}
and
\overline{W}_{\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}:\mathbf{y}\neq\mathbf{x}}Z_{\text{Re},\mathbf{y}}^{2}+Z_{\text{Im},\mathbf{y}}^{2}
.
Note that
W_{\mathbf{x}}\sim\chi^{2}_{2}
and
\overline{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\sim\chi^{2}_{K-2}
—where
\chi^{2}_{L}
is the chi-squared distribution with
L
degrees of freedom—and the two random variables are independent.
The chi-squared distribution is a special case of the Gamma distribution; in particular,
\chi^{2}_{L}=\text{{Gamma}}(L/2,2)
.
Thus,
W_{\mathbf{x}}\sim\text{{Gamma}}(1,2)
and independently
\overline{W}_{\mathbf{x}}\sim\text{{Gamma}}(K-1,2)
.
By the Beta-Gamma duality (see, e.g.,
[
BH19
]
), if
X\sim\text{{Gamma}}(a,\lambda)
and independently
Y\sim\text{{Gamma}}(b,\lambda)
, then
X/(X+Y)\sim\text{{Beta}}(a,b)
.
Thus,

\displaystyle p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{W_{\mathbf{x}}}{W_{\mathbf{x}}+\overline{W}_{\mathbf{x}}}\sim\text{{Beta}}(1,K-1)

(2.14)

as claimed.
∎

It will also be useful to consider states which are not fully Haar-random, but agree with the distribution of a Haar-random state up to the first
t
moments.
Such objects are known as state
t
-designs.

Definition 2.20

(State
t
-design)
.

An ensemble
\nu
over
k
-qubit states is called a state
t
-design, if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|^{\otimes t}]=\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|^{\otimes t}].

(2.15)

Intuitively, an ensemble
\nu
over states is a
t
-design if it exactly replicates the distribution over
t
copies of a random state sampled according to the Haar measure
\mu_{k}
.
We exclusively work with
exact
state
t
-designs, which exist for all
t

[
RS09
]
. However, our results would also carry over in the case of approximate state
t
-designs with minor modifications, provided the error is sufficiently small.

2.5
Distances between Distributions and States

Given two probability distributions
P,Q
over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}
, the
total variation
(TV) distance between them is

\displaystyle\TV(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}}|P(\mathbf{x})-Q(\mathbf{x})|=\max_{A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}}(P(A)-Q(A)).

(2.16)

The right-hand side expresses an operational interpretation of the TV distance as the best partitioning
A
of the sample space
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}
for the purpose of distinguishing
P
and
Q
.
The TV distance between
P
and
Q
bounds the difference in even probabilities when we draw from
Q
instead of
P
, in the sense that

\displaystyle\Pr_{P}[A]-\TV(P,Q)\leq\Pr_{Q}[A]\leq\Pr_{P}[A]+\TV(P,Q).

(2.17)

Being an
L^{1}
norm, the TV distance is a bona fide metric in the sense that it is positive-definite, symmetric, and satisfies the triangle inequality.
A basic fact about the TV distance is that it is
monotonic
under marginalization.
That is, for a joint distribution
(P,Q)
,

\displaystyle\TV(P_{1},P_{2})\leq\TV((P_{1},Q_{1}),(P_{2},Q_{2})).

(2.18)

Quantumly, we require also a distance measures between states.
Given two pure quantum states
\ket{\psi},\ket{\phi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{m}
, we use the
fidelity
between the states, which is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\phi})=|\langle\psi|\phi\rangle|^{2}.

(2.19)

If instead
\ket{\psi}
is a mixed state
\rho\in(\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})^{\otimes m}
, then
\mathsf{F}(\rho,\ket{\phi})=\langle\phi|\rho|\phi\rangle
.
A related distance measure is the
trace distance

\mathsf{T}(\rho,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\rho-\sigma\rVert_{1}
, where
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{1}
is the sum of the absolute value of the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
.
For our purposes, the only key facts about the trace distance is that the trace distance is negligible if and only if the infidelity
1-\mathsf{F}
is negligible
[
FV02
]
and that the trace distance decreases monotonically with quantum operations, i.e. for any quantum channel
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathsf{T}(\mathcal{E}(\rho),\mathcal{E}(\sigma))\leq\mathsf{T}(\rho,\sigma)
.

3
Quantum Stabilizer Decoding: Problems and Equivalences

Classically the exact definition of decoding is quite robust in the sense that minor modifications to the precise details of the computational problem usually result in an equivalent task.
We will show that this intuition fails quantumly in the sense that certain parts of the definitions are surprisingly sensitive to technical modifications.
For that reason, we will define various flavors of decoding. These include three types: decision or search; the structure of the logical state; and whether the recovery involves finding the logical state, the minimum weight error, or a valid recovery operator.
Furthermore, we define versions in both the worst case and the average case, as they tend to exhibit different phenomena even
qualitatively
.

We remark that there are two general notions of decoding both in the classical and quantum literature. The first is in the context of Shannon theory, known as maximum likelihood decoding, in which we define a probabilistic error model with the goal of recovering the encoded state with high probability after the channel has been applied. The second is in the context of Hamming theory, in which we aim to recover the encoded state after an arbitrary error of weight at most
w
has been applied.
The Hamming picture is only well-defined as a worst-case definition, and is indeed the definition which we adopt in the worst case.
In our average-case definitions, we adopt the Shannon picture using the depolarizing channel.
One can also formulate a worst-case definition in Shannon theory.
Namely, given a corrupted encoded state, determine the error which has the highest probability under the channel to have produced this state.
This worst case definition in the Shannon picture has been studied by
[
IP15
]
, and variants of the definitions range from
NP
-complete to
#P
-complete.

3.1
Worst-Case Problems

In this section, we will enumerate several worst-case decoding problems and draw equivalences between them.
Each of these problems has a classical analogue, and some of them are equivalent in the classical setting as well.
Firstly, there are the nearest codeword and error recovery problems: the classical task they correspond is recovering
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
from
\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
, where
\wt(\mathbf{e})\leq w
, and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k}
.
In the classical setting, there are three distinct ways of solving this problem, that are easily seen to be equivalent:

1.

Find the secret
\mathbf{x}
.

2.

Find a recovery operator
\mathbf{v}
, such that
(\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})-\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{Ax}
.

3.

Find the error
\mathbf{e}
.

Given an oracle for any of these three tasks, the other two can also be solved. The second and third tasks are clearly equivalent, since the only possible value of
\mathbf{v}
is the error
\mathbf{e}
.
Meanwhile, knowing the error allows one to obtain the secret, and vice versa.
We will define these three tasks quantumly—as a direct result of stabilizer degeneracy, they are no longer obviously equivalent.
The most relevant decoding task is (1), since by construction it characterizes the task of decoding.
For this problem, we will also define a decision version of the task; in the sequel we will craft a search to decision reduction.
We then define the remaining two problems, the error recovery versions.
The relationship between all three definitions is important to characterizing the qualitative difference between classical and quantum decoding.

We then define stabilizer decoding.
Classically, stabilizer decoding is the problem of finding a low-weight vector
\mathbf{e}
from its syndrome
\mathbf{Be}
, for
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times n}
.
This problem is dual to the nearest codeword problem: if
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times n}
is the left kernel of
\mathbf{A}
, i.e. its rows form a basis of the left-kernel of
\mathbf{A}
, then the syndrome
\mathbf{B}(\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})=\mathbf{BAx}+\mathbf{Be}=\mathbf{Be}
can be calculated.
If the stabilizer decoding problem can be solved, then
\mathbf{e}
may be recovered, solving the nearest codeword problem.
On the other hand, given
\mathbf{Be}
, any value
\mathbf{y}
so that
\mathbf{By}=\mathbf{Be}
satisfies
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
Hence, if the nearest codeword problem can be solved, recovering
\mathbf{x}
from
\mathbf{y}
is sufficient to find
\mathbf{e}
and decode the syndrome.
We develop the quantum generalization of this duality.

Let
\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}:=\ket{0^{n-k}}\otimes\ket{\psi}
.
Note that all equalities of states and Paulis are implicitly understood to be up to a phase.

3.1.1
Nearest Codeword Problems

Let us now give a formal definition of the quantum analog of the nearest codeword problem.

Definition 3.1

(Quantum Nearest Codeword Problem,
\mathsf{QNCP}
)
.

The quantum nearest codeword problem
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
is characterized by integers
k,n,w\in\mathbb{N}
. Given as input

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})

(3.1)

where
\mathbf{C}
is a Clifford encoding for a stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
of code distance
d
with
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
, where
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a Pauli error, and where
\ket{\psi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
is a logical state, we consider two variants:

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
is the task
of producing a
k
-qubit state within fidelity at least
\frac{2}{3}
of
\ket{\psi}
;

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
is the task of deciding whether there exists a Pauli
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
with
\wt(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})\leq w
with advantage at least
\frac{2}{3}
.

3.1.2
Error Recovery Problems

We now give a formal definition of the two relevant error recovery problems.

Definition 3.2

(Error Recovery Problems:
\mathsf{errQNCP}
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}
)
.

An error recovery problem is characterized by integers
k,n,w\in\mathbb{N}
. Given as input

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n},\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})

(3.2)

where

\mathbf{C}
is a Clifford encoding for a stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
of code distance
d
with
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
, where
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a Pauli error, and where
\ket{\psi}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
is a logical state, we consider two variants:

\bullet

\mathsf{recQNCP}(k,n,w)
is the task
of finding an error Pauli
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, such that
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
belongs to the stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
; and we let

\bullet

\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
is the task
of finding an error Pauli
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, such that
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
belongs to the stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
, and
\wt(E^{\prime})\leq w
.

\mathsf{QNCP}
is analogous to the classical task of recovering
\mathbf{x}
from
\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
.

\mathsf{recQNCP}
corresponds to finding some recovery operator, i.e. some vector
\mathbf{v}
for which
(\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})-\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{Ax}
.
Finally,
\mathsf{errQNCP}
corresponds to finding the error
\mathbf{e}
itself, because it requires one to find not only a recovery operator, but a low-weight one.

3.1.3
Syndrome Decoding Problems

Here, for the purpose of comparison with the above decoding tasks, we define quantum syndrome decoding, using the symplectic representation of stabilizer codes.

Definition 3.3

(Quantum Syndrome Decoding Problem,
\mathsf{QSDP}
)
.

The quantum syndrome decoding problem
\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
is characterized by integers
k,n,w\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given a symplectic stabilizer check matrix
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
(as in Definition
2.10
) which implicitly defines a stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
whose code distance
d
satisfies
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
, we let

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
be the task
of finding, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, an error
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
such that
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
when given as input
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v})
with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{H}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
and
\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))\leq w
; and we let

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
be the task
of determining, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, if there exists an error
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
such that
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
when given as input
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v})
with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{H}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
and
\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))\leq w
.

Remark 3.4
.

The success probability threshold
\frac{2}{3}
was just chosen as a constant larger than
\frac{1}{2}
, as in each case this success probability can be amplified to
1-\negl(n)
.
For the decision problems, this fact is immediate.
Running an oracle with advantage multiple times and taking the majority vote is sufficient to amplify the success probability.
For
\mathsf{errQNCP}
, the success probability amplification is also simple.
Indeed, repeating the algorithm with the same input and choosing the majority outcome will yield the desired output with amplified success probability.
Finally, for
\mathsf{QSDP}
, the success probability can be amplified as well. Each time the oracle is run, some error
\mathbf{e^{\prime}}
is obtained, and for a majority of these errors
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{S}
.
In particular, a majority of errors are in the same stabilizer equivalence class.
Running the oracle repeatedly and choosing the majority equivalence class amplifies the probability of choosing the correct class.

We will later show that for
\mathsf{recQNCP}
and
\mathsf{QNCP}
, the success probability can be amplified as well, because both are equivalent to
\mathsf{QSDP}
.
Because both of these problems have quantum inputs, the possibility of amplification is not immediately obvious.

3.1.4
Inequivalence of Quantum Definitions and the Short Vector Problem

The task
\mathsf{errQNCP}
of finding
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\wt(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})\leq w
and
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
is at least as hard as
\mathsf{recQNCP}(k,n,w)
.
However, the converse need not be true.
Suppose we find
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
.

\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
is a perfectly valid recovery map, and yet since
|\mathcal{S}|=2^{n-k}
there are exponentially many (assuming
k=O(n)
which is virtually always the case for practical considerations) possible recovery operators.
Finding one such operator whose weight is
\leq w
is precisely a short vector problem over a certain subspace of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
.
Unless this short vector problem can be solved efficiently in all cases,
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
does not reduce to
\mathsf{recQNCP}(k,n,w)
.

At the same time,
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
is an unnaturally strong computational task as a model for decoding, since any valid recovery operator will successfully execute the decoding.
This additional constraint of weight is more accurately described as a misgeneralization of classical intuition which unnecessarily injects an additional short vector problem into the task which does not aid the actual recovery operation search in any way.

In summary, we have presented three quantum worst-case decoding problem, and one dual syndrome decoding problem.
The task
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
encodes short vector problems in such a way that significantly impacts the hardness of the task without actually improving the quality of the recovery operator, and we therefore do not consider it a reasonable definition.
The two other definitions—finding a valid recovery operator and finding the logical state itself—are both reasonable but not obviously equivalent.
We next show, however, that in fact the two definitions are equivalent, and are themselves both equivalent to Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
.
Therefore, we will for the remainder of this paper work exclusively with
\mathsf{QNCP}
.

3.1.5
Tight Equivalence Between Search Decoding Definitions

We here study the relation between decoding, in the sense of finding the recovery operator or of purely finding the underlying logical state, and syndrome decoding.
First, we show that in the Decision setting,
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{QSDP}
are equivalent problems in an
instance-by-instance
fashion.
That is, if there is a quantum algorithm to solve one problem for a particular code
S
(with success probability
2/3
), then there is a quantum algorithm with the same runtime up to
\poly(n)
factors, which solves the other problem on the same code
S
.
A useful interpretation of this result is that the most general operation one can perform in decoding a code in the worst case is to first measure the syndrome.
At least in the worst case then, decoding quantum codes is truly a classical computational problem in the sense that no quantum state processing is required to capture the complexity of the task.

Lemma 3.5
.

There exists an instance-by-instance reduction from Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
.

Proof.

Say that
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C})
is an oracle which solves Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
with success probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, taking as input a Clifford circuit
\mathbf{C}
corresponding to a stabilizer code and an error weight
w
.
Define
\mathcal{B}
as follows.
Given
\mathbf{C}
and a noisy state
\ket{\phi}=\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
, first compute a corresponding stabilizer tableau
\mathcal{T}=\set{\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}}
and symplectic check matrix
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
via Theorem
2.11
.
Measure each stabilizer
\mathbf{P}_{i}
on
\ket{\phi}
, getting a syndrome sign
a_{i}\in\set{\pm 1}
, and let
v_{i}=\frac{1}{2}(1-a_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Run
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C})
on
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v})
and output its answer.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the stabilizer subgroup generated by
\mathcal{T}
.
Two possible Pauli errors differ by a stabilizer if and only if they have the same stabilizer syndrome measurements since
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
.
Suppose
\exists\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
and
\wt(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})\leq w
.
Then
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
generates the same syndrome as
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
.
Therefore,
\Symp(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
is a valid solution on the
\mathsf{QSDP}
instance
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v})
.
Conversely, suppose
\exists\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}
and
\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime}))\leq w
.
Then
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
produces the same syndrome as
\mathbf{E}
, and thus satisfies
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{B}
is correct if and only if
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C})
is correct, which by assumption happens with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists an instance-by-instance reduction from Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
.

Proof.

Say that
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{C})
is an oracle which solves Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
with success probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, for a Clifford circuit
\mathbf{C}
and error weight
w
.
Define
\mathcal{A}
as follows.
Given a check matrix
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
and a symplectic syndrome
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
, compute the corresponding Clifford circuit
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
via Theorem
2.11
.
Next, using Gaussian elimination, find any vector
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{v}
. Let
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Prepare the state
\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n}}
and run
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{C})
on
(\mathbf{C},\ket{\phi_{0}})
and output its answer.

Suppose that
\exists\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))\leq w
and
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{v}
.
Then
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{y}
, where
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{y}=0
.
Thus,
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{y})
is either a stabilizer or a logical operator.
As a result,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, where
\mathbf{x}
is defined by the logical operation of
\mathbf{y}
. This state is a structured instance of
\mathsf{QNCP}(n,k,w)
. Conversely, suppose that
\exists\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
such that
\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
. Since
\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{E^{\prime\prime}C}\ket{0^{n}}
,
\ket{\psi}=\ket{\mathbf{x}}
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
. Then
\Symp(\mathbf{E})=\mathbf{e}
induces the same syndrome as
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}
, so the instance
(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v})
is a structured instance for
\mathsf{QSDP}(k,n,w)
.
Thus,
\mathcal{A}
is correct precisely when
\mathcal{B}
is, and so also has success probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
and Decision
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
are instance-by-instance equivalent.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
.
∎

We next show equivalence of the search versions of the problem, using
\mathsf{recQNCP}
instead of
\mathsf{QNCP}
.

Theorem 3.8
.

There exists instance-by-instance reductions from
\mathsf{recQNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
and from
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to
\mathsf{recQNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
.
In particular, they are instance-by-instance equivalent.

Proof.

The proof proceeds similarly to those of Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
.
The reduction procedures are identical.
The only differences are as follows.
Reducing to Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
, we obtain
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathbf{e}=\Symp(\mathbf{E})
for the true error
\mathbf{E}
applied. Thus, we output
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
to obtain the correct
\mathsf{recQNCP}
answer.
Reducing to Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, we start with a trial guess
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
obtained from Gaussian elimination which gives the correct syndrome
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
.
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime})
may be written as
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{E}\,\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}
(for
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
), where
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}
is a logical
\mathbf{X}
Pauli such that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{u}}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{u}}

(3.3)

and
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}
is a logical
Z
Pauli such that
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{h}}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{h}}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{u}}.

(3.4)

By querying the
\mathsf{recQNCP}
solver with
(\mathbf{C},\ket{\phi_{0}})
, we obtain some
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
∎

Now, we will show that Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
and Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
are in fact instance-by-instance equivalent as well. As a consequence of these two statements, we will conclude that Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
and Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
are equivalent.

Theorem 3.9

(Instance-by-instance equivalence of Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
and Search
\mathsf{QSDP}
)
.

There exists (instance-by-instance) reductions from Search
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to Search
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
and from Search
\mathsf{QSDP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
to Search
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
.
In particular, they are instance-by-instance equivalent.

Proof.

Let the noisy state be
\ket{\phi}=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
, where
\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
.
Reducing to
\mathsf{QSDP}
, we will obtain the recovery operator
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
, where
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
is given by the
\mathsf{QSDP}
solver.
With probability
\frac{2}{3}
,
\mathbf{E^{\prime}E}\in\mathcal{S}
and thus by applying
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
to
\ket{\phi}
, we recover
\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
, at which point we apply
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
and discard the last
n-k
qubits to recover
\ket{\psi}
.
With probability at most
\frac{1}{3}
, the output state is arbitrarily far from
\ket{\psi}
, and we denote it
\rho_{\text{junk}}
. Hence the output
\rho
is

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{2}{3}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|+\frac{1}{3}\rho_{\text{junk}}

(3.5)

which satisfies
\langle\psi|\rho|\psi\rangle\geq\frac{2}{3}
.

Reducing to
\mathsf{QNCP}
, we start with a trial guess
\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
obtained from Gaussian elimination which gives the correct symplectic syndrome
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
, i.e.
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{v}
, where
\mathbf{e}=\Symp(E)
represents the true error and
\mathbf{H}
is the parity check matrix of the
\mathsf{QSDP}
instance.
Defining
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime})
, we prepare
\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{E^{\prime\prime}C}\ket{0^{n}}
. As in the proof of Theorem
3.8
,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{E}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}
for
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{0}}=\mathbf{E^{\prime\prime}C}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{EC}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{h}}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{u}}.

(3.6)

We submit
(\mathbf{C},\ket{\phi_{0}})
to the
\mathsf{QNCP}
solver and obtain a state
\rho_{0}
. Similarly, we prepare

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{+}}=\mathbf{E^{\prime\prime}C}\ket{0^{n-k},+^{k}}=\mathbf{EC}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{h}}\ket{0^{n-k},+^{k}}=\mathbf{EC}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{H}^{\otimes k})\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{h}},

(3.7)

where
\mathbf{H}
is the Hadamard gate (distinguished from a parity check matrix by context).
Submitting
(\mathbf{C},\ket{\phi_{+}})
to the
\mathsf{QNCP}
solver give a state
\rho_{+}
.
By assumption,
\langle\mathbf{u}|\rho_{0}|\mathbf{u}\rangle\geq\frac{2}{3}
, so with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
we obtain
\mathbf{u}
when we measure
\rho_{0}
in the computational basis.
Furthermore, we may repeat this step multiple times and output the majority outcome to obtain
\mathbf{u}
with an amplified probability of at least
\frac{5}{6}
.
Likewise we obtain
\mathbf{h}
with probability at least
\frac{5}{6}
when we measure
\rho_{+}
in the
\ket{\pm}
basis.
By a union bound, both are correct with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
From here, we efficiently compute logical Pauli representations
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\prime}
.
We then output
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\Symp(\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\prime})
as the solution to the
\mathsf{QSDP}
problem.
So long as
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{h}
were computed correctly,
\mathbf{E^{\prime\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{h}}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{E}
differ by at most a stabilizer.
This completes the proof of correctness.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

Search
\mathsf{QNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}\!\left(k,n,w\right)
are instance-by-instance equivalent.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Theorems
3.8
and
3.9
.
∎

Henceforth, we shall work exclusively with Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
as the search variant of Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
.
We will not directly work with any syndrome decoding definitions, since they equivalent to a
\mathsf{QNCP}
analog.

Remark 3.11
.

As a consequence of the above equivalence, the success probability of
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}
may also be amplified, just like
\mathsf{QSDP}
.
Indeed, there an algorithm for either of these problems implies one for
\mathsf{QSDP}
, whose success probability may be amplified.
The reductions from
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{recQNCP}
to
\mathsf{QSDP}
in Theorem
3.9
and Theorem
3.8
are easily seen to preserve arbitrary success probability, and therefore the amplified algorithm for
\mathsf{QSDP}
yields one for these two problems as well.

Remark 3.12
.

The instance-by-instance reductions involving
\mathsf{QSDP}
do not hold in the average case, because our reductions rely on being able to carefully choose the right logical state and error to give to the solver.
In the average case, we do not have freedom to choose the logical state and error as we desire.
Since
\mathsf{QSDP}
is not the most natural definition of decoding (as compared to the direct state recovery task), we do not even define an average-case analog.

3.2
Average-Case Problems

The main average-case decoding task of interest to us is termed
Learning Stabilizers with Noise
(
\mathsf{LSN}
) and was first defined by
[
PQS25
]
.
The security parameter of the problem will be
n
, the number of physical qubits.
The choice of
n
as security parameter differs from the convention for
\mathsf{LPN}
. However, it is necessary because we will eventually relate
\mathsf{LSN}
to other problems whose security depends solely on
n
; for example, when
k=1
.
Nevertheless, in the regimes where
n=\poly(k)
, the parameter
k
may be equivalently be taken as the security parameter. Typically, we consider constant rates, where either
k=\Theta(n)
, or vice versa, and thus the overall asymptotics end up being the same.

3.2.1
Learning Stabilizers with Noise

The average-case quantum decoding task directly corresponding to worst-case problem
\mathsf{QNCP}
is
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, to be introduced shortly.
But we will begin by introducing
\mathsf{LSN}
, which is an easier decoding problem where the output is a classical bitstring.
Due to this simplifying fact,
\mathsf{LSN}
has many favorable properties, which will allow us to establish self-reducibility properties and eventually relate it to
\mathsf{LPN}
, obtaining a lower bound on the hardness of
\mathsf{stateLSN}
as well.

Definition 3.13

(Learning Stabilizers with Noise,
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

The
Learning Stabilizers with Noise
problem, denoted by
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
, is characterized by integers
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
and a noise parameter
p\in(0,1)
. Here, both
p
and
k
can vary with
n
. We consider two variants:

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
is the following task: given as input a sample of the form

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\,\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right)

(3.8)

where
\mathbf{C}
is a random
n
-qubit Clifford operator and
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
is an
n
-qubit Pauli error, and
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is a random logical basis state, the task is to find
\mathbf{x}
; and

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
is the following task: given as input a simple which is either

\displaystyle\overbrace{\left(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right)}^{\text{Structured sample}}\quad\text{ or }\quad\overbrace{\Big(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes n}}{2^{n}}\Big)}^{\text{Unstructured sample}}\quad

(3.9)

where
\mathbf{C}
is a random
n
-qubit Clifford,
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
is an
n
-qubit Pauli,
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is a random basis state and
\mathbf{I}_{n}
is the
n
-qubit identity, the task is to distinguish which is the case.

In addition, we also consider the multi-shot, or multi-sample, variant of the problem, denoted by
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, which is characterized by an additional parameter
m\in\mathbb{N}
. In the search variant, the input consists of many independent samples of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.10)

where
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
are independently chosen Pauli errors, for
i\in[m]
, and the task is to recover
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
which appears in each of the samples. In the decision variant, the input either consists of the multi-sample instances above, or
m
copies of the identity matrix
(\mathbf{I}_{n}/2^{n})^{\otimes m}
.

Remark 3.14

(Worst-case vs average-case)
.

In the worst case, the decision variant of stabilizer decoding is deciding whether or not a solution exists at all.
In the average case, the decision variant is instead to distinguish between either a true noisy codeword or a maximally mixed state.
This distinction reflects a broader convention wherein worst-case formulations consist of solution existence, whereas average-case formulations consist of distinguishing two statistically different scenarios.
The latter convention is motivated by consistency with the classical
\mathsf{LPN}
formulation.

Remark 3.15

(Parameters)
.

For purposes of practical error correction, the relevant parameter regime for
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
and
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
with security parameter
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
k<n
,
m=\poly(n)
, and where
p
is a small constant.

We say that a quantum algorithm solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
(respectively,
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
) problem if it runs in time
\poly(n)
and succeeds with probability at least
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
over the random choice of Clifford, Pauli error, basis state
\mathbf{x}
, and its internal randomness.
The extra term
2^{-k}
is motivated by our desire to study the parameter regime where
k
is extremely small, e.g. a constant, at which point random guessing would trivially succeed with probability
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
For
k=\Omega(\log n)
, the presence of the
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
term becomes irrelevant.
In the decision variants of
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
(respectfully,
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
), we require an algorithm that can distinguish with advantage
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, or equivalently we require an algorithm that determines the sample type correctly with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
when the instance type (structured or unstructured) is chosen uniformly at random. Finally, when
m=\poly(n)
, we frequently also use the notation
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}(k,n,p)
to denote the multi-sample variant of the problem.

When the code distributions are uniformly random encodings, we will use the same abbreviations as for the search version.
We also define the polynomial-sample version of the decision problem in the same way.
We note a simple observation that the success probability of a
\mathsf{LSN}
solver for any distribution of the secret
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is uniformly lower bounded by its success probability when
\mathbf{x}
is drawn from the uniform distribution.
Therefore, our choice of letting
\mathbf{x}
be uniformly random in the definition is the hardest variant of the problem in terms of the distribution of the secret.

Lemma 3.16

(Secret re-randomization)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is an algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
with probability
q
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be an arbitrary distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, and define Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p,\mathcal{D})
to be the same task as Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, but with
\mathbf{x}
drawn from
\mathcal{D}
.
Then there is an algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p,\mathcal{D})
to probability
q
, using efficient quantum pre-processing and efficient classical post-processing, with a single call to
\mathcal{A}
in between.
The same statement holds for Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
with advantage
\delta
.

Proof.

Consider first the search variant.
Sample a random
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and for each sample
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})
(where
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{D}
), apply the logical operator
\mathbf{\overline{X}}^{(i)}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}
to the noisy code state.
This produces the states
\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{z}}
, where
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}
is a uniformly random element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
Running
\mathcal{A}
on the transformed samples, we obtain
\mathbf{z}
with probability
q
.
We then output
\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}
, thereby answering the Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p,\mathcal{D})
problem correctly with probability
q
.

In the decision variant, the transformation is the same.
We note that in the unstructured case, the given state is maximally mixed, so applying any unitary operator leaves the state invariant.
Thus, the advantage remains
\delta
in the decision variant as well.
∎

3.2.2
Learning Stabilizers With Noise, State Variant

The problem
\mathsf{LSN}
represents the average-case complexity of decoding a
classical
input after errors.
It is primarily useful when a certain application for
\mathsf{LSN}
is easier to analyze when the output of the task is classical.
The more general problem of stabilizer decoding is more naturally defined when the logical state itself is a completely uniformly random state.
We therefore next define
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, which replaces the logical computational basis state of
\mathsf{LSN}
with a Haar-random state.

Definition 3.17

(Learning Stabilizers with Noise, state variant
\mathsf{stateLSN}
)
.

The
\mathsf{state}
variant of the Learning Stabilizers with Noise problem, denoted by
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, is characterized by integers
k,n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
. Both
p
and
k
can vary with
n
. We consider two variants of the problem:

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
is the following task: given as input samples of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.11)

where
\mathbf{C}_{i}
are random
n
-qubit Clifford operators,
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
are
n
-qubit Paulis, and
\ket{\psi}\sim\mathfrak{\mu}_{k}
is a Haar random
k
-qubit state, the task is to output a quantum state
\rho
within average fidelity at least
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
of
\ket{\psi}
over the choice of
\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}
; that is,

\displaystyle\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[\braket{\psi|\rho|\psi}]\geq\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(3.12)

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
is the following task: given as input a sample which is either

\displaystyle\overbrace{\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}}^{\text{Structured samples}}\quad\text{ or }\quad\overbrace{\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{I}_{n}/2^{n}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}}^{\text{Unstructured samples}}\quad

(3.13)

where
\mathbf{C}_{i}
are random
n
-qubit Cliffords,
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
are
n
-qubit Paulis,
\ket{\psi}\sim\mathfrak{\mu}_{k}
is a Haar random
k
-qubit state, and
\mathbf{I}_{n}
is the
n
-qubit identity, the task is to distinguish which is the case with non-negligible advantage.

Like with
Search

\mathsf{LSN}
, a naive algorithm for
Search

\mathsf{stateLSN}
of outputting a maximally mixed state would succeed with probability exactly
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
.
A solution therefore constitutes a non-negligible improvement over this baseline. Overall, we define the problem parameters and advantage similar as for the regular
\mathsf{LSN}
problem (see Remark
3.15
).

Remark 3.18

(Amplification)
.

The definitions of a solution to
Search

\mathsf{LSN}
and
Search

\mathsf{stateLSN}
both require the weakest solutions, requiring only a chance of success non-negligibly better than random guessing.
A similar definition is conventionally used for
\mathsf{LPN}
.
In the classical setting, however, an algorithm for
\mathsf{LPN}
which succeeds with non-negligible probability can be efficiently amplified to an algorithm which succeeds with high probability.
This originates from the observation that classically, given a purported solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to a sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e})
, one can subtract
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x^{\prime}}
from
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}
and check if it is low-weight.
With high probability,
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}
is low-weight if and only if
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
.
In order to amplify the success probability, we split a
\mathsf{LPN}
sample into different chunks of rows, effectively obtaining multiple samples
\{(\mathbf{A}_{i},\;\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\}_{i\in[m]}
from a single one.
Then, the secrets can each be re-randomized by sampling uniformly random
\mathbf{y}_{i}
and then adding
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}
to each secret, yielding independent samples
\{(\mathbf{A}_{i},\;\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{i})\}_{i\in[m]}
.
Finally, applying the algorithm that solves
\mathsf{LPN}
for each of these independent samples, eventually one of them will be verified correct, and can be used to reconstruct
\mathbf{x}
.
In this way, the probability of success may be amplified.

Remark 3.19

(Increasing noise)
.

A direct consequence of Lemma
2.17
is that for
q>p
,
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
reduces to
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,q)
in both the search and decision variants, since we can always add extra noise to increase the noise parameter.
The same holds for
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}
.

Meanwhile, the success amplification procedure
cannot
be performed for either
\mathsf{LSN}
or
\mathsf{stateLSN}
.
One key issue is that
\mathsf{LSN}
does not admit an analogous recursive structure that enables us to split a single
\mathsf{LSN}
sample into many smaller
\mathsf{LSN}
samples.
More importantly, there does not seem to be an efficient way to verify a purported solution to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Therefore, it is unclear as to whether, quantumly, decoding with high probability is genuinely harder or equivalent to decoding with non-negligible probability.
Since we are interested in proving hardness lower bounds, we take the problem in the easiest form, wherein non-negligible success probability algorithms suffice.

Moreover, we later show that for Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}
, succeeding with non-negligible probability and with high probability are polynomially equivalent, by giving an efficient verification protocol that requires multiple samples.
The argument is fundamentally different from our previous classical one, because although we now have access to multiple samples, there is still no way to verify a correct solution to an
\mathsf{LSN}
problem efficiently.

When there are few samples, the decision and search versions of
\mathsf{stateLSN}
may be related directly to their
\mathsf{LSN}
counterparts.

Lemma 3.20

(Decision
\mathsf{stateLSN}=
Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
be parameters. A quantum algorithm solves
Decision
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k,n,p)
with success probability
q
if and only if it solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
with success probability
q
.

Proof.

It suffices to observe that the distributions of

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})\quad\text{ and }\quad(\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})

(3.14)

for a
\mathbf{C}
random
n
-qubit Clifford operator and
n
-qubit Pauli error
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
are identical when
\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}
is a Haar-random state and
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
. The density matrix of a Haar-random state is precisely the maximally mixed state
\mathbf{I}/2^{k}
on
k
qubits
[
Mel24
]
—just like for the uniform distribution over
k
random bits.
Therefore, the
the two decision tasks are identical.
∎

We next prove that with either one or two samples,
\mathsf{LSN}
reduces directly to
\mathsf{stateLSN}
with the same parameters.
This reduction follows from elementary properties of
t
-designs, which we first capture in a lemma.

Lemma 3.21

(Relaxation to
t
-designs)
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
be parameters. Then, the Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
problem is equivalent (in the sense that an efficient algorithm for one exists if and only if an efficient algorithm exists for the other) to a variant of the problem in which the encoded Haar state
\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}
is instead sampled according to a state
t
-design ensemble
\nu
, whenever
t\geq m+1
.

Proof.

We may interpret the input to
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
as the output of a channel
\mathcal{E}
which maps

\psi=\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}^{\otimes m}
to

\rho_{\psi}=\mathcal{E}(\psi)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}\\
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}\end{subarray}}\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\ket{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\bra{\mathbf{C}_{i}}\otimes(\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i})\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\bra{0^{n-k},\psi}(\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i})^{\dagger}\right].

(3.15)

Consider a state
\sigma
given by
m+1
copies of the logical state.
That is, let

\displaystyle\sigma=\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}\left[(\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi})^{\otimes(m+1)}\right].

(3.16)

An algorithm
\mathcal{D}
solving
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k,n,p)
, can be written as a map taking
\rho_{\psi}
to
\tau_{\psi}=\mathcal{D}(\rho_{\psi})
.
Then, the trace map
f
taking
\tau_{\psi}\otimes\psi
to
\langle\psi|\tau_{\psi}|\psi\rangle
measures the success probability.
In particular,

\displaystyle\Pr_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}\left[\langle\psi|\tau_{\psi}|\psi\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[f(\mathcal{D}(\rho_{\psi})\otimes\psi)]=\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}{\mathbb{E}}[f(\mathcal{D}(\rho_{\psi})\otimes\psi)]

(3.17)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}\left[\langle\psi|\tau_{\psi}|\psi\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right]

(3.18)

by the defining property of a state
t
-design for
t\geq m+1
.
∎

Proposition 3.22

(Search
\mathsf{LSN}
to
\mathsf{stateLSN}
with
\leq 2
samples)
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
be parameters.
There exists an efficient reduction from Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
to Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, for both
m=1
and
m=2
.

Proof.

To construct the reduction, we transform a given sample of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
and apply the solver for
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.
The input for the reduction is given by

\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}.

(3.19)

Sample a random Clifford operator
\mathbf{B}\sim\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
The reduction outputs

\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{B}),\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}.

(3.20)

Note that, for any
i\in[m]
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{B})
is a uniformly random Clifford because
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is a uniformly random Clifford.
Substituting
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{B})
, the distribution can be expressed as

\left\{(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})=(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})\right\}_{i=1}^{m},

(3.21)

where
\ket{\psi}=\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
.
Since the Cliffords form a unitary 3-design
[
Web15
]
,
\ket{\psi}
is now in the transformed state drawn from a 3-design.
The reduction is therefore completed by application of Lemma
3.21
.
∎

It is unclear whether a reduction from Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
exists in general.
That is, it is possible that decoding on average with a typical logical state is harder than decoding on average with a typical computational basis state.
However, we are able to give a reduction from Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k,n,p)
to
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
if the solver for the latter succeeds with probability non-negligibly greater than
\frac{1}{2}
, rather than the previously assumed
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
.

Proposition 3.23

(Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to high-success Search
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

Suppose that there is an efficient algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right)
.
Then there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that solves Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.

Proof.

Consider a sample

(\mathbf{C},\;\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})

(3.22)

of
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
First, measure the syndrome of the state and produce a random Pauli
\mathbf{P}
with the same syndrome.
We can efficiently sample
\mathbf{P}
since the distribution is specified by a uniformly random solution to a linear system of equations.
We may write
\mathbf{P}
in the form
\mathbf{ES}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}
,
\mathbf{S}
is a stabilizer, and
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}
are random logical
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
operators for the code described by
C
.
Now, run the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on

(\mathbf{C},\;\mathbf{PC}\ket{0^{n}})=(\mathbf{C},\;\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}_{1}}),

(3.23)

and on

\left(\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{H}^{\otimes k}),\;\mathbf{P}\left(\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{H}^{\otimes k})\right)\ket{0^{n}}\right)=\left(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}_{2}}\right),

(3.24)

where
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{H}^{\otimes k})
and
\mathbf{H}
is the Hadamard gate.
The equality above holds because conjugating the
\mathbf{Z}
Pauli operator on the last
k
qubits past the Hadamard operator
(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes\mathbf{H}^{\otimes k})
converts it to an
\mathbf{X}
Pauli.
Both of these states are valid samples of
\mathsf{LSN}
, since
\mathbf{y}_{1}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}
are uniformly random,
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
are both uniformly random Cliffords, and
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
.

These two applications of
\mathcal{A}
yield
\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}
as outputs.
By a union bound, the probability that both outputs are correct is

1-2\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(3.25)

In this case,
\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathbf{y}_{1}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbf{y}_{2}
, and both the
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Z}
logical Paulis are known.
Now, computing

\mathbf{C}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}\mathbf{PEC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}=\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}

(3.26)

yields the desired state
\ket{\psi}
.
We have therefore described a procedure
\mathcal{B}
that solves the problem exactly with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
The expected fidelity of the output state with the desired state
\ket{\psi}
is
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, as required for a solution for
\mathsf{stateLSN}
.
∎

The above proposition is not a reduction from
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
, because by our definition of
\mathsf{LSN}
a solution need only solve the problem with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
However, one consequence of this proposition is that it proves a strong connection between the relative hardness of
\mathsf{LSN}
and
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, and the ability to amplify algorithms for
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Specifically, if an efficient algorithm solving
\mathsf{LSN}
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
implies an efficient algorithm with probability
1-\negl(n)
, Proposition
3.23
implies a reduction from
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
We may therefore interpret Proposition
3.23
as a barrier to the amplification for
\mathsf{LSN}
, as this amplification is at least as difficult as reducing
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
By contrast, an algorithm that solves
\mathsf{LPN}
with probability
\geq\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
implies a solution with probability
\geq 1-\negl(n)
.

However, we are able to show that Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
and Search
\mathsf{LSN}
are completely equivalent when there are sufficiently few logical qubits.

Proposition 3.24

(Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
with very few logical qubits)
.

Let
k=O(\log n)
.
Suppose that there exists an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Then there is a quantum algorithm running in time
\poly(n)
that
solves Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

We begin with a sample
(\mathbf{C},\;\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})
of
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
Measure the syndrome of
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
and produce a Pauli
\mathbf{P}
with the same syndrome.
Hence,
\mathbf{PE}
is some logical operator for the code described by
\mathbf{C}
, i.e.
\mathbf{PE}=\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}\overline{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}\mathbf{S}
for some
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
and some stabilizer
\mathbf{S}
.
By applying
\mathbf{C}^{-1}
, we obtain

\ket{0^{n-k},\phi}=\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{PEC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}

(3.27)

By the logical description of
\mathbf{PE}
,
\ket{\phi}=\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}\ket{\psi}
since
\mathbf{S}
does not change
\ket{\psi}
.
If
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}c_{\mathbf{x}}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
, then a measurement of
\ket{\psi}
in the computational basis yields
\mathbf{x}
according to the distribution
\Pr[\mathbf{x}]=|c_{\mathbf{x}}|^{2}
.
The operator
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}\ket{\psi}
does not change this measurement distribution, while
\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}
shifts it by
\mathbf{y}_{1}
.
Hence, the measurement distribution of
\ket{\phi}
is
\Pr[\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}_{1}]=|c_{\mathbf{x}}|^{2}
.

Measuring
\ket{0^{n-k},\phi}
in the standard basis, the state
\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}_{1}}
is obtained with probability
|c_{\mathbf{x}}|^{2}
.
Then, we may again apply
\mathbf{PC}
to yield

\displaystyle\mathbf{PC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}_{1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{PE})\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}_{1}}

(3.28)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}},

(3.29)

where the last equality holds because
\mathbf{PE}
is a product of
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{y}_{2}}
which act trivially, and
\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{y}_{1}}
which shifts the state by
\mathbf{y}_{1}
.
Hence, this procedure produces a sample of
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
, where the input bitstring follows the distribution
\Pr[\mathbf{x}]=|c_{\mathbf{x}}|^{2}
.
We now use Lemma
3.16
to re-randomize the secret and then apply the
\mathsf{LSN}
oracle
\mathcal{O}
, which therefore succeeds with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
If
\mathcal{O}
returns
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, return
\ket{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
as the solution for the
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
instance.

We now show that this procedure solves
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
Let
F(\mathbf{w})
be the probability that the Search
\mathsf{LSN}
solver outputs an answer which differs from the correct answer by
\mathbf{w}
, and let
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})=|c_{\mathbf{z}}|^{2}
.
Then the state
\ket{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
is outputted with probability

\Pr[\mathcal{O}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}]=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})F(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{z}).

(3.30)

Furthermore, the fidelity of the outputted state
\ket{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
with
\ket{\psi}
is
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, so if we let
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, then the expected fidelity is

\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{w})F(\mathbf{w})\right]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{w})\right]F(\mathbf{w}),

(3.31)

where the equality is due to the fact that
F
depends only on the
\mathsf{LSN}
solver and not on
\ket{\psi}
.
For a
k
-qubit Haar-random state
\ket{\psi}
, we claim that
\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}[p(\mathbf{x})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x+w})]=\frac{2}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}
when
\mathbf{w}=0
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x+w})]=\frac{1}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}
otherwise.
The first equality can be derived by Lemma
2.19
, which states that for all
\mathbf{x}
,
p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})
follows a
\text{{Beta}}(1,2^{k}-1)
distribution.
The second moment of a
\text{{Beta}}(a,b)
distribution is known to be
\frac{a(a+1)}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}
.
In our case, therefore,

\displaystyle\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})^{2}]=\frac{1(1+1)}{(1+2^{k}-1+1)(1+2^{k}-1)}=\frac{2}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}.

(3.32)

As for the second equality,
\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x+w})]
is the same for any
\mathbf{w}
by symmetry.
Expanding

\displaystyle 1=\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{w})]

(3.33)

\displaystyle=2^{k}\frac{2}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}+(2^{2k}-2^{k})\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{w}_{0})],

(3.34)

where
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is any fixed vector.
Rearranging gives the second equality.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})p_{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+\mathbf{w})F(\mathbf{w})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\left(2F(\mathbf{0})+\sum_{\mathbf{w}\neq 0}F(\mathbf{w})\right)

(3.35)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}(2^{k}+1)}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\left(F(\mathbf{0})+1\right)

(3.36)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}+1}\left(\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}+1\right)

(3.37)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{(2^{k}+1)\poly(n)}.

(3.38)

In the first line, we used the fact that
F
is a probability distribution.
In the second line, note that by assumption
F(\mathbf{0})=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
since
F(\mathbf{0})
is the success probability of the
\mathsf{LSN}
oracle
\mathcal{O}
.
The remaining lines are direct algebra.
Since
k=O(\log(n))
,
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{(2^{k}+1)\poly(n)}=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, and hence the expected fidelity is sufficiently large to solve
\mathsf{stateLSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
∎

In the case of
\mathsf{LPN}
, there is a simple reduction that shows that increasing the number of logical bits
k
makes
\mathsf{LPN}
harder.
There is likewise a simple proof in the case of
\mathsf{LSN}
, but our proof holds only when
k
is super-logarithmic, due to the extra term
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
in the success probability.
When
k=O(\log n)
we do not rule out the possibility that a highly correlated decoder (e.g. one that tends to either get all logical bits correct or get most of them wrong) to do well on a larger
k
but poorly on a smaller
k
.

Lemma 3.25

(Increasing
k
makes
\mathsf{LSN}
harder)
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
be parameters.
For any
k^{\prime}\geq k
, with
k=\omega(\log n)
, there exists a reduction from Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k^{\prime},n,p)
.

Proof.

Given an input sample of the form

\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.39)

we sample
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k^{\prime}-k}
and define
\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{0}^{k}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{i}
. We then transform our samples into

\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{i}}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}=\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k^{\prime}},\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{x}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.40)

Since global phases may be neglected, this yields a sample for the
\mathsf{LSN}_{k^{\prime},n,p}^{m}
problem. In particular, with non-negligible probability, we can recover
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{x})
, and thus
\mathbf{x}
with non-negligible probability.
Since
k=\omega(\log n)
, this amounts to
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, thereby serving as a solution for
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.
∎

To achieve a proof for
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, however, we must make use of Lemma
3.21
.
The proof applies in the case where the number of samples
m\in\set{1,2}
and when the distribution of codes is uniform.

Lemma 3.26

(Increasing
k
makes
\mathsf{stateLSN}
harder)
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
be parameters.
For
k^{\prime}\geq k
, with
k=\omega(\log n)
, there exists a reduction from Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
to Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}^{m}(k^{\prime},n,p)
, for both
m=1
and
m=2
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.21
, with one or two samples
\mathsf{stateLSN}
is equivalent to the variant where
\ket{\psi}
is drawn from the uniform distribution
\nu
over stabilizer states (i.e. a random Clifford applied to
\ket{0^{k}}
, which is a unitary
3
-design).
We will thus perform the reduction for this variant.
The input to the reduction is of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.41)

where
\ket{\psi}
is a random
k
-qubit stabilizer state.
We will query a
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k^{\prime},n,p)^{m}
oracle on

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbf{B}),\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.42)

where
\mathbf{B}\sim\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime}}
is a random Clifford operator on the last
k^{\prime}
qubits.
Since
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
is uniformly random, we can set
