\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}_{i}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbf{B})
, and instead consider

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}(\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-k^{\prime})}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\dagger}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}\,,

(3.43)

where
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
is uniformly random and where
\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi}
results in
\ket{0^{n-k^{\prime}},\phi}
,
where
\ket{\phi}
is a random
k^{\prime}
-qubit stabilizer state.

Now, applying the solver for
\mathsf{stateLSN}(k^{\prime},n,p)^{m}
, which also solves the stabilizer state variant, we obtain a state
\sigma
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\ket{\psi},\mathbf{B}}\left[\braket{\phi|\sigma|\phi}\right]=\frac{1}{2^{k^{\prime}}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}=\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Equivalently,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ket{\psi}\sim\nu\\
\mathbf{B}\sim\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\left[\braket{0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi|\mathbf{B}\sigma\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}|0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi}\right]=\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(3.44)

Next, prepare the state
\tau=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{B}}[\mathbf{B}\tau\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}]
by applying
\mathbf{B}
to
\sigma
, and output the first
k
qubits of
\tau^{\prime}
.
The resulting state is of the form

\tau^{\prime}=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k^{\prime}-k}}\braket{\mathbf{x}|\tau|\mathbf{x}}.

(3.45)

Using the positivity of diagonal matrix elements of a density matrix,

\displaystyle\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\mu_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[\braket{\psi|\tau^{\prime}|\psi}]

\displaystyle=\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}{\mathbb{E}}[\braket{\psi|\tau^{\prime}|\psi}]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k^{\prime}-k}}\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\braket{\mathbf{x},\psi|\tau|\mathbf{x},\psi}\right]

(3.46)

\displaystyle\geq\underset{\ket{\psi}\sim\nu}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\braket{0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi|\tau|0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ket{\psi}\sim\nu\\
\mathbf{B}\sim\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}[\braket{0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi|\mathbf{B}\sigma\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}|0^{k^{\prime}-k},\psi}]

(3.47)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(3.48)

Since
k=\omega(\log n)
,
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, as desired.
∎

4
Classical Representation of Quantum Stabilizer Decoding

As formulated in Section
3
, the definitions of quantum stabilizer and classical decoding appear qualitatively quite different, rendering a reduction between them challenging to conceive.
More precisely,
\mathsf{LPN}
involves a generator matrix
\mathbf{A}
and a codeword corrupted by additive noise,
\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
, whereas
\mathsf{LSN}
involves a Clifford encoder
\mathbf{C}
and a noisy quantum state
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
.
In this section, we take a step to bridge this conceptual gap by introducing a problem, which we call the
classical representation
of
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Specifically, this problem satisfies two properties.
First, the classical representation is
equivalent
to the definition of
\mathsf{LSN}
from Section
3
in the sense that given an oracle which solves one problem, there is an efficient quantum algorithm which solves the other using a single call to the oracle.
Second, the classical representation has inputs of (roughly speaking) the form
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e})
, very much like that of
\mathsf{LPN}
.
This representation is therefore a key tool in many of our arguments in this work.

We now define this classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
. Note that we use the quantum and classical variant of
\mathsf{LSN}
interchangeably, and the precise variant is always clear in context.

Definition 4.1

(Learning Stabilizers with Noise, classical representation)
.

The classical formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
, is characterized by integers
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
. We consider two variants:

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
: this is the task of finding
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
given as input a sample of the form

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix},\;\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}\Big)

(4.1)

where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times k}
are uniformly random matrices subject to the constraint that the columns of
\mathbf{A}
and also of
\mathbf{B}
, respectively, are pairwise symplectically orthogonal (as in Definition
2.9
) and that
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix}
is full-rank, where
\mathbf{r}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
is random, and where
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
is a random depolarizing Pauli error in symplectic form.
That is,
\mathbf{e}=\Symp(\mathbf{E})
for
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
.

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
: this is the task of distinguishing between samples of the form

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix},\;\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}\Big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{u}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}\Big)

(4.2)

where all variables are as before, and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
is a uniformly random vector.

We refer to
\mathbf{r}
as the
junk
and
\mathbf{y}
as the
secret
.
In the
search variant
, we say that a
\poly(n)
(classical or quantum) algorithm solves the problem if it outputs
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, whereas in the
decision
variant of the problem we require a distinguishing advantage of at least
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.

In addition, we also consider in the classical representation the multi-sample variant of both
Search
and
Decision

\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
, which we denote by
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
; this problem features an additional parameter
m\in\mathbb{N}
which captures the number of independent samples. In the case of
Search

\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, these consist of

\displaystyle\left\{\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i}\Big)\right\}_{i\in[m]}

(4.3)

which are distributed as before, and where
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
is the same in each sample. In the case of
Decision

\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, these are either
m
samples of the previous form, or
m
samples of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\mathbf{u}_{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}\Big)\right\}_{i\in[m]}.

(4.4)

which are also independently distributed as in the single sample variant.
We emphasize that, just like in the standard formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}
, the secret is the same across samples.
However, the junk
is
re-sampled each time.

The classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
much more closely resembles
\mathsf{LPN}
, but we highlight the three major ways in which they differ.
After we establish the equivalence of the classical representation, the remainder of the reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
will be to perform transformations that bridge these differences.

(a)

Global symplectic condition
: The encoding matrix
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\,|\,\mathbf{B}\end{bmatrix}
requires that columns of
\mathbf{A}
are pairwise symplectically orthogonal, as are the columns of
\mathbf{B}
.

(b)

Junk vector
: while the secret in
\mathsf{LPN}
is the entire vector which the encoding matrix acts on, here it is only a small piece of the vector that the encoding piece acts on.
The remaining junk is re-generated across samples.

(c)

Error model
:
\mathbf{e}
is depolarizing instead of Bernoulli, implying that for
i\in[n]
,
e_{i}
and
e_{i+n}
are strongly correlated.

As shown in Fig.
3
,
\mathbf{A}
corresponds to the space of operators which fix the logical computational basis state in
\mathsf{LSN}
—that is, the stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
’s of the code.
Since in
\mathsf{LSN}
there is no distinction between stabilizers and logical
\mathbf{Z}
, we make no distinction of them here either, and instead consider a general space spanned by
n
independent symplectically orthogonal vectors.

\mathbf{B}
corresponds to the space of logical
\mathbf{X}
.
We observe that the presence of the junk is a direct consequence of quantum degeneracy.

Remark 4.2

(
\mathsf{LSN}
vs
\mathsf{stateLSN}
, revisited)
.

While the definition of
\mathsf{LSN}
, i.e. using logical computational basis states, was originally motivated to produce useful applications of the complexity of stabilizer decoding which require classical output, we observe here that the classical representation also crucially relies on the classical nature of the output.
Since the classical representation is a key element in our reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
, it sheds new light on the motivation behind the formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}
, namely that it admits a fully classical form which is more closely aligned with the structure of
\mathsf{LPN}
.

4.1
Equivalence with the Quantum Notion

We now show that the two formulations of
\mathsf{LSN}
are quantumly equivalent.
Note that while the equivalence is quantum, there is no truly quantum phenomena in the reduction in the sense that the only quantum operations are applying certain Clifford operations to a computational basis state.
The only reason the reduction is required to be quantum is because the standard definition of
\mathsf{LSN}
involves a quantum state.

When necessary, we will distinguish between the earlier and later definitions for
\mathsf{LSN}
by referring to the purely classical problems as Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
(classical) and Search
\mathsf{LSN}
(classical).
We say that two tasks are
strongly quantum-equivalent
if given an oracle which solves one task with probability
q
, there exists an efficient quantum algorithm which solves the other with probability at least
q
, using a single call to the oracle.

Theorem 4.3

(Classical representation is equivalent to
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
is strongly quantum-equivalent to Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical), and Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
is strongly quantum-equivalent to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical).

Proof.

We prove this theorem below in four pieces, via Lemmas
4.4
,

4.5
,
4.6
, and
4.7
.
∎

Lemma 4.4

(Reduction to classical representation, search)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical) with success probability
q
. Then there is an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with success probability
q
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

The input for the reduction is

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})\text{ for }i\in[m].

(4.5)

We first show that we can efficiently sample Paulis
\mathbf{P}_{i}
which are uniformly random, subject to the following conditions.

1.

\mathbf{P}_{i}
has the same syndrome as the error
\mathbf{E}_{i}
for any
i\in[m]
.

2.

\mathbf{C}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{C}_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{j}\mathbf{E}_{j}\mathbf{C}_{j}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, for any
i,j\in[m]
.

If we had only the first condition, the sampling efficiency would hold by the same argument given in the proof of Proposition
3.23
.
Namely, sampling a uniformly random Pauli with the same syndrome as
\mathbf{E}
is achieved by randomly sampling a solution to a linear system of equations.
Moreover, each
\mathbf{P}_{i}
differs from
\mathbf{E}
by some product of stabilizers and logical Paulis.
As for the second condition, for any
\mathbf{P}_{i}
such that

\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}_{i}}=\mathbf{C}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\neq\mathbf{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{1}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{1}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}=\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}_{1}},

(4.6)

we can always map
\mathbf{P}_{i}\mapsto\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{1}}\mathbf{P}_{i}
, which does not change the syndrome but removes the logical
\mathbf{X}
difference between
\mathbf{P}_{i}
and
\mathbf{P}_{1}
.
Since the logical
\mathbf{X}
part of
\mathbf{P}_{1}
is still uniformly random, we have sampled a random element in the set of Paulis satisfying the above two conditions.
Now, let
\mathbf{t}_{i}=\Symp(\mathbf{P}_{i})
for each
i
.
Furthermore, define

\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{z}_{1}^{(i)}&\dots&\mathbf{z}_{n}^{(i)}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{B}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{1}^{(i)}&\dots&\mathbf{x}_{k}^{(i)},\end{bmatrix}

(4.7)

where
\mathbf{z}_{j}^{(i)}=\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger})
and
\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(i)}=\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{j}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger})
.
For each
i
, the first
n-k
of the
\mathbf{z}_{j}^{(i)}
are the stabilizers of the code encoded by
\mathbf{C}_{i}
, and the last
k
are the logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators.
Likewise, the
\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(i)}
are the logical
\mathbf{X}
operators of the code
\mathbf{C}_{i}
.

We will show that with

\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{R}_{i},

(4.8)

where
\mathbf{R}_{i}\sim\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is a random invertible
n\times n
matrix,

\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{t}_{i}\right)\text{ for }i\in[m]

(4.9)

is a valid sample of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
under Definition
4.1
.
In particular, we will show that

\mathbf{t}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i},

(4.10)

where
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{r}_{i}
are uniformly random, while
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the symplectic representation of a depolarization error.
Intuitively, the definitions of
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
satisfy the symplectic orthogonality condition required of the classical representation, but presently
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
has an undesirable correlation.
Namely, we know that the
(n-k+j)
th column of
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
is not symplectically orthogonal with the
j
th column of
\mathbf{B}_{i}
, and is symplectically orthogonal with all other columns of
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
.
But
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
are required to be completely random, other then the fact that they each commute among themselves and are linearly disjoint.
This discrepancy arises that we have currently chosen a special basis for
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime})
where the first
n-k
columns are stabilizers and the last
k
columns are logical
\mathbf{Z}
operators.
By randomly choosing a new basis via application of
\mathbf{R}_{i}
, we obtain a truly random basis choice, thereby erasing the correlation structure between
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
and satisfying the distribution requirements of the classical representation.

First,
\mathbf{B}_{i}
has columns that are the symplectic representation of logical
\mathbf{X}
operators, and they are therefore random symplectically orthogonal columns.
Meanwhile,
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime})
is a uniformly random
n
-dimensional symplectically orthogonal subspace that is linearly disjoint from
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{i})
.
The conjugation of
\mathbf{Z}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{Z}_{n}
by a uniformly random Clifford
\mathbf{C}_{i}
produces a uniformly random set of
n
independent commuting Paulis.
This distribution in symplectic form is supported on subspaces
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime})
, which are each linearly disjoint from
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{i})
.
Since
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is a random matrix with the same image as
\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\prime}
, and the image is uniformly random, it is indeed a random matrix with symplectically orthogonal columns for which
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}
is full-rank.
The distributions of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
are therefore correct.

Now, the Pauli operator
\mathbf{P}_{i}
has the same syndrome as
\mathbf{E}_{i}
, implying that
\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}
is the product of a uniformly random stabilizer, a uniformly random
\mathbf{Z}
-logical operator, and a uniformly random
\mathbf{X}
-logical operator.
By ensuring that

\mathbf{C}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{C}_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{j}\mathbf{E}_{j}\mathbf{C}_{j}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}

(4.11)

for all
i,j\in[m]
, the logical
\mathbf{X}
operators must be the same, and therefore have symplectic representations
\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}
for a uniformly random bitstring
\mathbf{y}
.
Meanwhile, the remaining stabilizer and logical
\mathbf{Z}
operator yield the term
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}
, while
\Symp(\mathbf{E}_{i})=\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Thus, everything follows exactly the desired distribution, so Eqn. (
4.10
) is a valid sample for the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
.

We now call
\mathcal{O}
, which returns
\mathbf{y}
with probability
q
.
Observe that
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}=\ket{0^{n-k},(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y})}
for any
i
because
\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}
implements a logical
\mathbf{X}
operator which exactly corresponds to adding the
\mathbf{y}
obtained from the classical representation.
We recover
\mathbf{x}
from
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}
to complete the reduction.
∎

Lemma 4.5

(Reduction to classical representation, decision)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical) with success probability
q
. Then there is an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with success probability
q
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

The reduction takes as input the samples

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\rho_{i})\text{ for }i\in[m].

(4.12)

In this case,
\rho_{i}
is either the structured sample
\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\bra{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbf{E}_{i}^{\dagger}
mixed over random
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
, or it is the maximally mixed state.
We perform the same transformation as in Lemma
4.4
, giving classical samples of the form

\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{t}_{i}\right)\text{ for }i\in[m],

(4.13)

where

\mathbf{t}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i}.

(4.14)

By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, a structured sample of Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
indeed produces a structured sample as in Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical).
On the other hand, say that this same reduction was performed on input samples for which
\rho_{i}
are maximally mixed states.
The state
\rho_{i}
may then be equivalently considered a random state
\mathbf{R}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n}}
, where
\mathbf{R}_{i}
is a random Pauli operator, since this produces a maximally mixed state.
Then the Paulis
\mathbf{P}_{i}
would be chosen so that they have the same syndrome as
\mathbf{R}_{i}
, and random so that

\mathbf{C}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{R}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n}}=\mathbf{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{1}\ket{0^{n}}.

(4.15)

The resulting
\mathbf{P}_{i}
are independent and uniformly random, since we start by sampling a uniformly random Pauli with a uniformly random syndrome (that of
\mathbf{R}_{i}
), and then apply a choice of logical operator to enforce the above condition.
However, multiplying a uniformly random Pauli by another Pauli still gives a uniformly random Pauli.
Hence, in symplectic representation,
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is a uniformly random vector.
When we add it to
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}
, we still get a uniformly random vector.
Then
\mathbf{t}_{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
and
\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i}
are distributed correctly by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
Therefore, in both the unstructured and structured cases, the distributions are mapped correctly, so we can at this point call the decision oracle
\mathcal{O}
for the classical representation and output its answer to complete the reduction.
∎

We next show the reductions in the reverse direction, i.e. from the classical representation to the standard formulation of
\mathsf{LSN}
.

Lemma 4.6

(Reduction from classical representation, search)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with success probability
q
. Then there is an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical) with success probability
q
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

The reduction takes as input the samples

\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{t}_{i}\right)\text{ for }i\in[m],

(4.16)

for
\mathbf{t}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
We need to generate samples of the form
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
, and
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
.
The key step to doing so is the efficient sampling of a uniformly random Clifford
\mathbf{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{span}(\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}),\;\dots,\;\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{Z}_{n}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger}))=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i})

(4.17)

and
\Symp(\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{j}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\dagger})=(\mathbf{B}_{i})_{j}
, where
(\mathbf{B}_{i})_{j}
is the
j
th column of
\mathbf{B}_{i}
, for
j\in[k]
.
We do so by way of observing that up to phases which we do not consider, a Clifford is determined by its action on
\mathbf{Z}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{Z}_{n},\;\mathbf{X}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{X}_{n}
.
Presently,
\mathbf{A}_{i}
encodes how
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is to act on
\mathbf{Z}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{Z}_{n}
, while
\mathbf{B}_{i}
encodes how
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is to act on
\mathbf{X}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{X}_{k}
.
There are, however, two problems.
First,
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
\mathbf{B}_{i}
are completely independent whereas we would like an explicit basis correspondence where the
j
th logical
\mathbf{Z}
anticommutes exactly with the
j
th logical
\mathbf{X}
.
Second, the Clifford is underdetermined because we do not know how
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is to act on
\mathbf{X}_{k+1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{X}_{n}
; these missing draws are known as
destabilizers
.
We address these problems by way of an iterative sampling procedure.
Specifically, first draw randomly
n-k
independent basis vectors from
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
which are symplectically orthogonal to the columns fo
\mathbf{B}_{i}
; these are the stabilizers.
Draw the remaining
k
independent basis vectors from
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}_{i})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
such that the
j
th draw, in addition to being independent and symplectically orthogonal to the stabilizers and previous draws, is symplectically orthogonal to all but exactly the
j
th column of
\mathbf{B}_{i}
; this
j
th draw is a logical
\mathbf{Z}_{j}
.
The logical
\mathbf{X}
’s are given exactly by the columns of
\mathbf{B}_{i}
.
Finally, we draw a random completion of the
\mathbf{X}
part of the Paulis, i.e. the destabilizers.
To sample the
j
th destabilizers, we sample a random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
which is symplectically orthogonal to the logical
\mathbf{Z}
’s, logical
\mathbf{X}
’s, previously drawn destabilizers, and all but exactly the
j
th stabilizer.
All of these samplings are random solutions to an explicit set of linear equations, so they can be done efficiently.
Now that we have specified the action of the Clifford
\mathbf{C}_{i}
on an explicit complete basis set of Paulis, we can efficiently compute an exact description of
\mathbf{C}_{i}
, e.g. as a circuit
[
RCPK18
]
.
We have generated a uniformly random Clifford
\mathbf{C}_{i}
which upon acting on
\mathbf{Z}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{Z}_{n}
produces in symplectic form
\mathbf{A}_{i}
, and upon acting on
\mathbf{X}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{X}_{k}
produces in symplectic form
\mathbf{B}_{i}
.
To complete the argument that
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is uniformly random, we must show that the number of such
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is the same for each choice of
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i})
.
This fact follows directly from the fact that the Cliffords act transitively on Paulis (i.e. for any pair of Paulis there is a Clifford which maps one to the other by conjugation), so the set of valid Cliffords which produce each
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i})
are the same.
Since
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B}_{i})
is itself chosen uniformly at random, this argument completes the proof that
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is a uniformly random Clifford.

Next, let
\mathbf{P}_{i}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{t}_{i})
be the Pauli whose symplectic representation is
\mathbf{t}_{i}
.
We prepare the states

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{P}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n}})\text{ for }i\in[m].

(4.18)

By definition,
\mathbf{P}_{i}
is the product of three operators, whose symplectic parts are
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}
,
\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}
, and
\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
The
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}
component does nothing to the logical state.
The
\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}
component maps the logical state from
\ket{0^{k}}
to
\ket{\mathbf{y}}
.
Hence, the samples are exactly equal to

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}})\text{ for }i\in[m],

(4.19)

where
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
, and
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
,
as required for Definition
3.13
. We can then call the oracle for
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}(k,n,p)
, which will return
\mathbf{y}
with probability
q
, to complete the reduction.
∎

Lemma 4.7

(Reduction from classical representation, decision)
.

Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with success probability
q
. Then there is an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical) with success probability
q
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

The reduction takes as input the samples

\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\;\mathbf{t}_{i}\right)\text{ for }i\in[m],

(4.20)

where either

\mathbf{t}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{i}\\
\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}+\mathbf{e}_{i},

(4.21)

or
\mathbf{t}_{i}
is uniformly random.
In the former case, the procedure in the proof of Lemma
4.6
produces a valid structured sample
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{y}})
.
If this same procedure is applied to a uniformly random
\mathbf{t}_{i}
, then a uniformly random Pauli
\mathbf{P}_{i}
is applied to each state
\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n}}
, creating maximally mixed states.
Hence, the resulting samples are
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\rho_{i})
, for
\rho_{i}
maximally mixed.
It follows that unstructured samples of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
(classical) get mapped to unstructured samples of
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

5
Self-Reducibility of Quantum Stabilizer Decoding

Generally, the classical landscape of hardness results admit two non-trivial forms of self-reductions.
The first is a search-to-decision reduction, in which we show that an oracle solving the decision variant of a task can be called as the subroutine of an otherwise efficient algorithm to solve the search variant.
The second is a random self-reduction, wherein we show that an oracle solving a random instance of a task can be used to solve any instance of that task.
By definition, the worst case is always at least as hard as the average case, so the converse notion of an average-to-worst case reduction is vacuous.
Typically, the same can be said about a decision-to-search reduction.
Such a reduction is almost always trivial for classical problems because we can usually efficiently certify the validity of a proposed solution, and thus only accept correct answers.
However, in the quantum formulation it is unclear whether a search oracle is even useful in solving a decision problem, as we will show, because if is no longer easy to verify purported solutions.
Whether or not any of these three non-trivial reductions exist is an open problem for stabilizer decoding.
In this section, we resolve some of these open questions by either giving a reduction or providing evidence that no such reduction exists.

5.1
Worst-Case Reductions Between Decision and Search

We give reductions between search and decision.
In the worst case, we must assume a decision oracle which is capable not only of solving
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
for a specific
w
, but also of all
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
It is an open question as to whether this assumption can be relaxed.

Theorem 5.1

(Search-to-decision reduction,
\mathsf{QNCP}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=\set{\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}}
be a stabilizer tableau with code distance
d
such that
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
.
Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
be the associated Clifford circuit and let
\mathcal{S}
be the stabilizer subgroup generated by
\mathcal{T}
.
Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{T})
which solves Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w^{\prime})
for all
w^{\prime}\leq w
with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
Then there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{T})
with input
(\mathbf{C},\;\ket{\phi}=\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\psi})
such that
\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
, finds
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
with probability
\frac{2}{3}
.

Proof.

First, we apply the equivalence between
\mathsf{QNCP}
and
\mathsf{QSDP}
from Theorem
3.7
.
It is therefore enough to find a search-to-decision reduction for syndrome decoding.
In a slight abuse of notation, we assume instead that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
solve the respective syndrome decoding versions.
Here,
\mathcal{A}
outputs
YES
if there is a solution, and
NO
otherwise.
Let
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-k)\times 2n}
be the symplectic check matrix for
\mathcal{T}
, with rows
\mathbf{h}_{1}^{X},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{h}_{n}^{X},\;\mathbf{h}_{1}^{Z},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{h}_{n}^{Z}
.
Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-k}
be the measured symplectic syndrome and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be
\Symp(\mathbf{E})
.
Consider the following algorithm
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{v},w)
, where
CONTINUE
means to skip the rest of the loop iteration and immediately begin the next one.

1.

Using binary search and
\mathcal{A}
, find
w_{*}=\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))
.

2.

Initialize vectors
\mathbf{\hat{e}}^{X}\leftarrow 0^{n}
,
\mathbf{\hat{e}}^{Z}\leftarrow 0^{n}
,
\mathbf{\hat{v}}\leftarrow\mathbf{v}
, and an integer
\hat{w}\leftarrow w_{*}
.

3.

For
i
from 1 to
n
do:

(a)

If
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{H},\;\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{Z},\;\hat{w}-1)=\text{{YES}}
, set
\mathbf{\hat{v}}\leftarrow\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{Z}
,
\hat{w}\leftarrow\hat{w}-1
, and
\hat{e}_{i}^{X}\leftarrow 1
, then
CONTINUE
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{H},\;\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{X},\;\hat{w}-1)=\text{{YES}}
, set
\mathbf{\hat{v}}\leftarrow\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{X}
,
\hat{w}\leftarrow\hat{w}-1
, and
\hat{e}_{i}^{Z}\leftarrow 1
, then
CONTINUE
.

(c)

If
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{H},\;\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{X}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{Z},\;\hat{w}-1)=\text{{YES}}
, set
\mathbf{\hat{v}}\leftarrow\mathbf{\hat{v}}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{X}+\mathbf{h}_{i}^{Z}
,
\hat{w}\leftarrow\hat{w}-1
,
\hat{e}_{i}^{X}\leftarrow 1
, and
\hat{e}_{i}^{Z}\leftarrow 1
.

4.

Let
\mathbf{\hat{e}}=(\mathbf{\hat{e}}^{X},\mathbf{\hat{e}}^{Z})
. Output
\mathbf{\hat{e}}
.

All of the outputs of
\mathcal{A}
are correct with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, so by amplification we can make its error probability at most
1/9n^{2}
.
Then, by union bound,
\mathcal{A}
is correct all
3n\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil
times it is called with probability at least
2/3
; we henceforth assume perfect correctness without loss of generality.
The Search variant guarantees that there is a vector
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\wt(\mathbf{e})\leq w
and
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{v}
.
Step 1 uses the Decision algorithm
\mathcal{A}
’s perfect answers to find
w_{*}=\wt(\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))
.
This is the value for which
\mathcal{A}
outputs
YES
but for
w_{*}+1

\mathcal{A}
outputs
NO
.

Now, suppose that the error
\mathbf{E}=\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e})
, with
\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
and
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{v}
, is supported on the first qubit.
Then
\mathbf{E}
must have either
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}
, or
\mathbf{Z}
on the first qubit, which means that applying one of them must yield a solution with weight
\leq w_{*}-1
.
Hence, one of the three sub-calls in Step 3 is guaranteed to occur.
Suppose instead that
\mathbf{E}
is not supported on the first qubit.
If any one of the sub-calls in Step 3 occurs, then this would imply that increasing the weight of
\mathbf{E}
gave a valid solution with weight
\hat{w}-1=w_{*}-1
. But
w_{*}\leq w
, and since
w\leq\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
there cannot be another solution with weight
\leq w
giving the same syndrome.
Thus, we have a contradiction.
Consequently, Step 3 in the first iteration has some oracle sub-call if and only if there exists a solution supported on the first qubit, and records the action on the first qubit in symplectic notation.
Applying this logic iteratively proves correctness.
∎

A closer inspection of the above proof reveals that in fact the outputted error
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}
satisfies
\wt(\widetilde{\mathbf{E}})\leq w
in addition to
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{S}
.
This is the extra requirement which we imposed in the formulation of
\mathsf{errQNCP}
in Definition
3.2
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{errQNCP}
reduces to Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
.
At the same time, there is a simple decision-to-search reduction for
\mathsf{errQNCP}
, because the validity of a claimed
\mathsf{errQNCP}
solution can be easily checked.

Claim 5.2

(Decision-to-search reduction,
\mathsf{errQNCP}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T},\mathcal{S},\mathbf{C},w,d
be as in Theorem
5.1
.
Suppose there is an algorithm
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{T})
which solves Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
on the code with Clifford
\mathbf{C}
, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
Then there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which solves Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
, i.e. determines whether the instance of Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
is a
YES
(there is a solution) instance or
NO
(no solution) instance, with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{A}
converts to syndrome decoding by measuring the syndrome and using the symplectic representation to obtain check matrix
\mathbf{H}
and symplectic syndrome
\mathbf{v}
.
Then,
\mathcal{A}
runs Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}(k,n,w)
on the given instance, which will return some
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}
.
We compute
\mathbf{\widetilde{e}}=\Symp(\widetilde{\mathbf{E}})
and check whether
\mathbf{H\Omega}\mathbf{\widetilde{e}}=\mathbf{v}
and
\wt(\widetilde{\mathbf{E}})\leq w
. If so, output
YES
; otherwise output
NO
.
If the instance is
YES
, then
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}
will have the correct syndrome and be low-weight with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
, so
\mathcal{A}
will output
YES
with at least this probability.
If the instance is
NO
, then any
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}
which satisfies the syndrome must satisfy
\wt(\widetilde{\mathbf{E}})>w
, in which case
\mathcal{A}
will certainly say
NO
.
Thus, in either case
\mathcal{A}
succeeds with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.
∎

This reduction would not go through for Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
or Search
\mathsf{recQNCP}
, because there does not seem to be an efficient technique to verify the validity of solutions proposed by the search oracle.
It is an open question as to the existence or non-existence of a decision-to-search reduction using Search
\mathsf{QNCP}
or
\mathsf{recQNCP}
.

While tempting to conclude, it is not true that the above results imply an equivalence between Decision
\mathsf{QNCP}
and Search
\mathsf{errQNCP}
.
This is because the search-to-decision reduction requires a decision oracle which is capable of distinguishing whether the error is of weight above or below
w^{\prime}
for all
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
Such an oracle is exceptionally strong and is equivalent to solving
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w^{\prime})
for all
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
The search-to-decision reduction can be interpreted as revealing just how strong this oracle is, as it enables solving the short vector problem encoded within
\mathsf{errQNCP}
.

As we next explore, the relation between search and decision exhibits slightly different inherently quantum phenomena in the average case.
More precisely, the promise that instances are drawn from the uniform distribution makes it possible to construct reductions with a completely different caveat—using multiple samples—that equate average-case search and decision variants.

5.2
Average-Case Search-Decision Equivalence

In this section, we demonstrate a search-decision equivalence for
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}
.
This reduction relies crucially on the ability to obtain multiple samples; it is an open question as to whether the equivalence holds with a single sample.

For context, we recall the trivial decision-to-search procedure in classical decoding.
For
\mathsf{LPN}
, one may consider a sample of
\mathsf{LPN}
,
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
, and submit it to Search
\mathsf{LPN}
.
If
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}
, then with non-negligible probability, the search algorithm will return
\mathbf{\widetilde{x}}
such that
\mathbf{\widetilde{x}}=\mathbf{x}
.
The decision algorithm, after verifying
\mathbf{x}
by checking that
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{A\widetilde{x}}
is low-weight, can accept this sample.
However, if
\mathbf{y}
is uniformly random, a solution
\mathbf{x}
exists with negligible probability, so the decision algorithm will accept with negligible probability as well.

For
\mathsf{LSN}
, there is no longer an efficient algorithm to verify that a potential solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}
is indeed the solution.
A verification protocol would have to observe that
\mathbf{C}\ket{\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}}
and
\mathbf{EC}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
differ by a low-weight error.
However,
\mathbf{E}
is not the only operator that could have taken
\mathbf{C}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
to
\mathbf{EC}\ket{\mathbf{x}}
.
Indeed, there are exponentially many such operators, and determining whether one of them is low-weight is precisely a short vector problem.
Quantum degeneracy is therefore once again responsible for this qualitative difference between the classical and quantum settings.
Nevertheless, we show that given more samples of the decision problem, there is still a reduction to the search problem.

Theorem 5.3

(Decision-to-search reduction,
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an oracle which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability at least
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Then there exists an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{2m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with non-negligible advantage, using two calls to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

We begin with samples
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\rho_{i})
for
i\in[2m]
, where
\rho_{i}
are either independent maximally mixed states, or are code states
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{i},\mathbf{x}}\left[(\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i})\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}\bra{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i})^{\dagger}\right]
, where
\mathbf{E}_{i}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
are independent depolarization errors,
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{i}
are independent random Clifford operators.
The second option for
\rho_{i}
may equivalently be considered as the state
\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, where
\mathbf{E}_{i},\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{x}
are all stochastically chosen from the same distributions.

Split up the
2m
samples into two halves. The first half of samples,
(\mathbf{C}_{i},\rho_{i})
for
i\in[m]
, we run the search oracle on our samples as-is, and receive a proposed solution
\mathbf{z}_{1}
.
For the second half of the samples, randomly choose a bitstring
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, and apply
\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{(i)}
to each, i.e. apply the logical operation for
\mathbf{X}_{\mathbf{y}}
on the
i
th code.
If the
\rho_{i}
are maximally mixed, applying
\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{(i)}
does nothing.
If they are code states, then the states transform to

\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}^{(i)}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}.

(5.1)

We then run the search oracle on these transformed samples, obtaining a new output
\mathbf{z}_{2}
.
We accept if
\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z_{2}}=\mathbf{y}
, and reject otherwise.

Suppose first that all
2m
of our states were genuine noisy code states.
Then,
\mathbf{z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{z}_{2}
are independent, since they are the outcomes of the search oracle on completely independent inputs—the circuits and errors were independently sampled, and
\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}
for uniformly random
\mathbf{y}
is independent from
\mathbf{x}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}_{2}=\mathbf{y}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{1}=\mathbf{v},\;\mathbf{z}_{2}=\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{y}]

(5.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{1}=\mathbf{v}]\cdot\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{2}=\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{y}].

(5.3)

Let
F(\mathbf{w})
be the probability that the Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
solver outputs an answer which differs from the correct answer by
\mathbf{w}
.
By Lemma
3.16
,
F
has no dependence on the input itself.
Then

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}_{2}=\mathbf{y}]=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}F(\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{x})\cdot F(\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}F(\mathbf{v})^{2}.

(5.4)

We may write
F(\mathbf{v})=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\delta(\mathbf{v})
.
By normalization,
\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\delta(\mathbf{v})=0
; by assumption,
\delta(0)=\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}F(\mathbf{v})^{2}=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\frac{1}{2^{2k}}+\frac{1}{2^{k}}\delta(\mathbf{v})+\delta(\mathbf{v})^{2}=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}}\delta(\mathbf{v})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(5.5)

Suppose instead that all
2m
of the states were independent maximally mixed states.
Then the application of
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}
has no effect, as the state remains maximally mixed.
Therefore, the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}_{2}
is independent from
\mathbf{y}
.
But
\mathbf{y}
is uniformly random, so the probability that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}_{2}
is exactly
1/2^{k}
.
There is therefore a negligible chance that the distinguisher will accept in this case. The two cases differ by a non-negligible term. Therefore, the distinguisher constructed has non-negligible advantage, which completes the reduction.
∎

This proof takes independent samples and shifts half of them by a random string
\mathbf{y}
known only to the distinguisher.
If the samples were not structured, then the shift by
\mathbf{y}
is information-theoretically impossible to recover from the samples that were submitted to the search oracle.
Meanwhile, if they were structured, then a search oracle is capable of returning the two samples correctly, and the shift between them yields
\mathbf{y}
.
The above argument also works for
\mathsf{LPN}
, but unlike the standard technique with verification, it generalizes to the quantum setting as well.

For the search to decision reduction, our argument is inspired by the analogous search to decision reduction for
\mathsf{LPN}
.
It will require a lemma that shows that low-weight errors are capable of scrambling one qubit.

Lemma 5.4

(Low-weight errors scramble at least one qubit completely)
.

Let
\rho=\ket{y}\bra{y}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}}{2^{n-1}}
be an
n
qubit state for some
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, let
\mathbf{C}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
be a random Clifford, and let
\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
be an error.
Then for any
p=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
,
\ket{\mathbf{C}}\bra{\mathbf{C}}\otimes(\mathbf{EC})\rho(\mathbf{EC})^{\dagger}
is negligibly close in trace distance to
\ket{\mathbf{C}}\bra{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes n}}{2^{n}}
.

Proof.

The state
(\mathbf{EC})\rho(\mathbf{EC})^{\dagger}
may be rewritten as
(\mathbf{CE}^{\prime})\rho(\mathbf{CE}^{\prime})^{\dagger}
, where
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}\mathbf{EC}
.
The action of
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
on the state depends on the Pauli it applies to the first qubit.
If it is an
\mathbf{X}
or
\mathbf{XZ}
Pauli, then it flips the bit
y
, while if it is
\mathbf{I}
or
\mathbf{Z}
it does nothing.

\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
may always be expressed as
\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{P}
or
\mathbf{P}
, where
\mathbf{P}
is a Pauli on the last
n-1
qubits and potentially a
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
.

\mathbf{P}
fixes
\rho
, since the maximally mixed state will not be altered by the Pauli on the last
n-1
qubits, and
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
fixes
\ket{y}\bra{y}
.
If we can show that, knowing
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
can be expressed as
\mathbf{P}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
and as
\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{P}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon
, then

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{E}\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\rho(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})^{\dagger}\,|\,\mathbf{C}]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon\right)\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}}{2^{n-1}}\otimes|y\rangle\langle y|+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon\right)\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}}{2^{n-1}}\otimes|1-y\rangle\langle 1-y|

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes n}}{2^{n}}+\epsilon(\ket{y}\bra{y}-\ket{1-y}\bra{1-y})\otimes\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\otimes(n-1)}}{2^{n-1}}.

(5.7)

We here allow
\epsilon
to be negative, and instead aim to show that
|\epsilon|=\negl(n)
for all but a negligible fraction of
\mathbf{C}
.
If so, then the trace distance of
|\mathbf{C}\rangle\langle\mathbf{C}|\otimes\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\rho(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})^{\dagger}
with the maximally mixed state is also negligible.
Applying the unitary
\mathbf{C}
and the fact that the trace distance is preserved by unitary transformations, we would obtain the same statement for
(\mathbf{CE}^{\prime})\rho(\mathbf{CE}^{\prime})^{\dagger}
, completing the proof.

To bound
|\epsilon|
, we apply the Bernoulli representation of depolarizing noise as from Corollary
2.15
in order to decompose the error into a sum of independent Bernoulli errors.
In the symplectic representation,
\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}
is a
2n\times 2n
matrix
\mathbf{S}
that preserves the symplectic inner product
[
RCPK18
]
, such that
\mathbf{S}
maps
\mathbf{e}=\Symp(\mathbf{E})
to
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\Symp(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
.
The bit
e^{\prime}_{1}
determines whether
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}
applies a
\mathbf{X}
Pauli to the first qubit, and is the sole variable determining
\epsilon
.
Furthermore,
e^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\mathbf{e}
, where
\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}
is the first row of
\mathbf{S}
.
For a random
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}
follows the uniform distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
because the marginal distribution of the first row of a random matrix that preserves the symplectic inner product is uniformly random.

By Corollary
2.15
, we may write
\mathbf{e}=(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Z}})+(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}})
, where
e_{\mathbf{X}},e_{\mathbf{Y}},e_{\mathbf{Z}}
are i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}(q)
and
p=3[q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2}]
.
Thus,
e_{1}^{\prime}=\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{Z}}+\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Y}})
, where
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{X}}=(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{X}},0)
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{Z}}=(0,\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{Z}})
.
Each of these three terms are independent.
Consider the distribution of the first term,
\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{X}}
.
Let
m
be the weight of the first
n
entries of
\mathbf{r}
and assume that
m\geq\frac{n}{3}
.
Then
\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathsf{Bin}(m,q)
.
Using the fact that the probability that a Binomial random variable is even is
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1-2q)^{m}
and the exponential bound
1+x\leq e^{x}
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\widetilde{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathbf{X}}=0]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1-2q)^{m}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1-2q)^{n/3}

(5.8)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\text{exp}\left(-\frac{2}{3}qn\right).

(5.9)

So long as
q=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
, the above probability is
\frac{1}{2}+\eta
where
\eta=\negl(n)
.
Note that
\frac{3}{2}q\leq 3[q^{2}(1-q)+q(1-q)^{2}]\leq 3q
for all
q\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, so
q=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
if and only if
p=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
.

Now, given
b\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2}+\delta)
, for any independent random variable
b^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\prime})
, we can check by direct computation that
b+b^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p^{\prime\prime})
where
p^{\prime\prime}\in[\frac{1}{2}-\delta,\;\frac{1}{2}+\delta]
.
Thus, if
m\geq\frac{n}{3}
,
e_{1}^{\prime}=\mathbf{r}^{\intercal}\mathbf{e}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\epsilon)
where
|\epsilon|\leq\frac{1}{2}\text{exp}\left(\frac{-2qn}{3}\right)=\negl(n)
.
Therefore, we have nearly accomplished our goal set out in Eqn. (
5.6
) for any
\mathbf{C}
such that
m\geq 3
.
Since
\mathbf{r}
is uniformly random over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
, the Chernoff bound implies that
m\geq\frac{n}{3}
with probability
1-\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)
, which fully accomplishes our goal.
∎

Theorem 5.5

(Search-to-decision reduction,
\mathsf{LSN}
)
.

Let
p=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
.
Suppose that there exists an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with non-negligible advantage.
Then there exists an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\Omega(1)
, using
\poly(n)
calls to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

The input for the reduction is polynomially many samples

(\mathbf{C}_{i},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}).

(5.10)

We will construct a procedure to learn the
j
th bit of
\mathbf{x}
with non-negligible probability.
Repeating it polynomially many times with independent samples amplifies that probability to
1-o(\frac{1}{k})
.
Then, applying this procedure to each bit of
\mathbf{x}
, with independent samples of the search problem, we will obtain
\mathbf{x}
with a probability of at least a constant.

Without loss of generality, we will describe the procedure to obtain the first bit
x_{1}
.
Sample a random
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
and let
\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}
be a Pauli
\mathbf{X}
operator acting on the last
k
qubits according to
\mathbf{y}
.
For
i\in[m]
samples necessary to call the decision oracle once, sample a random Pauli on all qubits except the
(n-k+1)
th qubit (i.e. the first qubit corresponding to
\mathbf{x}
), and let their tensor product be
\mathbf{P}_{i}
.
Define
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}_{i}
as the Pauli so that
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\mathbf{C}_{i}\mathbf{P}_{i}
.
Prepare the samples

(\mathbf{C}_{i}(C_{n-k+1}\mathbf{P}_{i})\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}},\;\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}).

(5.11)

Here,
C_{n-k+1}\mathbf{U}
denotes a controlled operation
\mathbf{U}
with the
(n-k+1)
th qubit as the control.
Since
\mathbf{C}_{i}
is uniformly random, we may write
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}_{i}(C_{n-k+1}\mathbf{P}_{i})\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}
, and rewrite the samples as

(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}(C_{n-k+1}\mathbf{P}_{i})\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}).

(5.12)

Suppose first that
x_{1}=1
. Then the distribution is

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{P}^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{P}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\overline{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathbf{y}}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}})

(5.13)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}).

(5.14)

These are precisely structured
\mathsf{LSN}
samples.
Suppose instead that
x_{1}=0
. Then the controlled operation is equivalently the identity, so the distribution of a given sample is

(\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{E}_{i}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{P}_{i}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}).

(5.15)

The application of the random
\mathbf{P}_{i}
fully randomizes the state and yields the maximally mixed state for each sample on every qubit except the
(n-k+1)
th, which remains
x_{1}+y_{1}
with certainty.
However, by Lemma
5.4
, the error
\mathbf{E}_{i}
scrambles this final bit so that the state is negligibly close to the maximally mixed state, since
p=\omega\left(\frac{\log(n)}{n}\right)
.

We conclude that the two options,
x_{1}=0
and
x_{1}=1
, are precisely the types of samples that the decision problem can distinguish with non-negligible advantage.
This probability may be amplified by repeated trials (polynomially many times) such that the failure probability is
o(\frac{1}{k})
, so that, iterating over each
x_{j}
, the correct secret
\mathbf{x}
is outputted with probability
\Omega(1)
.
∎

Using the exact same proof strategy, we may obtain a reduction from Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}
for any
fixed

m
, and in particular for the case of greatest interest,
m=1
.
However, the reduction holds only when there are very few logical qubits.

Corollary 5.6

(Search-to-decision reduction,
\mathsf{LSN}
with very few logical qubits)
.

Let
p=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n})
and
k=O(\log n)
.
Suppose that there exists an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with non-negligible advantage.
Then there exists an efficient quantum algorithm which solves Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, using one call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Using one step of the approach from Theorem
5.5
, we may obtain the first bit of
\mathbf{x}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
. Guessing the remaining bits,
we obtain
\mathbf{x}
with probability
\geq\frac{1}{2^{k-1}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right)=\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, where the equality holds because
k=O(\log(n))
.
∎

5.3
Barriers Against Random Self-Reductions

One of the most direct ways to show that a problem is hard on average is to first show that it is hard in the worst case, and then construct a
random self-reduction
.
This reduction takes the form of an efficient algorithm that scrambles an arbitrary instance of the problem into a random one, such that (a) an average-case solver correctly solves the transformed task with high probability and (b) a solution of the scrambled instance can be transformed efficiently into a solution of the original given instance.
In general, random self-reductions for decoding tasks have proved difficult even in the classical case.
Until recently, no random self-reduction into
\mathsf{LPN}
was known for any parameter regime.
The work of
[
BLVW19
]
showed that for a relatively easy regime of worst-case decoding (in the sense that there exists a quasipolynomial time decoder in this regime) a random self-reduction to
\mathsf{LPN}
can be constructed.
However, the reduction only holds for a restricted subset of classical linear codes.
In the setting of quantum stabilizer codes, it is an open question as to whether any semblance of a random self-reduction can be achieved.
To that end, we first prove that no random self-reduction from an arbitrary worst-case
\mathsf{QNCP}
instance into
\mathsf{LSN}
can exist.
The culprit preventing the existence of such a reduction is exchange symmetry between the code and the error—both are Pauli strings which are randomly scrambled during the reduction, but one must be maximally random while the other cannot be maximally random (less we lose decodability), which is impossible.
However, we will show that an analogous no-go theorem also holds classically.
[
BLVW19
]
circumvent this barrier by finding a clever restriction of possible worst-case instances—codes whose codewords all have nearly the same weight—wherein the reduction is possible at least in some non-trivial parameter regime.
This reduction proceeds by choosing a sufficiently local/sparse scrambling process, which carefully balances scrambling a code with the above restricted structure, without completely scrambling the error.
Quantumly, there are two natural analogs of a local scrambling model: a random sparse Clifford operator, and a random local Clifford circuit.
We prove that these two natural analogs of a local scrambling process cannot produce a random self-reduction for
any
choice of restriction on worst-case instances.
Since locality/sparsity of a scrambling process is the only simple way to control the weight of an arbitrary error—which when too large destroys decodability even information theoretically—this result provides evidence that no random self-reduction exists at all, for any restriction.
We formally conjecture the nonexistence of random self-reductions into
\mathsf{LSN}
(and its variants) at the end of this section.

We begin with a formal definition of a random self-reduction in the stabilizer code setting.

Definition 5.7

(Random self-reduction distribution)
.

Fix parameters
n,k
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{n,k,p}=\Unif(\Stab(n,k))\otimes\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
, where
\Stab(n,k)
is set of
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer codes and
\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
is the
n
-fold depolarizing channel with probability
p
.
A
(\mathcal{V},\epsilon,w,p)

random self-reduction distribution
is an efficiently sampleable distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
over Cliffords
\mathcal{C}_{n}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}}((\mathbf{RC},\;\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}),\;\mathcal{D}_{n,k,p})\leq\epsilon,

(5.16)

for all
\mathbf{C}
whose corresponding stabilizer code
\mathbf{S}
is in
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}
, and for all
\mathbf{E}
such that
\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
.

Here,
\epsilon
quantifies the quality of the reduction.
We say that the reduction is
strong
if
\epsilon=\negl(n)
.

\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}
represents the restriction to a set of worst-case Clifford operators, analogous to the restrictions placed on the generator matrix in the classical reduction.
Lastly,
p
represents the error rate in the random case.
A reduction with
p=\frac{3}{4}
is always possible, as this error is equivalent to replacing the encoded state with a maximally mixed state.
We say that the reduction is
non-trivial
if
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Otherwise, the reduction is trivial, because the encoded state of the output of the reduction is negligibly close to the maximally mixed state.
We also consider
tableau-randomizing
random self-reduction distributions, where instead of randomizing
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer codes, we randomize a stabilizer tableau, an ordered list
\mathbf{S}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{S}_{n-k}
that forms a basis of a stabilizer subspace.
Applying
\mathbf{R}
yields
\mathbf{RS}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{RS}_{n-k}\mathbf{R}^{-1}
, and a random self-reduction distribution of this type must bring this stabilizer tableau close to the uniform distribution. While it is not strictly necessary for a random self-reduction to randomize a tableau, a distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
that randomizes stabilizer subspaces but not tableaus would be rather exotic. One of our barriers to a reduction will apply only to tableau-randomizing reductions, but still poses a significant obstacle.

The above definition establishes a
necessary
condition to achieve the type of worst-case to average-case reduction achieved in
[
BLVW19
]
and sought out in
[
PQS25
]
.
A desired reduction will take a worst-case instance state
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
, and apply the random distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, yielding

(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger})(\mathbf{RC})\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}},

(5.17)

for
\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
In order for this outputted state to be part of a valid
\mathsf{LSN}
instance, the distribution of
(\mathbf{RC})\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
must be close to the distribution of
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
for
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}
.
We have not mentioned the distribution over the secret
\mathbf{x}
because it is always easy to re-randomize the secret by way of Lemma
3.16
.

Remark 5.8

(Reduction for
\mathsf{stateLSN}
)
.

We do not consider random self-reductions for
\mathsf{stateLSN}
.
In the one-sample variant,
\mathsf{LSN}
and
\mathsf{stateLSN}
are equivalent by Lemma
3.20
.
In the multi-sample variant, the scrambling operator would have to map the secret to at least a higher-order
t
-design while also mapping Cliffords to Cliffords by multiplication, which is manifestly impossible except perhaps for
t\leq 3
, in which case the barriers are similar to those we will explore for
\mathsf{LSN}
itself.

We require
\mathbf{R}
to be a Clifford for the following reasons: If we are to be able to apply
\mathbf{R}
to a noisy state, it must be a unitary (we do not consider a potential more general notion of a self-reduction for which the dimension is allowed to change).
Furthermore, we require that
\mathbf{REC}=\mathbf{E^{\prime}RC}=(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger})\mathbf{RC}
.
The latter condition requires
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}=\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, implying that
\mathbf{R}
is necessarily a Clifford.
Therefore, the given definition is essentially as general as possible.
The only potential other operation one may consider is the application of an additional Pauli error
\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}
at the end to
\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}
, a technique analogous to noise flooding
[
HLL24
,
BCD+25
]
.
While we, for simplicity, do not consider this extra operation in our definition, our results below hold even if this extra operation is allowed, because our arguments are centered around the claim that in order for
\mathbf{R}
to scramble
\mathbf{C}
, it already necessarily scrambles the error
\mathbf{E}\mapsto\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}
to an information-theoretically un-decodable error.
Hence, adding further randomness via additional noise cannot help.
We will in this work consider only random self-reductions which are strong.

5.3.1
No Tableau-Randomizing Reduction Without Instance Structure

In principle, it is possible to construct a uniform distribution over low-dimensional subspaces of a space such that the basis for each is not uniformly random.
However, a scrambling operator which completely scrambles the set of stabilizer codes without scrambling tableaus appears difficult to construct, and natural scrambling operations typically produce randomized tableaus.
We here prove, however, that no general tableau-randomizing reduction is possible.
That is, if
\mathcal{V}=\Stab(n,k)
, then no strong non-trivial tableau-randomizing reduction exists.

Lemma 5.9

(Scrambling stabilizer tableaus scrambles the error)
.

If a distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
over
\mathcal{C}_{n}
takes every stabilizer tableau
\mathbf{S}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{S}_{n-k}
corresponding to a stabilizer code
S\in\Stab(n,k)
to a distribution
\mathbf{RS}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{RS}_{n-k}\mathbf{R}^{-1}
for
\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}
which has negligible distance to the uniform distribution over stabilizer tableaus (in TV distance), then
\mathcal{R}_{n}
must also take every error
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
by conjugation to have negligible distance from
\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

There exists a stabilizer tableau with the error
\mathbf{E}
in it, and after being randomized by
\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}
it is
\epsilon
-close in total variation distance to uniform over all stabilizer tableaus, for
\epsilon=\negl(n)
.
Hence,
\mathbf{RER}^{-1}
has a marginal distribution
\epsilon
-close to the marginal distribution of a single Pauli in a stabilizer tableau, i.e. a uniformly random Pauli in
\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\set{\mathbf{I}}
.
This distribution has negligible total variation distance from
\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n}
, and therefore
\mathbf{RER}^{-1}
does as well.
∎

Corollary 5.10

(No strong tableau-randomizing random-self-reduction for
\mathsf{QNCP}
)
.

There is no tableau-randomizing reduction
(\mathcal{V},\epsilon,w,p)
random self-reduction distribution, where
\mathcal{V}
is the set of all stabilized tableaus, from
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
with computational basis logical states to
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
such that
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
and
\epsilon=\negl(n)
.

Proof.

Suppose that such a reduction distribution exists,
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, over Cliffords.
Then the conditions of Lemma
5.9
are met, because for any stabilizer tableau, mixing by the distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
yields a marginal distribution on
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{R}\mathbf{S}_{n-k}\mathbf{R}^{-1}
that is
\epsilon
-close to the uniform distribution.
Hence,
\mathbf{RER}^{-1}
is also
\epsilon
-close to the uniform distribution
\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n}
, and it therefore cannot have negligible total variation distance from
\mathcal{D}_{3/4-1/\poly(n)}^{\otimes n}
.
Indeed, there is an efficient algorithm that distinguishes these distributions, namely by sampling polynomially many times and estimating the expected error weight, which is either
\frac{3}{4}n
or
\left(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}\right)n
.
But the existence of an efficient algorithm proves that the two distributions have non-negligible total variation distance.

∎

5.3.2
No Local Reduction for any Instance Structure

While Corollary
5.10
is a relatively strong barrier to random self-reductions, it is not an inherently quantum phenomenon.
To emphasize this fact, we prove in Appendix
A
that an analogous impossibility theorem holds classically.
However, classically there exists an explicit random self-reduction under a significant but nevertheless non-trivial set of restrictions in the worst-case instance.
Specifically,
[
BLVW19
]
shows that if the rate vanishes as
\frac{k}{n}=O(\frac{1}{n^{\epsilon}})
for a fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1)
; when the maximum error weight is
w=O\left(\frac{n^{\epsilon}}{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\log^{2}n\right)
; and when the worst-case instance’s classical linear code is sufficiently
balanced
; there exists a random self-reduction into
\mathsf{LPN}(k,n,p)
where
p=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Here, a
\beta
-
balanced
code is one for which all nonzero elements of the code space have weight between
(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)n
and
(\frac{1}{2}+\beta)n
.
Despite the fact that the worst-case instance has relatively low weight
w
and the
\mathsf{LPN}
instance has error probability so close to
\frac{1}{2}
that it is difficult to imagine any decoding algorithm other than brute-force enumerated list decoding, the self-reduction is nonetheless remarkable because it achieves
some
notion of a random self-reduction into
\mathsf{LPN}
for the first time.

Corollary
5.10
shows that no general reduction works because there is an inherent symmetry between the code and the error.
A tableau-randomizing random self-reduction wishes to re-randomize the stabilizer tableau completely into a uniformly random set of Paulis, whilst simultaneously re-randomizing the error into a distribution of Paulis which are not close to maximal in weight, as such errors are not decodable even information theoretically.
However, both the tableau and the error are Paulis, so without some structure to break their symmetry, there is no way to act differently on tableaus and errors.
The reduction of
[
BLVW19
]
implicitly incorporate this insight by using a
locally generated
distribution for their random self-reduction distribution.
Specifically, a reduction operator generated in a local manner (e.g. as the sum of random weight-1 vectors) guarantees a bounded norm which we can control.
Further, if we restrict the worst-case instance to have sufficiently low error weight and sufficiently high code weight, it becomes plausible that a weight-bounded random self-reduction operator could approximately randomize the code without completely randomizing the error.

Quantumly, the story appears substantially different.
Enforcing a specific locality of the reduction operator appears to be the only reasonable technique for preventing an over-scrambled error.
Yet, as we show, for natural notions of a local reduction operator in the quantum setting, there is no random self-reduction for
any
choice of restriction on stabilizer codes, and
any
restriction on the error weight.
This inherently quantum phenomenon arises from the qualitatively distinct aspects of scrambling over the Clifford group, as compared to scrambling on a vector space over a binary field.

Any reasonable generation of the classical local self-reduction operator would have to be a sufficiently sparse Clifford.
In particular, this means a Clifford operator
\mathbf{C}
for which its symplectic representation
\Symp(\mathbf{C})
has
o(n)
1s in each column, i.e.,
\max_{i}(|\mathbf{CX}_{i}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}|,\;|\mathbf{CZ}_{i}\mathbf{C}^{\dagger}|)=o(n)
.
Otherwise, even errors of constant weight could potentially be amplified to weight larger than
\frac{3}{4}n
, and therefore the randomized distribution of the error far from
\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n}
.

We prove, however, that no distribution over such non-trivially sparse Cliffords are viable self-reduction operators because there are simply not enough of them to reach the space of stabilizer codes.
While we otherwise consider strong reductions (
\epsilon=\negl(n)
) in this work, the below theorem is a no-go for any
\epsilon
which is non-negligibly away from
1
.

Theorem 5.11

(No random self-reduction from sparse Cliffords)
.

If
k=n-\Omega(n)
, then number of stabilizer codes
N=|\Stab(n,k)|
and the number of Cliffords
M
with column sparsity
d=o(n)
satisfy
\log(N)-\log(M)=\Omega(n^{2})
.
Consequently, no sparse random self-reduction distribution exists for any
\epsilon<1-2^{-o(n^{2})}
, any error weight
w\geq 1
, and any probability parameter
p
.

Proof.

The number of
n-k
dimensional stabilizer subspaces is lower bounded by the number of phase-free stabilizer subspaces (those for which no Pauli operators have no phase).
The number of such subspaces is equal to

\frac{C_{n-k}}{|\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})|},

(5.18)

where
C_{n-k}
is the number of ordered, symplectically orthogonal linearly independent lists in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
of length
n-k
, and
\operatorname{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is the set of invertible
(n-k)\times(n-k)
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
The number of bases of a given
(n-k)
-dimensional symplectic subspace with trivial symplectic inner product is
|\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})|
.

The order of
\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
can be upper bounded by the number of
(n-k)\times(n-k)
matrices, and therefore
\log_{2}(|\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})|)\leq(n-k)^{2}
.
Defining the complement rate
\overline{R}=1-\frac{k}{n}
, it follows that
\log_{2}(|\mathrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})|)\leq\overline{R}^{2}n^{2}
.
Meanwhile, the number of ordered symplectically orthogonal bases can be estimated iteratively as follows. In the
i
th step,
2^{2n-i+1}
of the elements commute with the previous
i-1
elements of the basis.
Of these elements,
2^{i-1}
are in the span of the previous
i-1
.
Taking the product of
2^{2n-i+1}-2^{i-1}
for
i\in[n-k]
, and noting that
2^{2n-i+1}-2^{i-1}\geq 2^{2n-i}
,

\displaystyle\log_{2}(C_{n-k})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n-k}(2n-i)=2n(n-k)-\frac{(n-k)(n-k+1)}{2}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=2n(n-k)-\frac{1}{2}n(n-k)+\frac{1}{2}(k-1)(n-k)

(5.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\overline{R}n^{2}+\frac{\overline{R}}{2}(k-1)n\geq\frac{3}{2}\overline{R}n^{2}.

(5.21)

By assumption,
k=n-\Omega(n)
, so equivalently
\overline{R}=\frac{n-k}{k}=\Omega(1)
.
Hence, noting that
\overline{R}\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\log_{2}(N)=\log_{2}(C_{n-k})-\log_{2}(|\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})|)\geq\left(\frac{3}{2}\overline{R}-\overline{R}^{2}\right)n^{2}=\Omega(n^{2}).

(5.22)

Next, we upper bound the number of sparse Cliffords whose symplectic representation has column-sparsity
d\leq n
.
Here, we are measuring sparseness in the symplectic representation, so the Cliffords are
2n\times 2n
matrices over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
which preserve the symplectic inner product.
Certainly the number of
d
-sparse Cliffords is at most the number of
d
-sparse
2n\times 2n
binary matrices.
The number of column vectors with a sparsity of
d
is
\sum_{i=0}^{d}{2n\choose i}
.
By binomial-entropy bounds (see, e.g., Chapter 1 of
[
RU08
]
),
\sum_{i=0}^{d}{2n\choose i}\leq 2^{nH_{2}(d/2n)}
, where
H_{2}(x)=-x\log_{2}(x)-(1-x)\log_{2}(1-x)
is the binary entropy.
For
d=o(n)
,
H_{2}(\frac{d}{2n})=o(1)
, so

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}{2n\choose i}\right)\leq nH_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2n}\right)=o(n).

(5.23)

Summing up over columns, we conclude that
\log_{2}(M)=o(n^{2})
, and therefore
\log_{2}(N)-\log_{2}(M)=\Omega(n^{2})
.
This completes the proof of the technical claim in the theorem.

To deduce that the above implies the non-existence of a sparse random self-reduction distribution for any
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\Stab(n,k)
, note that for any starting Clifford
\mathbf{C}
, randomizing over sparse Cliffords yields the distribution
\mathbf{RC}
, which has support of size
M=2^{o(n^{2})}

Meanwhile,
\Unif(\Stab(n,k))
is a uniform distribution over a support of size
N=2^{\Omega(n^{2})}
.
As a consequence, the event that these two distributions coincide has probability
p=2^{-\Omega(n^{2})+o(n^{2})}=2^{-\Omega(n^{2})}=\negl(n)
, implying

\TV(\mathbf{RC},\;\Unif(\Stab(n,k)))=1-2^{-\Omega(n^{2})}.

(5.24)

In particular, for any
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
p\in(0,1)
,

\TV((\mathbf{RC},\;\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}),\;\mathcal{D}_{n,k,p})=1-2^{-\Omega(n^{2})}.

(5.25)

This statement concerns only about the number of sparse Cliffords versus the number of stabilizer codes, so it is independent of the error.
∎

As a consequence of the above result, a random self-reduction distribution cannot have bounded sparsity.
This is in contrast to the classical case, in which the reduction of
[
BLVW19
]
does indeed have bounded sparsity.
Without completely bounded sparsity, one of the only remaining natural approaches is to generate a product of
m
random sparse operators in hopes that for a certain choice of
m
, a random self-reduction can be obtained.
We next show that for the simplest model of such an approach, no tableau-randomizing reduction exists.

A reasonable approach to the construction of a reduction operator is to take the product of Cliffords drawn from a small base distribution of sparse matrices.
We develop a formalism for the analysis of such constructions via a connection into the mixing of Markov processes.
A natural construction for the base distribution is a random
t
-local Clifford gate, where
t
is a constant.
That is, sample
t
random qubit indices, and apply a uniformly random Clifford on the
t
qubits.
Such a distribution need not be the only plausible base distribution, but it is most natural in the sense that it is completely symmetric in how it mixes various Paulis and is local not only in sparsity as a symplectic matrix but also in qubit support.
We will prove that no such construction can yield a strong and non-trivial tableau-randomizing reduction, beginning with a map into Markov theory.
A finite Markov process (also known as a Markov chain) is a sequence of random variables
(X_{t})_{t}
such that

\displaystyle\Pr[(X_{t})_{t}=(x_{t})_{t}]=\Pr[X_{t}=x_{t}\;|\;X_{t-1}=x_{t-1}]\cdots\Pr[X_{1}=x_{1}\;|\;X_{0}=x_{0}]\Pr[X_{0}=x_{0}].

(5.26)

In other words, conditioned on the previous value, the current value is independent of all other previous values.
Such a process over a finite state space
\Omega
can always be described by a transition matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Omega|\times|\Omega|}_{\geq 0}
, so that given an initial distribution
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Omega|}_{\geq 0}
, the law of
X_{t}
is given by
Q^{t}\mathbf{v}
.
A process is irreducible if every outcome in
\Omega
has positive probability of being reached in finitely many steps.
The chain is aperiodic if it does not return to any particular outcome at regular intervals.
A basic result of Markov theory is that irreducible and aperiodic chains converge to a limiting distribution, regardless of the starting state.
This limiting distribution is known as the
stationary distribution

\pi
, and satisfies
Q\pi=\pi
.
That is,
\pi
is the unique
+1
eigenvector of
Q
.
Convergence is required for a random self-reduction, as the ultimate reduction distribution must be uniformly random in order to scramble to code.
As such, we formulate our analytical strategy only for irreducible and aperiodic chains.

Definition 5.12

(Optimistic and pessimistic mixing times)
.

Let
M
be an irreducible and aperiodic Markov chain on state space
\Omega
with transition matrix
Q
and stationary distribution
\pi
. The
optimistic mixing time
is given by

\displaystyle\tau_{*}(\epsilon):=\min_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\min\set{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\,}{\,\TV(Q^{t}\mathbf{1}_{s},\;\pi)\leq\epsilon},

(5.27)

and the
pessimistic mixing time
is given by

\displaystyle\tau^{*}(\epsilon):=\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\min\set{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\,}{\,\TV(Q^{t}\mathbf{1}_{s},\;\pi)\leq\epsilon},

(5.28)

where
\mathbf{1}_{s}
is the indicator vector on
s
. Note that
\tau^{*}(\epsilon)
coincides with the standard definition of the mixing time of an irreducible, aperiodic Markov chain and represents the minimum time at which, regardless of starting point, the distribution is
\epsilon
-close to the stationary distribution. On the other hand,
\tau_{*}
represents the minimum time at which
some
choice of starting state will have fully mixed.

Suppose now that we have a reduction for
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
, given by a distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
over
\mathcal{C}_{n}
.
If we study how the Clifford acts on a Pauli string
\mathbf{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{\otimes n}
by conjugation, then we can interpret the action as transitions on a Markov chain.
The state space is
\Omega=\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\set{\mathbf{I}}
(i.e. all non-identity Paulis) and the transition matrix is
