\displaystyle Q_{\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}}=\Pr_{\mathbf{U}\sim\mathcal{D}}[\mathbf{UPU}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{P}_{2}\;|\;\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{1}].

(5.29)

We prove a much stronger statement below than necessary, which may be of interest in its own right.
Specifically, we show that the
t
-qubit random Clifford circuit model scrambles Paulis at an approximately uniform rate.
That is, whether the initial Pauli has weight 1 or weight
\frac{3}{4}n
, the process brings both Paulis to be
\epsilon
-close to a uniformly random non-identity Pauli at approximately the same rate.
This result implies that no restriction on the code (via a restriction on the structure of the stabilizer tableau Paulis) and no restriction on the error can prevent the code from being maximally scrambled before the error is also maximally scrambled.
In fact, there is not even a way to prevent the error from being over-scrambled before even the first Pauli in the tableau becomes maximally scrambled.
As such, no random self-reduction can exist for any subset of codes, and at any regime of parameters using this local model.

Lemma 5.13

(Small scrambling gap implies no tableau-randomizing reduction)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{n}
be a distribution over
\mathcal{C}_{n}
which defines a Markov chain
M
on non-identity Paulis by repeatedly conjugating an initial non-identity Pauli by independent samples
\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
Assume this chain to be irreducible and aperiodic, such that the stationary distribution is a uniformly random non-identity Pauli.
Let
\eta(\epsilon)>0
be the pessimistic error when scrambling optimistically, which we call the
scrambling gap
.
That is,
\eta(\epsilon)
is defined implicitly via

\displaystyle\tau^{*}(\eta(\epsilon))=\tau_{*}(\epsilon).

(5.30)

By definition,
\eta(\epsilon)\geq\epsilon
.
If
\eta(\epsilon)=O(\poly(n)\cdot\epsilon^{b})
for some constant
b>0
, then for any
k,w
and subset
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\Stab(n,k)
, there does not exist a positive integer
d
for which the distribution induced by applying
d
samples from
\mathcal{R}_{n}
yields a
(\mathcal{V},\epsilon,w,p)
random self-reduction operator as defined in Definition
5.7
, such that
\epsilon=\negl(n)
and
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}
be the distribution of
\mathbf{R}_{d}\cdots\mathbf{R}_{1}
, where
\mathbf{R}_{i}
are i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{R}_{n}
.
Suppose for contradiction that there exists a subset
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}
, a weight
w
, and an integer
d>0
for which the unitary operation defined by applying
d
samples from
\mathcal{R}_{n}
in sequence yields a strong and non-trivial self-reduction.
Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{V}
be a valid worst-case code and
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
1\leq\wt(\mathbf{E})\leq w
be a valid worst-case error.
By assumption,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}((\mathbf{RC},\;\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger}),\;\Unif(\mathcal{C}_{n})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})\leq\epsilon=\negl(n),

(5.31)

where
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
. Monotonicity of TV distance implies that

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})\leq\epsilon.

(5.32)

Let
\mathcal{T}=\set{\mathbf{P}_{1},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{P}_{n-k}}
be a stabilizer tableau corresponding to
\mathbf{C}
.
Then the stabilizer tableau corresponding to
\mathbf{RC}
is given by
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}=\set{\mathbf{RP}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger},\;\dots,\;\mathbf{RP}_{n-k}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger}}
.
The stabilizer tableau is computed deterministically from the corresponding Clifford, and the TV distance monotonically decreases with applying functions.
This is because applying functions can only restrict the set of possible events, and the variational form of the TV distance is a maximization over all events.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}\sim\mathcal{C}_{n}\end{subarray}}(\mathbf{RP}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger},\;\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{P}_{1}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\dagger})\leq\epsilon.

(5.33)

Equivalently, with
\mu_{n}
the distribution of a uniformly random non-identity Pauli,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RP}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger},\;\mu_{n})\leq\epsilon.

(5.34)

By definition of the optimistic mixing time and the above,
d\geq\tau_{*}(\epsilon)
.
Then by assumption,
\tau_{*}(\epsilon)=\tau^{*}(\eta(\epsilon))
, so
d\geq\tau^{*}(\eta(\epsilon))
, where
\eta(\epsilon)=\poly(n,\epsilon)\leq n^{a}\epsilon^{b}
for some constants
a,b>0
.
Using the definition of the pessimistic mixing time,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\;\mu_{n})\leq\eta(\epsilon)\leq n^{a}\epsilon^{b}.

(5.35)

By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\;\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n})\leq\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\;\mu_{n})+\TV(\mu_{n},\;\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n})\leq n^{a}\epsilon^{b}+\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right),

(5.36)

where the second equality holds because the total variation distance between
\mu_{n}
, a uniform distribution over Paulis, and
\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n}
, is
\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)
.
At the same time, as previously discussed,

\displaystyle\TV(\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n},\;\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})\geq\frac{1}{\poly(n)}.

(5.37)

By the triangle inequality and Eqn. (
5.32
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\poly(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\TV(\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n},\;\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})

(5.38)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\;\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})+\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RER}^{\dagger},\;\mathcal{D}_{3/4}^{\otimes n})

(5.39)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+n^{a}\epsilon^{b}+\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)

(5.40)

\displaystyle=\negl(n),

(5.41)

which is a contradiction.
∎

We now return to our particular model of study: random
t
-qubit Clifford operators applied in sequence, for a fixed constant
t\geq 2
.
It is convenient to re-express this Markov chain into a simpler form by way of the symplectic representation.
A
t
-qubit random Clifford acts on a Pauli by first choosing a subset of
t
qubits randomly, then doing nothing if the Pauli on those
t
qubits are all
I
.
Otherwise, it transforms the Pauli on those
t
qubits into a uniformly random non-identity Pauli.
The corresponding transformation in symplectic form is described below.

Definition 5.14

(
t
-Clifford chain)
.

Let
M
be a Markov chain whose state space is
\Omega:=\set{0,1,2,3}^{n}\setminus\set{0^{n}}
and whose transitions are given by the following rule. Let the state be
s\in\Omega
. Pick a random
t
-tuple
(i_{1},\;\dots\;i_{t})
where
i_{j}\in[n]
and all
i_{j}
are distinct. If
s_{i_{j}}=0
for all
j\in[t]
, do nothing. Otherwise, pick a random
t
-tuple
(a_{1},\;\dots,\;a_{t})\in\set{0,1,2,3}^{t}\setminus\set{0^{t}}
and replace
(s_{i_{1}},\;\dots,\;s_{i_{t}})
with
(a_{1},\;\dots,\;a_{t})
.

Note that this chain is irreducible and aperiodic
[
HL09
]
.
We now upper bound the pessimistic scrambling time and lower bound the optimistic scrambling time, and thereby show that the scrambling gap is sufficiently small to apply the nonexistence lemma (Lemma
5.13
).

Lemma 5.15

(Random
t
-qubit Cliffords, upper bound)
.

The
t
-Clifford chain from Definition
5.14
with
\tau^{*}(\epsilon)=O(n\log\frac{n}{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

This is proven by a combination of Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.2 in
[
HL09
]
.
While these theorems consider the case when
t=2
, it holds equivalently for
t\geq 2
a constant (it will not hold for
t=1
since no entanglement can be generated).
Note that the proof of Corollary 5.2 is not correct in
[
HL09
]
, but a corrected proof is given by
[
DJ11
]
.
∎

In our lower bound, it does not matter if
t
is not constant, so for completeness we include it in the asymptotic scaling statement.
While such a generalization is not necessary for this work, we believe that minor modifications to the upper bound proofs in
[
HL09
]
will give a matching scaling behavior of
O(\frac{n}{t}\log\frac{n}{\epsilon})
.
We prove the lower bound by a mapping into the coupon collector problem from probability theory, which we first recall.

Problem 5.16

(Coupon collector)
.

There are
n
distinct coupons in a bag, and we repeatedly draw
t
distinct coupons and then put them back.
How many draws do we need for the probability that you collected coupon 1 to be at least
1-\epsilon
?

Let us give a lower bound for Problem
5.16
. Define the event

\displaystyle C_{i}^{m}=\set{\text{coupon }i\text{ not drawn after }m\text{ draws}}.

(5.42)

We are interested in finding the minimum
m
such that
\Pr[C_{1}^{m}]\leq\epsilon
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
n>t
.
If
m=o(\frac{n}{t}\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
then
\Pr[C_{1}^{m}]>\epsilon
. Thus at least
m=\Omega(\frac{n}{t}\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
are required for
\Pr[C_{1}^{m}]\leq\epsilon
.

Proof.

First, note that for all
n>1
,
1-\frac{1}{n}\geq\text{exp}\left(\frac{1}{1-n}\right)
.
This follows from a simple transformation of the standard bound
1+x\leq e^{x}
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Equivalently,
e^{-x}\leq\frac{1}{1+x}
.
Let
x=\frac{1}{n-1}
so that
x>0
.
Then

\displaystyle\text{exp}\left(\frac{1}{1-n}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{exp}\left(-\frac{1}{n-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{n-1}}=\frac{n-1}{n-1+1}=\frac{n-1}{n}=1-\frac{1}{n}.

(5.43)

After
m
steps, the probability of
C_{1}^{m}
is

\displaystyle\Pr[C_{1}^{m}]=\left(1-\frac{t}{n}\right)^{m}\geq\text{exp}\left(-\frac{m}{\frac{n}{t}-1}\right)

(5.44)

by Eqn. (
5.43
).
Solving, we obtain

\displaystyle m<\left(\frac{n}{t}-1\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)=O\left(\frac{n}{t}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right).

(5.45)

Therefore, if
m=o(\frac{n}{t}\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
then then
\Pr[C_{1}^{m}]>\epsilon
.
∎

Lemma 5.18

(Random
t
-qubit Cliffords, lower bound)
.

The
t
-Clifford chain from Definition
5.14
has
\tau_{*}(\epsilon)=\Omega(\frac{n}{t}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
, for all
t<n)
.

Proof.

Let
d=o(\frac{n}{t}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
be the number of steps for which we run the Clifford chain.
Let
\mathbf{V}
be the unitary produced by this process.
We interpret the Clifford chain as a coupon collector problem.
In particular, without loss of generality assume that the first qubit supports the starting Pauli
\mathbf{P}_{0}\neq I
—we may always relabel the qubits until this is the case.
The probability that the first qubit is not in the support of
\mathbf{R}
(i.e.
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{R}_{\text{rest}}
) is at least the probability that after
d
steps the first qubit was never chosen in any of the
t
-tuples.
This is precisely the coupon collector problem as stated in Problem
5.16
. Let
A_{1}
be the event that
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{I}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{R}_{\text{rest}}
.
By Lemma
5.17
, if
d=o(\frac{n}{t})\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}
then
\Pr[A_{1}]>\epsilon
.
Let
\mathbf{P}_{1}:=\mathbf{RP}_{0}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger}
, and consider the event
B_{1}:=\set{\mathbf{P}_{1}\mathbf{P}_{0}\rvert_{1}=\mathbf{I}}
, where the notation
\mathbf{P}\rvert_{1}
indicates the restriction of the Pauli
\mathbf{P}
to the first qubit.
Let
B_{2}
denote the event where
\mathbf{P}_{1}\rvert_{1}\neq\mathbf{I}
.
Now let
\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}
be the distribution of
\mathbf{R}
.
Note that
\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}|B_{2}]\geq\Pr_{\mathbf{V}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1},B_{2}]=\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}]
.
The last equality holds because if
A_{1}
occurs then
B_{2}
does as well, since if the first qubit is not touched then the Pauli remains non-identity. Now,

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1}|B_{2}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1}\;|\;A_{1},B_{2}]\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}|B_{2}]

(5.46)

\displaystyle\phantom{hiii}+\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1}\;|\;\neg A_{1},B_{2}]\left(1-\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}|B_{2}]\right)

(5.47)

\displaystyle\geq(1)\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}]+\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)\left(1-\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[A_{1}]\right)>\frac{1}{3}+\frac{2\epsilon}{3}

(5.48)

since if
A_{1}
occurs,
\mathbf{R}
does not affect the first qubit so
P_{0}\rvert_{1}=P_{1}\rvert_{1}
and thus
P_{1}P_{0}\rvert_{1}=I
.
But if
A_{1}
does not occur, then
\mathbf{R}
touches the first qubit.
The first time
\mathbf{R}
touches the first qubit, it does so by scrambling it and
t-1
other qubits into a uniformly random non-identity Pauli on
t
terms.
In doing so, it removes any bias of the first qubit to any non-identity Pauli, and every subsequent Clifford leaves the marginal distribution unbiased between non-identity Paulis.
The probability of
B_{1}
conditioned on both
\neg A_{1}
and
B_{2}
is therefore
\frac{1}{3}
, since it is equally likely to be any of the three non-identity Paulis.

On the other hand, under the stationary distribution
\pi
, a uniformly random non-identity Pauli, the first qubit is exponentially close to uniformly random.
Since the excluded outcome is the identity, it is unbiased between non-identity Paulis on the first qubit, so the probability of
B_{1}
is
\frac{1}{3}
conditioned on
B_{2}
.
We therefore find that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1},B_{2}]}{\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]}-\frac{\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]}{\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]}\right|

\displaystyle>\left(\frac{1}{3}+\frac{2\epsilon}{3}\pm\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right)-\frac{1}{3}=\left(\frac{2}{3}\epsilon\pm\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right).

(5.49)

Now, note that
|\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]-\frac{3}{4}|\leq\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)
, being the probability that the first qubit has a non-identity Pauli under the distribution of a uniformly random non-identity Pauli.
We may therefore assume that
\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]\geq\frac{1}{2}-\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)
, or else the total variation distance of the two distributions would be at least
\frac{1}{4}
.
We therefore have a crude lower bound on each of the denominators.
Under this assumption,

\displaystyle\bigg|\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1},B_{2}]\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]

\displaystyle-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]\bigg|\geq\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]\cdot\left(\frac{2}{3}\epsilon\pm\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right)

(5.50)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right)\left(\frac{2}{3}\epsilon\pm\text{exp}\left(-\Omega(n)\right)\right)

(5.51)

\displaystyle=\Omega(\epsilon).

(5.52)

Now, assume for the sake of contradiction that both
\left|\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1},B_{2}]-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]\right|=o(\epsilon)
and
\left|\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]\right|=o(\epsilon)
.
Then, the difference above could be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bigg|\Pr_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1},B_{2}]\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]

\displaystyle-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]\Pr_{\mathbf{V}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]\bigg|

(5.53)

\displaystyle=\left|(\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]+o(\epsilon))\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}](\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]+o(\epsilon))\right|

(5.54)

\displaystyle=\left|o(\epsilon)(\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]+\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}])+o(\epsilon)^{2}\right|

(5.55)

\displaystyle=o(\epsilon).

(5.56)

This is a contradiction, and therefore

\displaystyle\TV_{\mathbf{R}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}(\mathbf{RP}_{0}\mathbf{R}^{\dagger},\;\pi)

\displaystyle\geq\max\left(\left|\Pr_{\mathbf{V}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{1},B_{2}]-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{1},B_{2}]\right|,\;\left|\Pr_{\mathbf{V}\sim\mathcal{R}_{n}^{d}}[B_{2}]-\Pr_{\pi}[B_{2}]\right|\right)

(5.57)

\displaystyle=\Omega(\epsilon).

(5.58)

Consequently, if
d=o(\frac{n}{t}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
then regardless of starting Pauli, the distribution is
\Omega(\epsilon)
away from the stationary distribution.
∎

Theorem 5.19

(No local tableau-randomizing reduction for any parameter regime)
.

Fix any logical qubit function
k(n)
and weight function
w(n)
, and any subset of codes
\mathcal{V}(n)\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}
.
Then there is no strong, non-trivial random self-reduction from
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w)
(with computational basis logical states) to
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
.
More precisely, there is no
(\mathcal{V}(n),\;\epsilon,\;w(n),\;p(n))
random self-reduction distribution
\mathcal{R}_{n}
on
\llbracket n,k\rrbracket
stabilizer codes, such that
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
and
\epsilon=\negl(n)
, and
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is a sequence of random
t
-local Cliffords.

Proof.

The pessimistic mixing time of the chain is given by
\tau^{*}(\epsilon)=O(n\log\frac{n}{\epsilon})\leq\alpha n\log\frac{n}{\epsilon}
for sufficiently large
n
, by Lemma
5.15
. The optimistic mixing time is given by
\tau_{*}(\epsilon)=\Omega(n\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})\geq\beta n\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}
for sufficiently large
n
, by Lemma
5.18
. Here
\alpha,\beta
are constants. Then

\displaystyle\beta n\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\leq\tau_{*}(\epsilon)=\tau^{*}(\eta(\epsilon))\leq\alpha n\log\frac{n}{\eta(\epsilon)}.

(5.59)

Solving, we find that
\eta(\epsilon)\leq n\epsilon^{\beta/\alpha}
. Hence,
\eta(\epsilon)
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
5.13
, which completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

These combined results show that the landscape of random self-reducibility for stabilizer codes qualitatively differs from the classical analog.
Both classically and quantumly, restriction on the set of allowed codes in the worst case, as well as the weight of the worst-case error, is necessary.
However, with such restrictions, some notion of a random self-reduction is achievable, because local scrambling distributions there mix certain high-weight bitstrings substantially more quickly than low-weight bitstrings.
Therefore, by assigning codes to be comprised of the certain structured high-weight bitstrings and the errors to be comprised of low-weight bitstrings, a local model suffices as a reduction distribution.
Quantumly, the natural analogs of local scrambling—sparse Cliffords or products of sparse Cliffords—have no clear gap.
Sparse Cliffords unconditionally cannot give a reduction, and products of sparse Cliffords cannot randomize a tableau without randomizing the error.
Therefore, quantumly it appears that the primary barrier is not necessarily finding the right restriction of stabilizer codes, but rather finding how to (a) scramble the code space without scrambling the tableau and (b) scramble using non-sparse Cliffords while also not blowing up the error.
Satisfying just one of these conditions already appears challenging, so the two constraints together pose a substantial barrier to a random self-reduction.
It remains an open question as to whether these barriers can be surpassed, or can be strengthened to a complete impossibility theorem for random self-reductions.
We conjecture that, at least in the case of tableau-randomizing reductions, analysis techniques generalizing the scrambling gap formalism will reveal unconditionally that a reduction cannot scramble the tableau.

Conjecture 5.20
.

There is no
(\mathcal{V},\epsilon,w,p)
tableau-randomizing random self-reduction operator (as defined in Definition
5.7
) for
\mathsf{LSN}
, for any
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
w\geq 1
, such that
\epsilon=\negl(n)
and
p=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.

At the same time, these barriers do not definitely prove the non-existence of a random self-reduction.
Moreover, we have not considered an even more general notion of a random self-reduction, e.g. when the dimensions
n,k
can increase by a polynomial factor.
It is unclear whether allowing for a more broad definition of a random self-reduction can circumvent the strength of the barrier discussed above.
More precisely, can a random self-reduction be achieved by overcoming the above barriers, by increasing the dimension (i.e. by letting the reduction operator
\mathbf{R}
be an isometry), or by letting
\epsilon=\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
(in which case we would require a smaller
p
to be non-trivial, e.g.
p<\frac{3}{4}-\Omega(1)
)?

6
Average-Case Hardness of Quantum Stabilizer Decoding

Despite the apparent challenges in giving a random self-reduction for
\mathsf{LSN}
, we establish in this section the algorithmic hardness of
\mathsf{LSN}
by directly reducing
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
More precisely, we show that
\mathsf{LSN}^{m}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
for any
k\geq 1
,
p=\Omega(n^{-(1-\epsilon)})
with
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and any
m\geq 1
is harder than an instance of
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(k^{\prime},\;2n,\;p^{\prime}\right)
for
k^{\prime}=p^{\prime}n
,
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{6}
.
These parameters include any problem instance with a constant error probability
p
.
In this regime, our result states that
\mathsf{LSN}
with
any
number of samples
m
and
any
number of logical qubits
k\geq 1
, is as hard as
\mathsf{LPN}
with a constant rate.
In spite of much effort, no sub-exponential time algorithms for
\mathsf{LPN}
at constant rate and constant error probability are known.
Thus, the existence of a sub-exponential time algorithm which can decode a typical quantum stabilizer code—subject to a constant noise rate—with non-negligible probability implies by way of this reduction a cryptographic breakthrough in the form of a sub-exponential algorithm for the hardest regime of
\mathsf{LSN}
.

It is illustrative to observe how standard classical attacks fail on
\mathsf{LSN}
.
We discuss the algorithms of BKW
[
BKW03
]
and Lyubashevsky
[
Lyu05
]
which apply respectively in the regimes of negligible rate and inverse-polynomial rate.
The BKW algorithm finds a sparse vector
\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}
that is in the left-kernel of the code
\mathbf{A}
.
Then, calculating
\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e})=\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{e}
solves Decision
\mathsf{LPN}
, because this product is 1 with low probability if
\mathbf{s}
is sufficiently small.
However, in order for a sparse vector to exist at all, there must be superpolynomially (in
k
) many rows.
The runtime of the algorithm is
2^{O(k/\log k)}
, and in particular sub-exponential.

\mathsf{LSN}
corresponds to a problem of
\mathsf{LPN}
where the rate is artificially restricted (in the sense that the encoding matrix in the classical representation has dimensions
2n\times(n+k)
, so the “rate” is at least
1/2
), and therefore BKW cannot apply at all.
Lyubashevsy’s algorithm is a technique for amplifying polynomially many rows (
k^{1+\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
) to superpolynomially many, and then applying the BKW algorithm to achieve a runtime of
2^{O(k/\log\log k)}
.
Once again,
\mathsf{LSN}
with constant probability and any rate corresponds to an
\mathsf{LPN}
problem with a constant rate, and therefore Lyubashevsky’s algorithm fails as well.

This explicit relationship between
\mathsf{LPN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}
establishes a qualitative difference between
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
and
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.
The former problem has a trivial brute force algorithm with runtime
O(2^{k}n^{3})
: guess the solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, and check if
\mathbf{Ax}^{\prime}
is close to the sample
\mathbf{Ax}+\mathbf{e}
.
On the other hand, even when
k=1
,
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
is as hard as
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(k^{\prime},\;2n,\;p^{\prime}\right)
for
k^{\prime}=p^{\prime}n
,
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{6}
.
Therefore, there cannot be an algorithm solving
\mathsf{LSN}
with any runtime of the form
O(f(k)\cdot\poly(n))
for any
f(k)
(e.g.
f(k)=2^{k}
or
2^{2^{k}}
), as such an algorithm would imply an
efficient
algorithm for constant-rate
\mathsf{LPN}
.
Previously, we noted this barrier by observing that there is no obvious way to certify the validity of a proposed answer
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to a
\mathsf{LSN}
instance
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
.
To do so would be to solve a certain short vector problem: determining if
\mathbf{EC}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{C}\ket{0^{n-k},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
differ by a low-weight Pauli.
We can now establish a much stronger statement, namely that the existence of an efficient certification protocol, or equivalently the existence of a solution to this short vector problem, would imply a polynomial time algorithm solving
\mathsf{LPN}
in the hardest regime.
One interpretation of these results is that while in
\mathsf{LPN}
the natural security parameter is
k
, in
\mathsf{LSN}
the natural security parameter is
n
, making
\mathsf{LSN}
substantially harder when
n
and
k
differ significantly.

Our proof of this reduction ties together several components from previous sections.
Because
\mathsf{LSN}
and
\mathsf{LPN}
differ greatly even at a qualitative level in their definitions, we introduce several hybrids to bridge the gap.
On the quantum side, we will use exclusively the equivalent classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
developed in Section
4
.
This representation appears much more qualitatively similar to
\mathsf{LPN}
in the sense that the samples are of the form
(\mathbf{B},\;\mathbf{B}\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{e})
.
However, it differs in that (a)
\mathbf{z}
is mostly junk (the secret constitutes a small sliver of
\mathbf{z}
), (b) the columns of
\mathbf{B}
have certain symplectic orthogonality conditions, and (c) the error is depolarizing instead of Bernoulli.
Therefore, on the classical side, we will introduce a problem which we call
symplectic learning parity with noise
(
\mathsf{sympLPN}
), which differs from
\mathsf{LPN}
in that the encoding matrix has symplectically orthogonal columns and that the error is depolarizing.
We reduce
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
by modifying the code and error, then from
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
.
The latter reduction proceeds by a strange step wherein we embed directly into the junk, not into the secret of
\mathsf{LSN}
.
As a consequence, we can reduce only into the decision variant of
\mathsf{LSN}
instead of the search variant.
Since we wish ultimately to reduce to the search variant, we will give the reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
to the multi-sample variant of Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
and then apply our multi-sample decision-to-search reduction from Section
5
.
This together will complete the reductive steps from Fig.
2
.
This final step is particularly interesting, because the multi-sample variant plays a crucial role despite being a non-standard notion of quantum error correction.

6.1
Symplectic Learning Parity with Noise

To begin, we formally introduce the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
problem. We introduce both
search
and
decision
variants of the problem; although we will mainly consider the latter variant.
Informally,
\mathsf{sympLPN}
is the task of decoding a random classical linear code with a symplectically self-orthogonal encoding matrix
\mathbf{A}
, rate
\frac{k}{n}=\frac{1}{2}
, and error drawn from a depolarizing distribution (in symplectic representation).

Definition 6.1

(
\mathsf{sympLPN}
)
.

The Symplectic Learning Parity with Noise problem, denoted by
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,p\right)
, is characterized by an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in(0,1)
. We distinguish between two variants of the problem:

\bullet

Search

\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,p\right)
is the task of finding
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
given as input a sample of the form

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n},\;\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2))

(6.1)

where
\mathbf{A}
is random subject to the constraint that its
n
columns are linearly independent and symplectically orthogonal, and where
\mathbf{e}
is a depolarizing error with probability parameter
p
, i.e.
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e})\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
.

\bullet

Decision

\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,p\right)
is the task of distinguishing between the samples

\displaystyle(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n},\;\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2))\quad\text{ or }\quad(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n},\;\mathbf{u}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n})

(6.2)

where
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{e}
are as before and
\mathbf{u}
is a uniformly random bitstring.

This definition is motivated by bridging part but not all of the differences between
\mathsf{LPN}
and the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
.
We will first prove that Decision
\mathsf{LPN}
reduces to Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}
, and then construct this latter reduction.
To reduce
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
, we first construct an algorithm that takes a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(2n-\ell(1+\epsilon))\times l}
, and adds
\ell(1+\epsilon)
rows to create a matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}
with symplectically orthogonal columns.
This algorithm has the property that it takes the uniform distribution over codes
\mathbf{A}
to a distribution close to uniform over symplectically orthogonal codes.
We then correct the error distribution by applying the depolarizing decomposition into Bernoullis from Corollary
2.15
.

Lemma 6.2

(Symplectic matrix extension)
.

Let
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
be parameters such that
\ell(1+\epsilon)\leq n
.
There exists a
\poly(n)
time classical algorithm which on input a uniformly random
(2n-\ell(1+\epsilon))\times\ell
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, outputs

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\\
\mathbf{A^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}

(6.3)

for a matrix
\mathbf{A^{\prime}}
with dimensions
\ell(1+\epsilon)\times\ell
, such that

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{B}\sim\nu}(\mathbf{B},\mu_{2n,\ell})=\negl(\ell),

(6.4)

where
\mu_{2n,\ell}
is the uniform distribution over
2n\times\ell
full-rank matrices with symplectically orthogonal columns and
\nu
is the output distribution of the algorithm.

Proof.

Decompose
\boldsymbol{A}
as

\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{N}_{1}\\
\mathbf{M}\\
\mathbf{N}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

(6.5)

where
\mathbf{N}_{1},\mathbf{N}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{(n-\ell(1+\epsilon))\times\ell}
, and
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell(1+\epsilon)\times\ell}
.
The matrix
\mathbf{B}
to has symplectically orthogonal columns if and only if the matrix

\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{N}_{1})^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{N}_{2}+\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{A^{\prime}}

(6.6)

is symmetric.
We can check this fact by observing that
\mathbf{S}_{ij}+\mathbf{S}_{ji}
is precisely the symplectic inner product of the
i
th and
j
th columns of
\mathbf{B}
.
Hence,
\mathbf{S}_{ij}=\mathbf{S}_{ji}
corresponds to symplectic orthogonality.

We sample a random symmetric matrix
\mathbf{S^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell\times\ell}
, and let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{S^{\prime}}-(\mathbf{N}_{1})^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{N}_{2}
.
Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{A^{\prime}}
for which
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{A^{\prime}}=\mathbf{T}
; this can be done efficiently being that it is a random solution to a system of linear equations.
(If no such matrix exists, then output the zero matrix.)
Append
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{A}
to form
\mathbf{B}
.
For any such choice of
\mathbf{A^{\prime}}
,

\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{N}_{1})^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{N}_{2}+\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{A^{\prime}}=\mathbf{S^{\prime}},

(6.7)

and therefore
\mathbf{B}
is symplectically orthogonal as desired.

We next show that if
\mathbf{A}
is uniformly random, then
\mathbf{B}
is close to a uniformly random full-rank matrix with symplectically orthogonal columns.
We do so by proving that for almost all
\mathbf{A}
, the number of choices
n_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{A})
for a full-rank symplectically orthogonal
\mathbf{B}
are the same.
Suppose first that
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell\times\ell(1+\epsilon)}
is full-rank; this occurs with probability
r=1-\negl(\ell)
since
\mathbf{A}
is uniformly random.
Note that by dimensionality,
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}
being full-rank implies that
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{A}
are full-rank.
Then we claim that

n_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{A})=2^{\ell(\ell+1)/2}\cdot 2^{\epsilon\ell^{2}}.

(6.8)

There are
2^{\ell(\ell+1)/2}
choices for
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
.
For each
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, there are
2^{\dim\mathbf{A}^{\prime}-\dim\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}=2^{(1+\epsilon)\ell^{2}-\ell^{2}}=2^{\epsilon\ell^{2}}
choices of
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
by the assumption that
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}
is full rank.
For a fixed
\mathbf{A}
, the choice of
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
uniquely determines
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, so every pair
(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{S}^{\prime})
gives a distinct solution
\mathbf{B}
, proving the claim.
Let
n_{\nu}
be the number of
\mathbf{B}
that the algorithm can produce, conditioned on a full-rank
\mathbf{M}^{\intercal}
.

n_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{A})
does not depend on
\mathbf{A}
, so

\displaystyle n_{\nu}=2^{(2n-\ell(1+\epsilon))\ell}\cdot 2^{\ell(\ell+1)/2}\cdot 2^{\epsilon\ell^{2}}=2^{2n\ell-\ell^{2}(1+\epsilon)+\ell(\ell+1)/2+\epsilon\ell^{2}}=2^{2n\ell-\ell(\ell-1)/2}.

(6.9)

On the other hand, we claim that there are exactly

\displaystyle n_{\mu}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}(2^{2n-(j-1)}-2^{j-1})

(6.10)

2n\times\ell
binary matrices which are full-rank and symplectically orthogonal.
This can be seen from an iterative construction procedure: at the
j
th step, out of
2^{2n}
possible strings, the condition of symplectic orthogonality with
j-1
previous independent strings decreases the dimensionality by
j-1
.
Furthermore, among the
2^{2n-(j-1)}
strings which are symplectically orthogonal to the previous strings,
2^{j-1}
lie in the span of the previous strings, so we subtract off those strings.
Asymptotically, however, this subtraction bears no relevance.
This is because our assumption of
\ell(1+\epsilon)\leq n
implies that
n-\ell\geq\epsilon\ell
, and

\displaystyle\frac{2^{j-1}}{2^{2n-(j-1)}}=2^{-2(n-(j-1))}\leq 2^{-2(n-(\ell-1))}\leq 2^{-2(\epsilon\ell+1)}=2^{-\epsilon\ell}=\negl(\ell).

(6.11)

Hence,
n_{\mu}=r^{\prime}\cdot 2^{2n\ell-\ell(\ell-1)/2}=r^{\prime}\cdot n_{\nu}
, where
r^{\prime}=1-\negl(\ell)
.
To complete the proof, note that according to the algorithmic distribution
\nu
, conditioned on an event (
M^{\intercal}
is full rank) which has probability
r=1-\negl(\ell)
, the distribution is uniform over
n_{\nu}
elements.
Meanwhile, according to the desired distribution
\mu_{2n,\ell}
, every full-rank symplectically orthogonal matrix has equal probability of being drawn, of which there are
n_{\mu}=r^{\prime}n_{\nu}=(1-\negl(\ell))n_{\nu}
of them.
These probabilities differ by a negligible multiplicative factor, with
\nu
conditioning on an event with
1-\negl(\ell)
probability.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{B}\sim\nu}(\mathbf{B},\mu_{2n,\ell})=\negl(\ell)

(6.12)

as desired.
∎

Before demonstrating the reduction, we show that Bernoulli errors on a fixed, known set of indices can be randomized to produce independent Bernoulli errors with lower probability on all indices.

Lemma 6.3

(Bernoulli noise extension)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
be parameters such that
m=\omega(\log n)
, and let
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
be a vector such that
e_{i}=0
for
i\leq n-m
, and
e_{i}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
for
i>n-m
.
Then for any constant
\delta\in(0,1)
, there is an efficiently sampleable distribution
\mu_{n,m}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that

\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\sim S^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{n,m}\end{subarray}}(\pi(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}}),\;\mathsf{Ber}(p)^{\otimes n})=\negl(n).

(6.13)

Here,
p=\frac{m}{2n}\left(1+\delta\right)
and
\pi
is a uniformly random permutation drawn from the symmetric group
S^{n}
on
n
elements.

Proof.

We will construct a procedure for sampling
\mathbf{e^{\prime}}
.
First, sample
T\sim\mathsf{Bin}(n,\;2p)
, so that
\mathbb{E}[T]=2np=m(1+\delta)
.
By a Chernoff bound,

\Pr\left[M\geq\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}[T]\right]\leq\text{exp}\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2+\delta}\mathbb{E}[T]\right)=\text{exp}\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}(1+\delta)}{2+\delta}m\right)=\negl(n),

(6.14)

since
m=\omega(\log n)
.
Therefore, with probability
1-\negl(n)
,

T\geq m(1+\delta)\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)=m\left(1+\frac{\delta}{2}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\right)>m.

(6.15)

Choose
\mathbf{e^{\prime}}
to be a vector where the first
n-T
entries are zero, and the rest are sampled uniformly randomly.
Conditioned on the event that
T>m
,
\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
is equidistributed as
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
itself, since
\mathbf{e}
has zeros in the first
n-m<n-T
entries, and the remaining entries are uniformly random.

Because
\pi
erases any correlation with index, the distribution of
\pi(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}})
, conditioned on
T>m
, can be equivalently represented by first sampling
T\sim\mathsf{Bin}(n,\;2p)
random indices, and then choosing the bit at each index to be 1 with probability
\frac{1}{2}
independently.
The random choice of indices can instead be generated by independently for each index selecting it with probability
2p
.
Thus, each bit is
1
independently with probability
2p\cdot\frac{1}{2}=p
.
Consequently, except under a negligibly improbable event,
\pi(\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
is distributed as
\mathsf{Ber}(p)^{\otimes n}
, which completes the proof.
∎

We now use the above lemmas to give a reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
.

Theorem 6.4

(
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
)
.

Let
n,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
be parameters and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be a constant, such that
\ell(1+\epsilon)\leq n
and
\ell=\Omega(n^{\epsilon})
.
Define
r=\frac{\ell}{2n}(1+\epsilon)^{2}\leq p+\frac{\ell}{2n}(1+3\epsilon)
, where
p\in(0,1)
, and
q=p+r-2pr\leq p+\frac{\ell}{2n}(1+3\epsilon)
.
Let
\mathcal{O}
be an oracle that solves Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,\;3q\right)
with advantage
\eta
.
Then there exists an algorithm, running in time
\poly(n)
, which solves Decision
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(\ell,\;\lceil 2n-\ell(1+\epsilon)\rceil,p\right)
to advantage
\eta-\negl(n)
, using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
be a sample of Decision
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(\ell,\;2n-\ell(1+\epsilon),p\right)
, where either
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}
or
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell}
.
First, suppose that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}
.
By Lemma
6.2
, we may obtain a full-rank, symplectically orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}\\
\mathbf{A^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}
such that the distribution over
\mathbf{B}
is negligibly close to uniform over full-rank symplectically orthogonal matrices in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}
.
Sample
\mathbf{r}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell(1+\epsilon)}
and define

\mathbf{y^{\prime}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{r}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{y}_{1}\\
\mathbf{y}_{2}\end{bmatrix},

(6.16)

where
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
.
Likewise, we re-write
\mathbf{B}
as

\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{1}\\
\mathbf{A}_{2}\end{bmatrix},

(6.17)

where
\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times\ell}
.
By definition,
\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}_{1}
for a Bernoulli error
\mathbf{e}_{1}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)^{\otimes n}
.
Because
\mathbf{r}
is uniformly random, we may also equivalently express
\mathbf{y}_{2}
as

\mathbf{y}_{2}=\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}_{2}+\mathbf{f},

(6.18)

where
\mathbf{e}_{2}
is
\mathsf{Ber}(p)
on the first
n-\ell(1+\epsilon)
bits and zero on the rest, and
\mathbf{f}
is zero on the first
n-\ell(1+\epsilon)
bits and independently
\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
on the rest.

Next, sample a random permutation
\pi\sim S^{n}
. Denote by
\pi\oplus\pi
a permutation operator on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
whereby the first
n
and last
n
bits are permuted according to the same permutation
\pi\in S^{n}
.
Denote
(\pi\oplus\pi)(\mathbf{M})
for a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}
the action of
\pi\oplus\pi
column-wise on
\mathbf{M}
.
By definition,
\pi\oplus\pi
preserves the symplectic inner product, since for each
i\in[n]
indices
(i,i+n)
are relabeled jointly to
(j,j+n)
for some
j\in[n]
.
Thus, the distribution of a full-rank symplectically orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{B}
is invariant under action of
\pi\oplus\pi
.
We also sample
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
using Lemma
6.3
, with parameters
m=\ell(1+\epsilon)
and
\delta=\epsilon
.
Now, define

\mathbf{z}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{z_{1}}\\
\mathbf{z_{2}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\pi(\mathbf{y_{1}})\\
\pi(\mathbf{y_{2}}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}})\end{bmatrix}

(6.19)

and

\mathbf{K}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{1}\\
\mathbf{K}_{2}\end{bmatrix}=(\pi\oplus\pi)(\mathbf{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\pi(\mathbf{A}_{1})\\
\pi(\mathbf{A}_{2})\end{bmatrix}.

(6.20)

Under these definitions,

\mathbf{z}_{1}=\mathbf{K}_{1}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}_{1},\quad\mathbf{z}_{2}=\mathbf{K}_{2}\mathbf{x}+\pi(\mathbf{e}_{2})+\pi(\mathbf{f}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}}).

(6.21)

Marginally,
\pi(\mathbf{f}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})\sim\mathsf{Ber}(q)^{\otimes n}
up to
\negl(n)
corrections in probabilities by Lemma
6.3
.
Meanwhile, independently,
\pi(\mathbf{e}_{2})\sim\mathsf{Ber}(p)^{\otimes n}
.
Hence,
\pi(\mathbf{e}_{2})+\pi(\mathbf{f}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime})
is distributed as an
n
-fold Bernoulli with probability
r=\frac{l}{2n}(1+\epsilon)^{2}\leq\frac{l}{2n}(1+3\epsilon)
.
Summing the independent Bernoulli errors
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{e}_{2})
and
\mathbf{P}(\mathbf{f}+\mathbf{e^{\prime}})
gives an
n
-fold Bernoulli distribution with probability parameter
q=p(1-r)+r(1-p)=p+r-2pr\leq p+r
.
This error parameter is larger than that of
\mathbf{e}_{1}
by
(1-2p)r
.
By way of Lemma
2.16
, we may sample some
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(q)
as well.

Presently, our transformed sample

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{K},\;\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{g}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(6.22)

is nearly distributed as a valid
\mathsf{sympLPN}
structured sample, except (a)
\mathbf{C}
has
\ell
columns instead of
n
, and is not necessarily full rank, and (b) the error is Bernoulli instead of depolarizing.
The first is remedied by additional sampling: we construct a random completion
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times(n-\ell)}
of
\mathbf{K}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times\ell}
.
More precisely, the
\ell\times\ell
submatrix consisting of the first
\ell
columns of
\mathbf{K}
is marginally uniformly random, and so full rank with probability
1-\negl(\ell)=1-\negl(n)
.
Thus,
\mathbf{K}
is also full rank with probability
1-\negl(n)
.
We can then iteratively sample the next column of
\mathbf{L}
subject to being independent from and symplectically orthogonal to all previous columns as well as columns of
\mathbf{K}
.
We also extend the secret by sampling
\mathbf{w}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n-\ell}
.
Now, our updated sample is of the form

\displaystyle\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}\;\mathbf{L}\end{bmatrix},\;\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{Kw}+\mathbf{g}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{Kw}\end{bmatrix}\right).

(6.23)

The last remaining issue is the distribution of the error, which must be transformed from Bernoulli to depolarizing.
By Corollary
2.15
, this transformation can be done by sampling a random
\mathbf{h}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(q)^{\otimes n}
and adding
\mathbf{h}
to each of the two halves of the output.
The cost of such an operation is that the error probability increases by at most a factor of 3.
Since we can always increase the error to exactly a factor of 3 larger by Lemma
2.17
, we can assume that the probability increases to
3q
.
The final sample

\displaystyle\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}\;\mathbf{L}\end{bmatrix},\;\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{z}_{1}+\mathbf{Kw}+\mathbf{g}+\mathbf{h}\\
\mathbf{z}_{2}+\mathbf{Kw}+\mathbf{h}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(6.24)

has, up to
\negl(n)
corrections discussed above, the distribution of a
\mathsf{sympLPN}(n,\;3q)
instance.

Finally, suppose instead that the given
\mathsf{LPN}
sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
was unstructured, so that
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ell}
.
Then the above transformations would still map
\mathbf{A}
to a valid
\mathsf{sympLPN}
encoding matrix (up to negligible corrections), but all the transformations performed on
\mathbf{y}
leave its distribution invariant because
\mathbf{y}
is uniformly random and we extend it by padding a uniformly random vector
\mathbf{r}
.
Thus, the unstructured
\mathsf{LPN}
sample also maps to an unstructured
\mathsf{sympLPN}
sample.
We now run
\mathcal{O}
on the transformed sample and output its decision.
By the above, we will correctly decide the
\mathsf{LPN}
instance if and only if we correctly decide the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
instance, unless a negligibly rare event occurs.
Consequently, our probability of success can decrease by at most
\negl(n)
from
\mathcal{O}
’s probability of success.
This completes the reduction.
∎

6.2
From Symplectic
\mathsf{LPN}
to
\mathsf{LSN}

Initially, the reasonable next step may appear to be a reduction from Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
.
By dimension counting alone, the natural way to perform this reduction is to put the
2n\times n

\mathsf{sympLPN}
encoding matrix
\mathbf{B}
into the stabilizer/logical
\mathbf{Z}
part of the classical representation of
\mathsf{LSN}
, and then generate the secret/logical
\mathbf{X}
part ourselves.
While this reduction does go through exactly as stated, it is not helpful in showing that
Search

\mathsf{LSN}
is hard, because we have no way of reducing Decision
\mathsf{LSN}
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
.
There is also no natural way to reduce Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}
directly to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
.
This is because we generate the secret for
\mathsf{LSN}
ourselves in the above reduction sketch, so a search oracle finding the
\mathsf{LSN}
secret has no utility at all.
To circumvent this barrier, we give a stronger decision reduction—namely, into multi-sample
\mathsf{LSN}
—and then apply our decision-to-search reduction from Section
5
so as to finally reduce to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
(and thereby also Decision and Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
).

Lemma 6.5

(Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}
to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}
)
.

Let
k\geq 1
be any positive integer, which may depend on
n
.
Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves the classical representation of Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with advantage
\eta
.
Then there exists an efficient classical algorithm which solves Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,p\right)
to advantage
\frac{\eta}{\poly(n)}
using a single call to
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

A sample of Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,p\right)
consists of
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
, where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
is a uniformly random full-rank symplectically orthogonal matrix.
In the structured case,
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{e}
, for uniformly random
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, and
\Symp^{-1}(\mathbf{e})\sim\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n}
.
In the unstructured case,
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
.
Suppose
\mathcal{O}
uses
m=\poly(n)
samples.
Recall that each sample is of the form
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\;\mathbf{B}_{i},\;\mathbf{z}_{i})
, where in the structured case
\mathbf{z}_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
and in the unstructured case
\mathbf{z}_{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
.
The matrices
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and vectors
\mathbf{r}_{i}
are the
junk
matrices and vectors, respectively.
They are re-generated for each sample and contain no information about the
\mathsf{LSN}
secret
\mathbf{y}
.
Our basic strategy is to embed the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
data
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
into the junk matrix and vector for one of the samples.
This is a strict departure from the typical forms of coding reductions, wherein the secret from one problem becomes the secret in the other.
This usual strategy is infeasible in our case, as the dimensions are completely mismatched, e.g. it is impossible to embed a secret of length
n
into the secret of
\mathsf{LSN}
if
k=1
.
If
m=1
, there is no ambiguity as to which sample we would embed into, and we would thus be essentially done.
However, since we have an arbitrary polynomial number of samples, we must thoughtfully choose which sample to embed the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
data into, so as to maintain a reduction.
To do so, we will proceed by a randomized version of the hybrid argument.
That is, we will choose a
random
sample out of the
m
possibilities in which to embed the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
data.

Sample a random index
j\in[m]
and a random
\mathsf{LSN}
secret
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
, and output

\left(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix},\mathbf{z}_{i}\right)\text{ for }i\in[l],

(6.25)

where

1.

If
i>j
, the
i
th sample is structured with secret
\mathbf{y}
.
That is,
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
,
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times k}
are uniformly random symplectically orthogonal matrices such that
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}
is full rank, and
\mathbf{z}_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{r}_{i}+\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{e}_{i}
where
\mathbf{r}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n}
is uniformly random and
\mathbf{e}_{i}\sim\Symp(\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes n})
.

2.

If
i=j
, we embed the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
data into the sample.
Formally,
\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times k}
is uniformly random and symplectically orthogonal such that
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}
is full rank, and
\mathbf{z}_{i}=\mathbf{B}_{i}\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{z}
,

3.

If
i<j
, the
i
th sample is unstructured.
That is,
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times n}
,
\mathbf{B}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2n\times k}
are uniformly random symplectically orthogonal matrices such that
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{B}_{i}\end{bmatrix}
is full rank, and
\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
is uniformly random.

We then apply the oracle
\mathcal{O}
to the resulting sample, and output its decision.

Depending on whether the original sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
was structured or unstructured, the set of
m
samples submitted to
\mathcal{O}
has a different number of structured and unstructured samples.
The first
j-1
samples are always unstructured, by construction, and samples
j+1,\;\dots,\;m
are always structured.
Meanwhile, the
j
th sample is structured if and only if the
\mathsf{sympLPN}
instance
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
is structured.

Let
p_{k}
be the probability that
\mathcal{O}
outputs
STRUCTURED
when the first
k
of samples are structured, and the remaining
m-k
are unstructured.
Then, the probability that
\mathcal{O}
outputs
STRUCTURED
by our reduction when
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
is indeed structured is

\Pr[\mathcal{O}=\text{{STRUCTURED}}\,|\,\text{{STRUCTURED}}]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}p_{k},

(6.26)

since
j
is chosen randomly from 1 to
m
. Likewise, when
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{z})
is unstructured,

\Pr[\mathcal{O}=\text{{UNSTRUCTURED}}\,|\,\text{{UNSTRUCTURED}}]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{k},

(6.27)

The difference in these two probabilities is

\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}p_{k}-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{k}\right|=\frac{1}{m}|p_{0}-p_{m}|.

(6.28)

However, having
0
unstructured samples and
m
unstructured samples are precisely the two cases of Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
, and by assumption
\mathcal{O}
succeeds in this problem with advantage
\eta
.
Therefore,
|p_{0}-p_{m}|=\eta
, and as a result the advantage our reduction has on Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}(n,p)
, given by Eqn. (
6.28
), is
\frac{\eta}{m}
.
∎

With these lemmas, we may at last establish the hardness of Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
.

Theorem 6.6

(Average-case hardness of stabilizer decoding with any logical dimension)
.

Let
p=\Omega\left(n^{-(1-\epsilon)}\right)
, for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
a constant, and let
k\geq 1
be an arbitrary number of logical qubits (which may depend on
n
).
Suppose that there is an oracle
\mathcal{O}
which solves the classical representation of Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
Then there is a classical algorithm running in
\poly(n)
time which solves Decision
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(\lfloor\frac{np}{6}\rfloor,\;2n,\;\frac{p}{6}\right)
with advantage
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, calling
\mathcal{O}
as a subroutine.
By the equivalence of
\mathsf{LSN}
and its classical representation, if
\mathcal{O}
instead solves the conventional formulation of Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,n,p\right)
there is also a quantum algorithm running in
\poly(n)
time which solves Decision
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(\lfloor\frac{np}{6}\rfloor,\;2n,\;\frac{p}{6}\right)
with advantage
\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, calling
\mathcal{O}
as a subroutine.

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{6}
and
k^{\prime}=\lfloor np^{\prime}\rfloor
; let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be a constant.
We have an immediate reduction from
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(k^{\prime},\;2n,\;p^{\prime}\right)
to
\mathsf{LPN}\!\left(k^{\prime},\;\lfloor 2n-(1+\epsilon)np^{\prime}\rfloor,\;p^{\prime}\right)
, since we are always free to discard rows.
By Theorem
6.4
, this latter problem reduces to Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,\;3q\right)
, with
q=p^{\prime}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{2n}(1+3\epsilon)\leq\left(1+\frac{1+3\epsilon}{2}\right)p^{\prime}
.
Using Lemma
2.17
, we can assume that
q
is exactly this upper bound by adding extra noise.
Then, by Lemma
6.5
, Decision
\mathsf{sympLPN}\!\left(n,\;3q\right)
reduces to Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,n,3q\right)
.
Finally, Decision
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,\;n,\;3q\right)
reduces to Search
\mathsf{LSN}^{\poly}\!\left(k,\;n,\;3q\right)
by Theorem
5.3
, which reduces easily to Search
\mathsf{LSN}\!\left(k,\;n,\;3q\right)
by ignoring all but the first sample.
Choosing
\epsilon=\frac{1}{3}
, we have
q\leq 2p^{\prime}
, so
3q\leq 6p^{\prime}=p
.
The composition of these reductions proves the theorem.
∎

7
Summary and Outlook

This work explored the hardness of decoding quantum stabilizer codes from several perspectives.
We showed that quantum degeneracy and the inability to directly verify solutions caused the very definition of quantum decoding to lose its robustness to slight perturbations, e.g. finding the error is likely not equivalent (in an instance-by-instance manner) to recovering the logical state itself.
Nonetheless, all definitions are
NP
-complete.
We next studied the relation between
\mathsf{LPN}
and
\mathsf{LSN}
; that is, between decoding random classical and random quantum codes.
The gap between these two problems is significant even conceptually.
We bridge this gap from both directions.
Quantumly, we introduced a completely classical representation of quantum decoding, which shares the same qualitative structure as
\mathsf{LPN}
while also being equivalent to
\mathsf{LSN}
.
Classically, we introduced a symplectic variant of
\mathsf{LPN}
which guarantees that the generator matrix satisfies a symplectic orthogonality condition naturally baked into quantum codes.
We then reduced
\mathsf{LPN}
to its symplectic variant, and reduced that to
\mathsf{LSN}
.

At the same time, we examined the hardness of
\mathsf{LSN}
from the perspective of self-reducibility.
We give search-to-decision reductions (and, in the average case, decision-to-search), but prove at the same time that several reasonable notions of a random self-reduction from the worst-case decoding problem to the average case
\mathsf{LSN}
do not exist.
One interpretation of this distinction is that search-to-decision reductions can be accomplished with only local operators, whereas a random self-reduction necessitates a great deal of entanglement.
The entangling operations required, however, act in a highly uniform way (namely, they mix all Paulis at approximately the same rate), which ultimately bars a reduction.

A natural question stemming from this work is whether the converse reduction from
\mathsf{LSN}
to
\mathsf{LPN}
holds; namely, is there a reduction from
\mathsf{LSN}(k,n,p)
to
\mathsf{LPN}(\Theta(np),\;\Theta(n),\;\Theta(p))
?
The existence of such a reduction would help shed light on the the exact relationship between quantum and classical decoding more generally.

There are also several open questions centered around strengthening both the positive and negative results we give about reductions among quantum decoding.
For example, we give a reduction from Search
\mathsf{stateLSN}
to Search
\mathsf{LSN}
only when
k=O(\log(n))
or the solver for
\mathsf{LSN}
succeeds with a very large probability, namely substantially more than
\frac{1}{2}
(as compared to the usual
\frac{1}{2^{k}}+\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
).
Whether or not this reduction can be improved remains open.
Likewise, we give search-to-decision reductions in the worst case with the requirement of a
\mathsf{QNCP}(k,n,w^{\prime})
solver that works for each choice of
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
It is unclear whether a reduction exists with a weaker assumption, e.g. if one requires only a solver with weight exactly
w
.
From the random self-reduction front, an important question is whether one can prove with no assumptions at all that no restricted notion of a random self-reduction exists, or whether such a reduction does exist but requires an exotic algorithmic approach.

Finally, from the point of view of applications, there is a long history of
\mathsf{LPN}
as the basis for a great deal of cryptographic protocols
[
Pie12
]
.
A natural question concerns whether we can also base interesting cryptography on hard problems in quantum, rather than classical error correction.
If cryptographic schemes based on
\mathsf{LPN}
can be adapted to
\mathsf{sympLPN}
or
\mathsf{LSN}
, then new classical cryptography could be built from natural quantum-inspired problems.
Finally—and perhaps, more interestingly—cryptography based
on these assumptions may turn out be harder to break than any cryptography based on
\mathsf{LPN}
.

Appendix A
Nonexistence of unstructured classical random self-reductions

We here sketch a proof that there is no random self-reduction into
\mathsf{LPN}(k,n,p)
from the worst-case version, known as
\mathsf{NCP}(k,n,w)
, which holds for all classical linear codes.
In this case, the reduction distribution is given by a random vector
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, drawn from a distribution
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
, which multiplies the code on the left.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k}
be a generator matrix and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
an error with
|\mathbf{e}|\leq w
, and
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k}
be the logical bitstring.
Let
\mathbf{r}
be drawn from any distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
, transforming
(\boldsymbol{A},\mathbf{e})\longrightarrow(\mathbf{r}^{T}\boldsymbol{A},\;\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r})
.
Suppose for any
\boldsymbol{A}
, the law of
\mathbf{r}^{T}\boldsymbol{A}
is within TV distance
\epsilon
of
\Unif(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{k})
. Then the law of
\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r}
is also
\epsilon
-close in TV distance to
\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

Fix some choice of
\mathbf{e}
. Choose any
C
whose first row is
\mathbf{e}
. By monotonicity of TV distance,
\TV(\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r},\;\Unif(\mathbb{Z}_{2}))\leq\TV(\mathbf{r}^{T}\boldsymbol{A},\;\Unif(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}))\leq\epsilon
. But
\Unif(\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem A.2
.

There is no strong random self-reduction from
\mathsf{NCP}(k,n,w)
to
\mathsf{LPN}(k,n,p)
such that
p=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.

Proof.

Suppose that such a reduction distribution
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
exists, so that

\displaystyle\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{r}\sim\boldsymbol{R}_{n}}((\mathbf{r}^{T}\boldsymbol{A},\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r}),\;\Unif(\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n\times k})\otimes\mathsf{Ber}(p))\leq\epsilon=\negl(n).

(A.1)

By monotonicity, the TV between just the codes is
\leq\epsilon
. Hence, by Lemma
A.1
, the TV distance between the smoothed error and
\mathsf{Ber}(\frac{1}{2})
is
\leq\epsilon
. However,

\displaystyle\TV\left(\mathsf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\delta\right),\;\mathsf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left|p-(p-\delta)\right|+\left|(1-p)-(1-p+\delta)\right|\right)=\delta.

(A.2)

For
p=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
, the above and the triangle inequality imply that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\poly(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\TV\left(\mathsf{Ber}(p),\mathsf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{r}\sim\boldsymbol{R}_{n}}(\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r},\;\mathsf{Ber}(p))+\mathop{\TV}\limits_{\mathbf{r}\sim\boldsymbol{R}_{n}}\left(\mathbf{e}\cdot\mathbf{r},\;\mathsf{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)

(A.4)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\epsilon=2\epsilon=\negl(n),

(A.5)

a contradiction for sufficiently large
n
.
∎
